#6748. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

#6746. Is Social Media Really the Great Equalizer? Here’s What Hulu’s ‘The Circle’ Host Tinx Has to Say (Exclusive)

I sat down with Christina Najjar to talk alliances, authenticity, and the responsibility of being the “internet’s big sister.”

After a seven-season run on Netflix, The Circle has found a new home on Hulu, and things look very, very different. Rather than “normies,” the cast is now made up of familiar faces from the Walt Disney Company reality TV ecosystem and micro-influencers disguised as civilians. (Even the player with the most modest initial following, Cyrus Irani, had over 10,000 followers at the time his casting was announced. Is it a problem? Not necessarily. But it’s certainly a switch-up from season 1’s social media skeptic Shubham Goel.)

The revival series is also airing in near-real time, with new episodes dropping Tuesday through Thursday and audience voting being used to shape the process. And perhaps the biggest change: stepping into hosting duties in place of comedian and actor Michelle Buteau is SiriusXM’s Christina “Tinx” Najjar, who rose to fame on social media during the COVID-19 pandemic.

As a longtime Circle devotee, I was...curious about what these changes might mean for my favorite competition series. So when I got the chance to fly to Atlanta to visit the set (and meet Tinx) myself, I took it. There I chatted with her about the power of social media, the potential problem of having celebs in the Circle, and the player she thinks might win it all.

I truly couldn’t believe it. I know how many loyal fans The Circle has, so when I got the call that I got it—I mean, I’m still pinching myself every day. It’s a really big moment for me, and I know I have big shoes to fill. And I really just want to make everybody proud.

I remember watching the very first episode and thinking, “Wow, this is such a fresh concept, and it’s truly a social experiment for the modern age.” So the fact that I’m here all these years later is truly a pinch-me moment.

I relate to it so much, because in COVID, I was living in a studio apartment in L.A. I had just moved. I didn’t have a lot of friends, and all I had was my phone. That’s why I initially picked up TikTok and thought, “Who knows what’s going to happen in the world? I might as well make a few videos and try to make friends online.” And as soon as I started creating content, I just realized, “Wow, I love connecting with people. I love entertaining people.” It’s been a wild six years ever since.

I grew up doing theater in high school, but honestly, no. I truthfully had a lot of limiting beliefs about myself. I just thought there was no way I could ever be an influencer. I didn’t feel pretty enough. I didn’t think I was put together enough. And I think one thing COVID taught us is that anybody who has a dream can go for it. Social media is really the great equalizer in that sense, because if you are funny, if you have something to say, you can get online and you can connect with people.

It’s such an honor, and I love that people look up to me for advice. It’s the honor of my life that people trust me enough to call me on my radio show for advice or write to me, and I just try to be a voice of reason and remind women to know their worth. I feel like this is a journey I was meant to be on, and all of the opportunities that being online has afforded me, because it really has given me so much. The fact that I’m sitting here as the host of The Circle is—again, I can’t believe it.

It’s so different because I’m used to just holding up my phone, recording, cutting quickly, and pressing post. This is a totally different operation, but the crew is so cool, production is so cool, and the writers that I work with are just so smart and so funny. I was actually shocked when they explained to me the process of how close to the wire we are. It is as close to real time as you could ever think.

Because I have lived online for six years, I post in real time myself. I’m someone who wakes up and I’m like, “Hey guys, I’m going to the gym. Here’s what I’m eating for breakfast.” I don’t know if I would know how to do it any other way, because I’m just, like, an oversharer, so I think I would just be myself and see what happened. But I can be strategic. I feel like I would make a lot of alliances. I’m good at that.

I honestly think maybe the celebrities are at a slight disadvantage because, as you say, people have perceptions about them. I feel like they also have more of a target on their backs.

He was shocked. He had such a good strategy. I was honestly shocked he got out, too. He’s so likable, and everyone knew it was really him. He got got. He got got by Justin [Hinsley], if I’m being honest, who has very strong strategy. I think Justin is incredibly strategic and knows how to play the game.

I mean, Hannah [Brown], obviously. I don’t think she knows how to play any other way. She’s so genuine, and she’s definitely playing with her heart. So we’ll see how that fares and whether it’s strategy or heart that wins.

I think it’s totally fine, because there’s a lot on the line here. This is a popularity contest, but there’s a big prize at the end. So I think it’s fun when people are ruthless. Why not? It’s a game, and games are meant to be won.

#6745. Front Office: Radhi Devlukia

The host of the A Really Good Cry podcast turned to designer Jess Cooney to create a home office-meets-studio made for deep conversations.

Radhi Devlukia isn’t afraid of tears. On her podcast, A Really Good Cry, the whole point is to get personal, and to do so with guests as varied as supermodel Miranda Kerr, dermatologists, hospice nurses, personal finance advisors—and Devlukia’s husband, fellow podcaster Jay Shetty.

Now, two years into the show, Devlukia, a best-selling author, cook, and founder of beverage company Juni, is unveiling a new studio and office space located within her and Shetty’s Los Angeles house. Devlukia turned to designer Jess Cooney, who has a namesake Berkshires-based interiors firm, to craft a space designed to support these really good cries. Cooney had never tackled a podcast studio before but was ready for the challenge, which included building a room that could both serve as a set—technical components, artificial light, and all—as well as a welcoming home office.

From the start, Cooney knew what the space would not include. “We wanted to move away from the tropes usually seen in podcast studios, like styled bookshelves or cluttered backgrounds that detract from the people being interviewed, and also anything logo or merch heavy,” she says. “And toward something that felt warmer and more inviting.”

Instead, she took cues from spaces like fashion designer Bella Freud’s London apartment, Jean Cocteau’s Villa Santo Sospir in the Côte d’Azur, and Paloma Elsesser’s home by Gregory Rockwell. The resulting office, says Cooney, “has this incredible womb-like, feminine atmosphere that cocoons and holds you.”

Below, Devlukia takes our Front Office survey, giving a behind-the-scenes look at her new home base.

Radhi Devlukia: The hanging art piece in the middle of the window, Kissed by Katie Burdon.

When you need to lock in and power through work, what’s your setup?

Doors open, facing the window, instrumental music playing. I also move areas a lot during work hours to stay focused, so sometimes I’m at the desk, sometimes on the swivel chairs, sometimes on the floor by the window!

I have articles printed about my grandma and grandad on my canvas memory wall. They taught at schools in Africa in the 1970s and there were articles written about how much they were doing for the community. I also have vintage ceramics—I love the mix of old and new.

It really depends. If there are two of us, we’re on the swivel chairs. If there are a few of us, we’re around the marble table. We love keeping things casual at the office so sometimes people sit on the cozy rug or wherever feels comfortable.

What was the best meeting you’ve had there?

We have had so many creative meetings in this office. It’s such a calming and flowing environment. The whole rebrand of the podcast was ideated in this new office, which I’m obsessed with now.

What would you change about it if you could?

Maybe just having the ability to shoot with the blinds open.

What’s the funniest thing that happened there?

My team loves to prank me, so there’s been a lot of laughter in our office!

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

#6743. Thom Browne and Mr Porter Just Released an Exclusive New Capsule Collection

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The 17-piece lineup makes its debut on young restaurateur George McNally.

Launching this week on Mr Porter is a 17-piece capsule collection created with New York’s own Thom Browne. Browne is famed in men’s fashion for his conceptual runway shows—staged in Florence, Milan, Paris and, of course New York—but beneath all the elevated showmanship lies a broad and distinctly wearable aesthetic.

Consisting of casual and tailored pieces and extending to luggage, home goods, and even the recently launched Thom Browne x Bose headphones, the collection puts a youthful twist on Browne’s signature style that blends sportswear and suits with a rigorous attention to detail. Browne has acted as Chairman of the Council of Fashion Designers of America since 1923 and won its prestigious Menswear Designer award four times in his 25-year career.

To showcase the collaboration, the capsule was photographed by Ian Kenneth Bird on young New York restaurateur George McNally. If the name McNally rings a bell, that’s no accident; his surname carries a lot of weight in the New York food scene. George is the son of Keith (Balthazar, Minetta Tavern) and nephew of Brian (Indochine), both past masters of creating downtown Manhattan restaurants with an enduring buzz in a notoriously fickle foodie scene.

George—who, at just 23, has worked pretty much every restaurant position going in New York and London since he was a teenager—is tracing his own culinary path with Faux, his hot new space in Tribeca, serving reasonably priced, loosely French-Mediterranean food, and a space that stays open late for a city that, proverbially, never sleeps.

Go to Mr Porter to see and shop the collection and read George’s accompanying interview.

#6739. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

As threat actors adopt more targeted and convincing tactics, education leaders need practical ways to reduce risk without undermining the open, connected environments their institutions depend on.

#6738. Hailey Bieber’s 30 Best Looks in Honor of Her 30th Birthday

From polka dots and fur to red-carpet statements

Happy birthday, Hailey Bieber! The star has officially turned 30, and to mark another turn around the sun, she kicked off a week of celebrations with a new navy-blue Rhode drop, a festive campaign, and an intimate video series that followed a week in her life. Throughout the vlogs, we saw the founder in fittings and makeup chairs, spending time with son Jack Blues, and reflecting on the past decade. “Becoming a mom definitely changed me the most in my twenties,” she told the camera. “I always knew I wanted to be a mom, I always knew I wanted kids, and it just totally transforms you as a person for the better.”

Of course, the birth-week celebrations have also come with a wardrobe to match. From shearling coats to archival statements, she ushered in the new season with everything from an ivory satin Oriori coat trimmed in gray-white fur to a black silk Armani Privé Fall 2005 set to a Yohji Yamamoto Spring 1998 jacket previously seen on Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy. And we did not expect anything less from the star.

Throughout the years, Bieber has become as known for her style as for her Rhode lipgloss phone cases, playing with understated “quiet luxury,” classic Los Angeles athleisure, and tons of Saint Laurent leather. As she grew from a teenage model into a business owner and, later, young mother, her taste has evolved over time—but through it all, she has given us some major fashion inspiration.

So, in honor of her 30th birthday, we rounded up our 30 favorite looks from the star. And between her plethora of Met Gala ensembles, polka-dot prints, and mismatched moments with husband Justin, there was a lot to pick from.

For the New York Times Dealbook Summit, Bieber takes a page from Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy and wears a Yohji Yamamoto Spring 1998 jacket previously worn by the late It girl.

Stepping out in New York City, Bieber pairs her Phoebe Philo cropped fur coat with blue jeans and vintage Manolos.

The star wears the coat of the season: Conner Ives’s reconstituted fur robe in white silk mikado.

#6736. 12 Watches That Make a Great Gift for the Man in Your Life

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re on a Panerai budget or something closer to Casio.

A watch is a sentimental gift. It’s not something casually given—not a tie or a fancy notebook or even a good pen or a nice bottle of booze. Don’t get me wrong, those are delightful things to receive and all appropriate for a run-of-the-mill holiday or birthday celebration. But some years aren’t run-of-the-mill. Some years require something extra. Some years are watch years—and trust me, you’ll know it when you get there.

But what watch? It’s a personal choice, of course, but a personal choice that you’re making for someone else. Luckily, you likely know them pretty well. Around these parts, Esquire knows watches pretty well. Here are 12 suggestions, in price ranges from midwestern frugal to multigeneration inheritance. No matter how much you’re comfortable spending, you can’t go wrong with any of these watches.

A classic watch for a classic guy. The Khaki Field Expedition is the quintessential field watch. Rich history of American craftsmanship, purpose-driven aesthetics, functional compass bezel (even if I can’t figure out how to use it for the life of me), and in the past few years it’s been available with the always practical NATO strap. It’s an ideal watch for the horologist, who’ll appreciate the history, the movement, and the exhibition case back. Win-win, guaranteed all-star gift.

I’m an unabashed Citizen fan. The Promaster “Fugu” is my favorite watch in the $500 range, and the Japanese brand’s Eco-Drive offerings are even more affordable. The Promaster Sea Eco-Drive is almost criminally underpriced for the watch that you get. We’re talking an emerald-green dial with a matching unidirectional 60-minute bezel, 200 meter depth rating, and a stainless-steel bracelet for well under $300. This is a great gift for the practically driven, classic dive-watch fan. And if the green dial is too swerve-y, the black and the blue are both excellent choices.

Good doesn’t always mean expensive. A big ol’ dive watch isn’t appropriate for every occasion or every guy. Simpler can be better. Like this under-$100 life-proof G-Shock. It treads the line between classic G-Shock aesthetics and little Casio reference. A watch equally appropriate for a man who never thinks about watches or one who is always thinking about watches—Schrödinger’s timepiece of no taste or good taste. And, hot take, it pairs better with a suit than sneakers do. Keep your shoes formal and let your watch carry the high-low combo.

If he’s not a G-Shock man, he may be an Ironman. A Timex Ironman, that is, even if he’s not logging an insane amount of weekend mileage. As the world grows wary of increased technocracy-driven smart designs, there’s a simple nostalgia to the ’90s-tech runner’s watch. Turns out they were right all along. You don’t need a phone on your wrist, just a stopwatch and an alarm clock and a 100-lap memory to boot. I also like the orange accents here. If he is midwestern frugal and would object to a gift over $100, this scaled-back version also plays.

Shinola’s Duck Watch references ’60s and ’70s skin divers, which sounds dirty but really is just a term for smaller, less depth-rated dive watches and the culture of free diving and spearfishing around them. That’s a reductive explanation that some watch nerds will quibble with, but it’ll serve us for now. The two important things to take away are 1) there was a magazine of the same name that you can find on eBay and 2) Shinola’s modern version is a killer watch. I have this one in Canary Yellow, but I recently borrowed the new Mallard Brown colorway for a photo shoot, and I love it. Hated to send it back. In the sun, the dial turns almost iridescent, like the titular duck’s feathers. It’s battery powered, water-resistant, and not fussy at all.

[Editor’s note: The Mallard Brown colorway is currently out of stock, so for the time being we will direct you to the Canary.]

Let’s get fancy. But let’s get fancy with purpose. More on Rolexes soon, but I think Panerai makes the most exciting watches in the $5,000 to $15,000 range. I realize that’s a large range, one encompassing the price of a decent motorcycle or a used Camry, but I’m talking luxury Swiss-made watches here. This is the tip of the iceberg. Panerai has design panache and exquisite movements, a design that’s not too “look at me” but still far enough outside the classic mold that it demands a second look. It’s not a watch for everyone, but man, those who will love it will love it immensely.

Recently I hosted a friend and fellow style writer for a will-not-be-named rival publication at the Detroit headquarters of T-shirt maker Soft Goods MFG. He had a couple enviable pieces (understated Nitty Gritty corduroy jacket, Paraboot derbies, perfectly faded vintage Levi’s), but I kept looking at his watch, a Unimatic Quattro. In the interest of individuality and my own love of a good suede, I’m suggesting this Todd Snyder collab as the next gift for that extra-special man in your life. It’s Unimatic with a twist, Italian design with an Americana POV, like spaghetti western for your wrist. Clint Eastwood fans rejoice.

Timex and J. Crew’s recent trout-forward collab watch sold out in an instant—unfortunately before I could get my hands on one. But there are other options for the outdoorsy dude. Especially one who cares about stopping forest fires. This Vero Smokey Bear option toes the line between campy and classy with aplomb. It’ll look good while you’re chopping wood or a little Wes Anderson–y at the lodge with a glass of bourbon in hand, telling stories about the trout that got away.

TAG can get a bad rap in the watch-collecting community, but ignore the critics, especially if your intended recipient is a racing enthusiast. TAG Heuer has a long history in F1 and Grand Prix, and it looks as much at home in the cockpit of a fancy Brad Pitt–piloted car as it does in the fan stands or the sports bar. If you want to step it up a level, the Monaco is a luxury take on a race-day watch—all European angles and rich leather.

This is the only pilot’s watch on the list—and for good reason. It’s the most handsome pilot’s watch on the market. Marathon developed these in partnership with the Kelly Air Force Base in 1986 and specced it out to withstand high-altitude flight, or even an ejection. If you’re in the market for a classic pilot’s chronograph, Bremont makes the best one. But if you are in the market for a no-frills, all-style, practically designed pilot’s watch, the Marathon Arctic Navigator is your ticket.

Maybe he’s a Rolex Pepsi guy, but you’re on a non-Swiss-made budget. I’ll let you in on a secret: Seiko makes a pretty damn good approximation. I particularly like that this one is PADI co-branded and automatic, with a 200 meter depth rating. Even better, it’s under $500.

For my money, Doxa makes some of the most interesting dive watches out there. Is this on the list because I’d really love if someone took the hint and bought one for me? Hard to say. What’s easy to say is that the orange-dial Doxa Sub 200T is a showstopper. It’s got a slightly smaller profile and a more interesting silhouette than the original 200 but still retains the necessary details, like its unidirectional rotating bezel with its integrated U.S. Navy no-decompression limit table. No rules on whether that’s used underwater or just at the dinner table, either. It’s good to know how long to cook a steak for.

#6735. Jackie Kennedy’s Private Letters to Truman Capote Reveal a Fuller Picture of Their Friendship

Heading to auction on November 21, the six notes are estimated to fetch as much as $80,000.

First Lady Jacqueline Kennedy’s sister, Lee Radziwill, was one of celebrated author Truman Capote’s “Swans,” developing a close, if complicated, friendship with the novelist. But Jackie had her own relationship with Capote, one that is less widely known. The pair corresponded with each other in the 1960s and Kennedy may have even gotten an early look at Capote’s 1966 novel, In Cold Blood.

“I have your precious book and am so overjoyed,” she wrote to Capote in an undated card, presumed to be from June 1963. “The minute Jack takes off for Europe I will retreat to the country + indulge myself by reading every page—not just the new ones.”

That letter, along with five others between Kennedy and Capote, will soon hit the auction block, ELLE Decor can reveal. The notes, three of which are written on White House letterhead, will be sold together in a single lot through Cornell Auctions, estimated to fetch between $40,000 and $80,000. Together, they reveal new details about the friendship between the former first lady and one of America’s great authors.

Available for the first time ever, these private letters are part of a larger auction of Capote-linked items accumulated over decades by his biographer, Gerald Clarke, author of 1988’s Capote. The sale, “The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote,” will take place on November 21.

Kennedy’s notes are all believed to have been written in 1963 and 1964, with four bearing postmark dates. During that period, Kennedy suffered personal tragedies, losing her infant son Patrick days after he was born as well as her husband, President John F. Kennedy, who was assassinated in November 1963.

The letters indicate a warm relationship between Kennedy and Capote. In several of the messages, the first lady makes reference to various presents and mementos she received from the writer.

One particularly meaningful gift came in the wake of President John F. Kennedy’s death, after Jackie and Capote saw each other at a gathering hosted by decorator Billy Baldwin. “The most beautiful porcelain rose that I ever saw or dreamed of—came to me the other day from you,” the former first lady wrote in a three-page December 1964 missive, handwritten on mourning stationery bearing a crest.

In another letter, Kennedy makes reference to a gift she sent to Capote, inspired by a visit to the novelist’s home. “In your Japanese-Victorian room I spotted two large apothecary jars of the same kind—I found this one in 1955 in Paris—so it has been with me for many years in many places,” she wrote in an undated letter, believed to be from December 1964.

Handwritten on black-bordered stationery, that note was addressed to Capote’s house at 70 Willow Street in Brooklyn Heights, where he wrote Breakfast at Tiffany’s and In Cold Blood.

Read Jackie Kennedy’s Letters to Truman Capote

Beyond the Kennedy notes, also up for auction is Capote’s four-piece formal dinner invitation from President Lyndon B. Johnson in 1966, which has an estimate of $600 to $2,000. Other highlights include a German pencil sharpener in a leather pouch; a chalkware bulldog; a monogrammed duffel bag; and jewel-like paperweights made by the author himself.

Taken together, these pieces paint a fuller picture of Capote’s life and work as a writer, as well as Kennedy’s enthusiastic support of his talents. “I keep thinking what power a great writer has. All the things you write move people,” she wrote. “I am glad you became a writer.”

“The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote” will take place November 21 starting at 11 a.m.; bidding can be done online via LiveAuctioneers or in person at Cornell Auctions in Bellport, New York.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6727. Black Friday Came Early! Here Are the Beauty Deals Actually Worth Shopping Right Now

I tracked all the best sales on LED masks, hair dryers, viral skincare, makeup, and more.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Unlike the days of yore, Black Friday isn’t really just on the Friday after Thanksgiving anymore. Honestly, it’s more than Cyber Week too. We basically have a whole month of deals to look forward to once November rolls around. And if you were looking to stock up on your favorite skincare products, try out a new beauty device, grab a gift set for your bestie or mom, or just avoid paying full price for your shampoo and conditioner (me), this month has some fantastic deals for you.

I’ve covered Black Friday as Cosmopolitan’s beauty editor for the past five years (!), so I know what beauty deals are actually worth shopping during this huge sales event versus better deals you can find throughout the year. As a shopping expert and beauty editor at Cosmopolitan, I’ve been tracking all the Black Friday beauty deals for a few weeks already, and there are quite a few things you can already get on a good discount right now.

#6726. Bulgari Brings High Jewelry to a Palm Beach Stronghold

To celebrate its new boutique, the Italian house unveiled its Eclettica collection at a quintessential South Florida estate.

Above: To celebrate Bulgari’s new collection and boutique, guests roamed the grounds of a waterfront Palm Beach estate, designed by architect Addison Mizner.

Few people shaped the look of South Florida as much as architect Addison Mizner. A pioneer of Spanish Colonial Revival architecture, his Mediterranean style quickly took Palm Beach by storm in the 1920s, as he designed and built more than 50 Florida mansions and villas. And his impact still lingers, as, on Wednesday night, stars like Venus Williams and Chanel Iman spent their evening seated under the tennis pavilion at one of Mizner’s famed waterfront Palm Beach estates.

The crowd was there to celebrate a far newer building in the neighborhood: Bulgari’s Palm Beach boutique, which opened earlier this week. In honor of the store’s debut, the brand brought its newest high jewelry collection, Eclettica, to the United States for the first time.

“Palm Beach embodies a distinctive spirit of effortless sophistication,” said Bulgari CEO Laura Burdese.

Located in a heritage building in Palm Beach’s shopping district, the boutique is modeled after a traditional Italian piazza. Anchored by four travertine columns, the open space encourages clients to move through the maison’s most famous collections. Venini’s classic Serpenti chandelier, made of Murano glass and seen in Bvlgari boutiques worldwide, serves as a focal point in a shape reminiscent of the brand’s many symbolic snakes.

Elsewhere in the store, decorative panels by Milan-based Pictalab depict Floridian ocean and beach views, grounding the Italian maison in its surrounding landscape. Among the art is the Eclettica collection, which borrows its name from eclecticism and stays true to its moniker: mixing inspirations from painting, sculpture, and architecture, translating them into bold, colorful pieces.

It’s a fitting style for Palm Beach, where Mizner’s vibrant Mediterranean and Spanish-influenced designs borrow inspiration from entire eras and regions, giving the area its everlasting mix-master flair.

“We are delighted to bring Eclettica to this extraordinary destination,” Burdese said, “where its bold artistic vision and exceptional craftsmanship find a natural resonance with the city’s vibrant character.”

#6724. Winter Essentials Worth Giving—and Keeping

Bring warmth and classic style to city days and outdoor adventures with gear from The North Face.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It’s an oft-expressed sentiment, but it’s true: The holidays turn people’s attention toward friends, family, and community. There’s a beauty in the selfless, caring gifts people give to others. And while all that giving feels great, the holidays are also a good time to treat yourself. Plus, it’s getting colder.

Whether you equate The North Face with everyday city wear or outdoor adventures, it’s a solid, time-tested spot to get a gift for a loved one (or yourself). The brand has jackets and accessories with familiar winter looks, as well as products with less expected silhouettes that still offer protection from the elements in an understated style.

Here’s a look at some of the best cold-weather gear that will have you wrapping up the gift of warmth and durability… even if that gift is for yourself.

This is an easy gifting win because the warm, hooded, insulated jacket is a recognizable The North Face staple, evoking an unpretentious spin on the brand’s iconic Nuptse Jacket.

It’s familiar, but it’s also practical—the kind of gift you feel good about because it’s going to get a ton of use. (And it can withstand the repeated wears.) The attached hood offers protection from biting winds, and the straightforward color options make it easy to pair with a variety of outfits, whether it’s something fun or part of a heavy bundling during the coldest days of winter. This is the perfect type of winter coat: warm but not too puffy.

The Zaneck Jacket is a great alternative to the Aconcagua, stepping away from the outdoorsy look the brand is best known for. It’s still waterproof and insulated, but it has a longer, more modern cut.

The Zaneck comes with a removable faux fur lining for the hood, which, in addition to being warm, means there are a couple of different looks that can be teased out of it. The straight cuffs add to its stylish feel in a way that doesn’t scream “I’m dressed for warmth and not fashion,” even though it’s doing both.

The Lone Peak Triclimate 2 has a lot in common with the Zaneck. From a distance, they cut a similar silhouette. However, the Lone Peak adds a little versatility if you’re looking for a jacket that can handle a variety of climates.

A removable, insulated inner jacket is attached to a removable, insulated top layer, which is fit for rainy conditions or as a spring or fall jacket. The insulated layer converts the whole thing into a winter-ready jacket. It also sports underarm vents capable of releasing heat in the middle of a long hike, ideal for outdoorsy types. The hood is removable and it’s got adjustable cuffs and an adjustable hem, making this a do-it-all jacket.

It might be easy to ignore the Pertex Jacket. It’s a bit unassuming. It doesn’t cut as stylish of a figure as the Zaneck, but it’s endlessly useful. The lightweight Pertex fabric will keep you dry in a downpour, but it also offers warmth without marshmallow-like bulk.

The lightweight aspect makes this the kind of coat you toss in the backseat on the way to a hike, just in case. It’s also great for layering, whether it’s tossed on over a sweater or layered under an overcoat to make a favorite jacket more winter-ready.

This edition of the classic Base Camp Duffel echoes the design details we love in The North Face’s jackets. While it’s got enough style and a heap of color options for daily use, it’s built for adventure.

Made with water- and tear-resistant fabric, it boasts 71 liters of storage space, a roomier option to the similar 31-liter version. Like all of the brand’s products, it’s built as if the designers have been through the rigors of outdoor adventures (and they probably have). It’s got a sweeping D-zip opening, backpack straps, mesh pockets, and compression straps in case you’re curious if you can pack 73 liters worth of gear into the 71-liter space. (You know you’ve tried.)

The North Face has a lot of hats, none more iconic than this. The Salty Lined Beanie has a classic feel, fitting snuggly over the head with rib-knit texture, a cuffed shape, soft fabric, and ample color options to ensure it will match any jacket or accessory. The Salty Lined Beanie encapsulates the entire The North Face vibe in a small way: It’s stylish enough for a subway ride to work and practical enough for an outdoor adventure. Best of all, it’ll keep your ears warm in either instance.

Often, the best glove is the one that disappears into the outfit. It looks seamless, keeping hands warm without drawing too much attention. The Etip Recycled Glove is about functionality first and foremost, but the simple design and understated colors makes it look good with a variety of winter outfits.

While we all like to pretend we aren’t attached to our phones, the Etip makes a great modern gift because its stretchy fleece delivers a snug fit and the palms have touchscreen compatibility. That means you won’t have to take off your gloves to respond to that important text while the snow is falling.

#6717. A Menorca Farmhouse Finds Its Easy Elegance

Isabel López-Quesada honored the historical essence of a 19th-century Menorcan retreat while introducing vibrant colors and an elevated ease.

Above: In the living room, López-Quesada replicated the original ceiling beams and finished the walls in traditional Menorcan lime plaster. A sofa of her design is grouped with a 1960s Tito Agnoli rattan daybed, a Mexican palm leaf stool, and a custom cocktail table upholstered with a Moroccan rug.

For Spanish designer Isabel López-Quesada, summers tend to revolve around Biarritz, in southwestern France, where she has a home. Still, she always carves out time for Menorca, regarded as the most laid-back of Spain’s Balearic Islands. That vibe suits the ELLE Decor A-list designer, whose signature style is luxurious but unfussy, harmonizing old and new with breezy refinement.

Typically, López-Quesada goes there to relax and spend time with friends, but around five years ago she received a call from a Bilbao family who asked her to refresh their vacation home outside Mahón, Menorca’s capital. An old farm property, or finca, the five-bedroom residence required extensive updates. López-Quesada was steadfast in preserving such historical elements as a well and donkey stables, which she transformed into an indoor-outdoor bathroom with a patio and marble tub. “It was important to respect the style of Menorca,” she says. “We were very careful with the materials, so it all goes together and conveys the sentiment I want the house to have.”

López-Quesada founded her namesake studio as a precocious 20-year-old Madrileña 40 years ago. Initially inspired by her mother’s yen for decorating and the work of celebrated Spanish designers such as Paco Muñoz and Fernando Benjumea, López-Quesada garnered wide attention in the early 2000s, when she converted an old Madrid wax factory into an atelier and a luxurious home for her family, filled with antiques and contemporary pieces. Soon she had interest from clients all over the world. Today López-Quesada’s 12-person team includes one of her three adult children, daughter Isabel Llanza. Five years ago the pair co-founded the lifestyle brand Isita Home, selling products they make themselves or discover on their travels.

The Menorca project began by gutting the house’s interior and rebuilding it “in an old style,” López-Quesada says. Central to it all was adding custom windows and doors to each room so the island’s golden light could flood the spaces. Vaulted and beamed ceilings were left intact or were recreated. Reconfiguring the floor plan, López-Quesada eschewed a proper dining room in favor of an eat-in open kitchen. “Most of the time you are eating outside,” she says.

With Menorca’s unrelenting summer sun, bougainvillea-draped pergolas were a must for leisurely alfresco lunches and dinners. Swaths of white lantana and lily of the Nile border the terraces, while “the pool reflects the sky like a mirror and provides a refreshing feel,” López-Quesada says.

Her collaborator on the gardens was Spanish landscape architect Luis González-Camino. “My vision was to design a water-efficient Mediterranean garden, divided into distinct yet connected ‘garden rooms’ for different seasons and times of day,” he says. González-Camino preserved the property as a natural Mediterranean woodland, with wild olive trees providing cover.

Indoors, López-Quesada installed French terra-cotta floor tiles chosen for their aged look. The three fireplaces were all handcrafted in traditional Menorcan style—a little imperfect, painted white—and decorated with vintage tiles the designer keeps in her Madrid warehouse.

She offset the white ceilings and lime plaster walls with vibrant textiles and tilework in blues and greens inspired by Menorca’s native palette. An upstairs bedroom is anchored by an iron four-poster bed with a blue-and-white-striped canopy; its bath has a shower lined with lapis-colored zellige tiles. Doors, which across Menorca are customarily painted in bold shades, here are finished in verdant hues.

Custom cabinets are designed with whimsically patterned cutouts for airflow, since humidity is a major factor. “On the islands, if you close the cupboards completely, mushrooms will grow inside,” López-Quesada says.

Step Inside This Renovated Menorca Retreat

She arrayed the spaces with a lively mix of vintage and new furnishings, emphasizing organic materials: rattan chairs, bamboo shades, Moroccan rugs, lots of linen and cotton fabrics in spirited florals or stripes. “If I use a heavy piece, then the rest is lighter,” she says. The tableware maintains the blue and green theme, including pieces from Isita Home and hand-blown glassware from neighboring Mallorca.

In the end, the owners asked López-Quesada to redo their home in Spain’s Basque region as well. It’s one of several projects she currently has across the country, including reimagining a hotel in Barcelona, and she’s also designing homes in Paris, Shanghai, and the Dominican Republic. Many of her projects are with repeat clients. “That’s how it works,” the designer says. “It’s like a chain.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

Sarah Cristobal is a writer, editor, and creative consultant. She is currently editor-at-large for 10 Magazine USA, among other freelance projects.

#6711. What’s a Vintage Car Rally Really Like? Inside the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run.

How I got mixed up in a race with a bunch of priceless historic automobiles and views of Italy’s spectacular Alpine scenery—and won.

In vintage rally driving, there are generally two types of competitor. Both share a passion for keeping priceless and nearly century-old vehicles in use on the open road rather than displaying them under glass. But some see the drive itself as challenge enough, while stalwarts adhere to what’s called the road book. This is a hundred or so pages of complex navigational directions that the copilot uses to navigate instead of a map, maintaining a constant flow of instructions to the driver. It is this book and how closely you follow it that decides the eventual winner.

Gianandrea Redaelli, a car nut whose family has long roots in the Italian printing business, is firmly in the “road book” camp. In early September, I climbed into the copilot seat of his 1939 Aston Martin C-Type Speed Model Roadster Bertelli—just one of two ever built, with an experimental aerodynamic body that earned it the nickname “the Flying Pea”—and stayed there as he drove six hundred kilometers of “left at the traffic lights, right at the cow” for the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run. The rally started in Venice and ended, after a scenic but challenging detour high into the Dolomite mountains, at Monza, the home of Italian Formula 1, just in time for practice day of the Grand Prix.

Venice might not seem the most logical starting line for a car rally given the proverbial wetness of the streets. But the city was chosen this year because it was also the end point of the last LV rally of its kind: the La Serenissima Run from Monte Carlo to Venice in 2012. Gianandrea won that one as copilot, with his father, Giuseppe, at the wheel. The pair also won the one before it—the Boheme across Hungary, the Czech Republic, and Austria—in 2006. This time, however, the pressure was on me too.

I have been a copilot before, and I can tell you it’s no picnic. Rally racing in vintage cars is less about speed and more about timing. It’s an exercise that requires pinpoint accuracy at the time-stamp points in the overall course and, most critically, in the regularities. These are fiendishly short time-over-distance sections in which crossing the line just a few hundredths of a second late—or early—earns strict penalties. Sensibly, the day before the race began, Gianandrea took me out in the Aston in the Veneto countryside to practice, especially those critical regularities.

I could have opted for an easy life. The rally allowed competing cars, dating from 1931 to 1969, to use the road book or follow the course using a map in a dedicated phone app. But where’s the fun in that?

“If you join a competition, you follow the regulations and strive to do your best,” Gianandrea says. “As an Italian, if you paint numbers on the door of my car, I will want to win.”

Every car in a rally like this comes with its own idiosyncrasies. On flat ground, someone driving a souped-up 2026 sports car would find these tests difficult to nail. In the reality of race conditions, the regularities often involve steep climbs or descents and unexpected corners, alling for an expert hand at the wheel—and foot on the gas pedal. The tendency of some car doors to swing open at hard bends (ours has the “suicide” doors with hinges on the back instead of the front, beloved by early carmakers) only adds to the excitement. The purpose of driver and copilot is to battle and overcome all the mechanical and computational challenges—and get our ride to the finish line bang on time.

Louis Vuitton has been involved in these glamorous and exacting vintage rallies since 1993. The Dolomites Run represents an enthusiastic return to the genre after a fourteen-year gap and, perhaps tellingly, the company’s global sponsorship (since 2025) of Formula 1. Unconfirmed rumors circulated in the field that the choice of ending the rally at a Grand Prix could well be repeated—and soon—in other countries in the future.

It’s easy, after all, to see why a fusion of style and cars with six- and seven-figure price tags might appeal to Louis Vuitton and competitors alike. Not surprisingly, at every coffee and lunch stop, the LV effect sprinkled the race with an added sense of luxury. In between, our snaking caravan carried the glamour of a century of motoring along with it. At every hairpin you got a glimpse of a gull-wing Mercedes, a 1931 Bugatti two-seater (my personal fave), and a shimmering behemoth of a Bentley in quick succession. These cars would turn heads anywhere. But in Italy, arguably the home of the sports car, they brought smiles, applause, and gasps in even the tiniest village that passed by in our slipstream. I swear I even saw a nun with a glint in her eye at one set of traffic lights.

Navigating, I was quickly learning, is not so much about providing a constant babble of information as it is knowing how to give clear and simple instructions—and only when strictly needed. As the finish neared in Monza at the end of two long days on the road, I was able to give Gianandrea the most helpful instruction of all: “Nothing for 15 kilometers.”

“That,” he very kindly replied, “is the mark of a pro.”

Even after the engines cooled, we were still clueless about how well we’d done overall. That changed at the final event, a dinner inside the Sforzesco Castle in Milan. Gianandrea stepped up, beaming, as winner of the race, and for the first time as driver. I stood somewhat sheepishly behind him as copilot as he received the trophy from Pietro Beccari, CEO of both Louis Vuitton and Louis Vuitton Fashion Group. It was an impressive swirl of Murano glass created by Venini, housed in its own equally impressive traveling case, made (naturellement) at Louis Vuitton’s atelier in Asnières.

Gianandrea has promised me visiting rights.

11 Winter Colognes You Can Get for Under $100

20 Watches You Can Buy on Amazon Under $150

#6708. Is It Just Us? Or Was 2026 the Year of Whimsy?

In an age marred by burnout and algorithms, people are once again making room for the weird, the joyful, and the wonderfully unproductive. We tapped 18 tastemakers to learn how they’re doing it.

Let’s face it: Growing up is hard. One day, you’re listening to The Cheetah Girls soundtrack on the way to school with an armful of Silly Bandz and the next, you’re worried about making rent, hours lost to Google Sheets, and an unexpected medical bill when you still have a $52 request floating in a sea of Venmo transactions.

As for the world we’ve grown into? Nearly two years into Trump’s second presidential term, with AI raising questions about how much of our thinking we’ll hand over to machines, adult life can feel relentlessly optimized and not as chic or shiny as we imagined it as kids. But even if the future feels uncertain, we continue to hope. 2026 has revived the desire to make room for the colorful, playful, and wonderfully impractical again, and that impulse has a name: whimsy.

“As more of our everyday experiences are shaped by technology and algorithms, people are finding value in things that feel more personal, creative, and unexpected. Inspiration is still incredibly important, but so is the freedom to take what you see and make it your own,” Sydney Stanback, global trends and insights lead at Pinterest, tells Cosmopolitan.

“People are craving more imagination and fun in their everyday lives. There is a real appetite for aesthetics that feel less polished and predictable and more personal, and whimsy gives people room to try something they might not have considered before.”

And the numbers don’t lie. Pinterest searches for “bedazzling patterns” have increased by a whopping 9,187 percent this year, while “bedazzled phone case” searches are up 8,012 percent. Even “whimsical office outfits” have jumped 885 percent.

From Kylie Jenner’s bejeweled birthday cake and “princess kitty”–themed fete to the deconstructed silhouettes and glittery garments taking over spring/summer 2027 runways, we’re all finding increasingly creative ways to inject personality into our days. In an increasingly automated world, doing things simply because we can is the ultimate act of rebellion. At its core, whimsy is about making magic out of the mundane, whether we’re revisiting childhood obsessions now that we (sort of) have adult money or taking the DIY approach to life to make it more vibrant.

That’s why Cosmopolitan invited 18 colorful, charismatic, and unapologetically authentic tastemakers to share how they’re romanticizing life and embracing their eccentricities. Because to be whimsical is to be human!

#6705. Noah Cyrus Delivers a Daring Take on Sister-of-the-Bride Attire

She chose a goth naked dress for Miley’s intimate nuptials this weekend

This past weekend, Miley Cyrus and Maxx Morando tied the knot with an intimate ceremony in Palm Springs, California, where both the bride and groom wore Saint Laurent by Anthony Vaccarello. For the occasion, Cyrus chose a silk boatneck maxi dress with an open back, while Morando wore a classic black tuxedo. And based on early photos from the ceremony, it appears that all of their guests were asked to stick to a black-and-white color scheme, too.

This was further evidenced late last night, when Cyrus’s younger sister, Noah, shared a series of Instagram photos showing off her gothic sister-of-the-bride ensemble.

For the wedding weekend, Noah chose a plunging scoop-neck maxi dress in black, made of a semi-sheer fabric that was decorated with jacquard florals. The body-hugging design featured cap sleeves and slight ruffles at the bottom of the skirt, and Noah paired it with a set of matching peep-toe heels, while layering a black blazer on top and matching panties underneath.

Later, based on photos she shared to her Instagram Story, it appears Noah switched into another black dress—a high-neck bodycon piece with a large silk sash tied into a bow along her waist.

Other members of Cyrus’s family who were in attendance included her mom, Tish, and her mother’s husband, Dominic Purcell, as well as Cyrus’s dad, Billy Ray Cyrus, and his girlfriend, Elizabeth Hurley. Miley’s older half-sister, Brandi, was also seen leaving the venue with her fiancé, Matt Southcombe. It is unclear whether Miley’s younger brother, Braison, or her half-brothers, Trace and Christopher, were present.

#6704. Festive Thanksgiving Nail Ideas for Every Kind of Holiday Plan

From cranberry red to coffee-glazed shimmer, these manis are holiday-ready.

Whether you’re curling up by the fire, hosting the family dinner, or bringing dessert to a Friendsgiving, a compliment-worthy manicure is a small comfort when the day gets chaotic. No matter what comes your way, at least your nails are done. And with all the passing, pouring, and photo-posing ahead, your hands are sure to get plenty of attention.

They’ll be passing the cranberry sauce, raising a glass for the toast, clutching a mug of cider—you name it. The good news is that fall offers one of the richest color palettes of the year. Think deep cranberry, burnished copper, mossy olive, and warm cinnamon, all shades that glow against autumn light and flatter nearly every skin tone.

And this season’s nail trends are as varied as the dishes on your table, from minimalist milky neutrals for the understated host to hand-painted leaves and shimmering gold flecks for the one who loves a little drama.

Ahead, check out some Thanksgiving nail ideas to inspire your next set.

You simply cannot go wrong with brown cat-eye nails. Whether you love a long almond silhouette or prefer a shorter, softer shape, this design feels rich, polished, and effortlessly autumnal.

Plaid nails in autumnal shades bring a fresh, playful spin to a fall manicure.

We love an ombré nail set, and this milky-to-espresso gradient fits right in with the season’s warm neutrals. The soft fade from creamy white to deep, coffee-bean brown feels cozy and sophisticated, like a latte in manicure form.

This butternut-squash yellow adds a fun pop of color to a darker fall palette.

Coffee-glazed nails are the manicure equivalent of a warm mug in your hands. It’s an easy pick for anyone who wants a little shimmer without going full metallic.

Can’t pick one fall shade? Wear them all, one on each finger. You’ll be surprised how well they work together.

Rich cherry red nails get a glam upgrade with a delicate gold cuticle line, a classic shade with a jewelry-box finish.

Deep, velvety, and effortlessly chic, this forest-inspired hue pairs beautifully with gold jewelry and cozy knits.

Brown nails with a white chrome topcoat melt into a frosty, taupe-y shade that’s simply stunning.

Rich, moody, and perfectly autumnal, this duo looks like a cranberry-and-herb tablescape on your fingertips.

Tortoiseshell nails are the chicest way to bring warm amber and espresso swirls to your Thanksgiving look.

Sheer, creamy, and effortlessly polished, this minimalist shade pairs with everything from cable-knit sweaters to your fanciest holiday dress.

Delicate, understated, and just a little whimsical, this look pairs a sheer neutral base with one or two hand-painted leaves and a dark French-tip manicure.

#6703. HigherDOSE’s Red Light Hat Is the Most Discreet Hair Growth-Promoting Device Out There—And It’s on Sale for Black Friday

Shop all the brand’s Black Friday LED deals below.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

As someone who constantly wears slicked-back updos, is a hair-bleaching retiree, and is still recovering from an overly short haircut last year, I'm very focused on improving my hair health. I use everything from hair growth serums to strengthening shampoos, and I’m also a major proponent of hair-growth devices.

There's tons of tools on the market that promote hair growth and scalp circulation via LED, but I'm most loyal to HigherDOSE's Red Light Hat. Out of all the helmets and brushes out there, it's the easiest to use, and the most subtle. This accessory lets your red-light treatment go unnoticed—and right now, the hat is 20% off for Black Friday (and other of team Cosmo’s favorite HigherDOSE gadgets are up to 40% off).

From the outside, it looks like your regular branded baseball cap. But on the inside, there are actually 120 LED lights emitting red light (with a wavelength of 650 nm, aka the same strength as your beloved red light mask) directly to your scalp. Red light therapy helps create a healthier scalp environment so hair follicles can function at their best. The wavelengths penetrate the scalp to boost circulation and help deliver oxygen and nutrients to the follicles, which is why these devices can work even better when paired with a hair serum that's packed with nourishing ingredients.

And luckily, when you wear the cap and turn it on (there's a mini battery on the back that you just click to start), you can't see any red light coming from the hat. It just looks like a plain ol' cap, and despite the padding and lights, it's actually comfortable to wear. I even keep a spare one sitting at my desk, and no one in the office knows when I'm undergoing a full hair-strengthening treatment. Plus, the battery life itself is another win—you only need to charge it fully once every few weeks.

If you want to improve your scalp health, Black Friday is the best time to invest in the device with this 20%-off deal. That’s why I love this sauna blanket—it gives me the benefits of a sauna session without irritating my acne-prone skin. I just tuck a towel inside, which I clean between uses to avoid body breakouts, and the interior is easy to wipe down after every session.”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

#6699. Decoding the Over-the-Top Outfits in All’s Fair Season 2

Kim Kardashian, Glenn Close, and more live out a fabulous fashion fantasy in All’s Fair season 2.

No further questions: All’s Fair season 2 is one of the most fashionable series you can stream right now. When the show debuted just over a year ago, it made headlines for being Hulu’s biggest scripted launch in three years. Is it any wonder? It combines drama, style, and female powerhouses with major personality.

The show follows stylish lawyers played by Kim Kardashian, Naomi Watts, Sarah Paulson, Niecy Nash-Betts, Teyana Taylor, and Glenn Close, each with their own distinct aesthetics that range from extremely rare archival ’90s vintage to quiet luxury glam. All’s Fair season 2 debuted a few weeks ago and is well underway, with the finale airing on Wednesday, Dec. 2, 2026. While we wait for the next episodes, we caught up with Paula Bradley, the costume designer behind it all.

When we started out with the new season, we did have a note from Ryan Murphy just to tone it down a little, not a lot, but just a little, in regard to that they are lawyers, not to do so many hats on Niecy—it’s hard for the eyeline. Then it was really just about combing through the season’s collections and seeing who did what, and which silhouettes go where, because at this point we’ve kind of developed our characters. The big difference for season 2 was we really upped our jewelry game.

So, it started with Glenn. She had asked if she could have some real pearls that she just wore all the time. And so we wrote to Tiffany’s, and then we wrote to everybody, we found jewelers that we’d never heard of that did fantastical things. Tiffany loaned us three incredible brooches that were so ridiculously valuable, and some pearls and diamonds and earrings for Glenn. Boucheron did jewelry for everybody for the Scorpio Ball. We had to have new safes installed in the office and in the truck.

Yes. We bought a lot of vintage for Teyana Taylor. We had vintage Alaïa and Gaultier, and we bought an incredible vintage outfit that—I can’t say who wears it in the end—we bought this Galliano runway piece that I had them split into two invoices so that I could put it in the budget. There was a sweater set that was really, really stunning. I don’t dress Kim, Soki Mak dresses Kim. She starts with just going to the auction houses and the vintage places around Europe and Paris, and just collecting everything that she can for Kim.

So, if we start with Sarah Paulson, who plays Carr, she’s the most edgy of all of the characters. She always has a shoulder, sometimes it’s the softer shoulder, but mostly it’s a very sharp shoulder. It was based on the old Saint Laurent. She always has a feminine moment, so the bows have become her—big bows, big earrings, and moments of softness just to soften it up.

Then Naomi Watts (Liberty Ronson) is our quiet luxury. She walks into everything, and she’s monochromatic, although this season we started to pull her a little bit more into the dark side.

Niecy Nash-Betts (Emerald)—it was hard to lose the hats for Niecy, so we went into the belts because her waist is so snatched. And Niecy, for me, represents her own culture incredibly well. That body is so Jessica Rabbit, and it’s so for real. And so she’s always about color, sass, attitude, and glamour. I would say she’s the most glamorous of my characters.

Glenn Close (Dina) is our classic. She’s our classic WASP. So her clothes usually have embroidery or a little bit of extra texture, or she always has a lace edge. She’s a little bit more heirloom, you know? Old Hollywood.

I can. So, I can’t give away the ending because it hasn’t landed yet, and it’s quite a shocker. But obviously the opening, and re-creating the scorpion cape (for Kim) was amazing, because there still are a few ladies who do that beading, who did it in the ’50s and ’60s for Judy Garland and Liza Minnelli, and worked with Bob Mackie. They dip those beads into different solutions of bleach to age them appropriately. They pulled threads to get the edges a little bit frayed. And I worry that that talent and that skill set will soon have disappeared. So that was amazing.

The other thing that I love is the Balenciaga bright yellow highlighter coat that Sarah wears, because she always wears black or gray. And that moment of her just walking through the hallways in that giant oversize cocoon coat, and the glasses and the shoes—we did it just like the runway—was a real joy.

And Naomi, we got her in a couple of Saint Laurent pieces with stronger shoulders, and that was great, too. But the opening—that was supposed to be the opening, but it’s actually the second scene—is also my favorite. So we made Sarah a leather shirt with a giant leather bow, with the shoulders, and Glenn had been at the Erdem show in London, and so we put Glenn in that fabulous denim suit with all the white embroidery. I think it just set the season off.

Sarah has the Fendi that came off the runway this season. And Sarah’s really the only one with a bag game, because she comes in and out of the law firm. So she still carried the Prada from last season into this season, but she also has a really great Balenciaga bag.

I think it’s more the totes—when they have them, it’s the tote bags, because that’s their professional, legal side. They all have a set of work totes that you believe that they could pull documents out of. I think that’s probably the most relatable. I think we all, even though it’s not good for our shoulders, it’s not good for our backs, most women I know, we all have a set of really great tote bags that we put our very heavy computers in and carry all our stuff in. We like a good tote, and I think that’s probably the most relatable part of it.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6696. The Art of the Winter Layer

Use these cold-weather staples from The North Face for practical warmth with polished style.

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like any specialized skill set, winter layering is one that must be continually practiced and perfected, lest your talents wane. It’s not as easy as throwing on a pair of cashmere leggings and a coordinating hoodie—that’s only the beginning. From there, it takes a careful curation of hero pieces (like puffer jackets and parkas), as well as a cast of supporting details (hats, gloves, and high-performance footwear), to layer effectively and aesthetically in the harshest conditions.

That said, even the most adept artist is nothing without an arsenal of trusted creative tools. And in these bitter temps, you’ll want to reach for the best, which essentially means any piece of gear with The North Face’s iconic rectangular logo attached to it. The storied brand has dominated the outdoor space for decades, having perfected the art of elevated style at elevated altitudes.

As we head into the chilliest season of the year, look to The North Face’s vast selection of dependable staples for all your layering needs. Designed both for performance and appearances, these pieces are in it for the long run.

Whether painting or dressing, it’s essential to start any creative endeavor with a blank canvas made of quality materials. A good base should be versatile and simple to pair with a host of helpful staples. Long-sleeve tees, cashmere turtlenecks, and crewneck sweatshirts, for example, are all good jumping-off points.

If you’re looking for a high-performance piece with enough style power to stand on its own, we recommend the Women’s Chabot 2.0 Mock-Neck. This cozy layering top offers the same reliable comfort of a traditional insulated thermal with a few modern updates. Though understated by design, its delightfully oversized fit and a funky waffle knit texture make the basic feel intentional and interesting.

For a closer-fitting base, the Women’s Golden Ridge Ribbed Long-Sleeve Tee layers easily under other pieces. Its slim fit and ribbed texture give this practical layer a polished look, while thumbholes at the cuffs add a functional detail. Bonus points for the tee’s anti-odor technology that helps keep the fabric feeling fresh.

Women’s Golden Ridge Ribbed Long-Sleeve Tee

After you’ve selected the first layer, the next stop should be your coat closet. This is where you’ll select the hero of your look. While each piece of the layering puzzle is uniquely important, the power jacket is a non-negotiable. The right iteration should supply a thick layer of warmth, while also doing its part to make the outfit, as a whole, feel cohesive.

Those who crave a more subdued winter aesthetic will love the classic appeal of the Women’s Arctic Bomber. The waterproof winter jacket boasts all the utility of a parka, with the sleek design details most commonly found in trend-focused outerwear. In four comforting colorways, this winter coat is an insulation powerhouse in a chic, wearable package.

Look to the Women’s Hydrenalite Down Jacket for a more classic puffer. This work-horse is a versatile down layer that can be slipped on at any time to keep you warm for any duration that you spend outdoors, whether you are on the mountain or running around town.

For the more artistically inclined, try the Women’s Yumiori Off Peak Full Zip Jacket. This fleece outer layer offers unfailing warmth and a distinctly sporty flavor. Its two-tone color palette, meanwhile, adds a fun, whimsical feel to the utilitarian, zip-up-style silhouette.

During the summer months, it’s all about the mani-pedi. But once that icy weather blows in, self-care starts to look a bit different. The North Face’s Etip Gloves are basically a hand treatment on the go. Crafted from soft, stretchy fleece made with 93% recycled polyester, these tech-friendly gloves will keep your hands comfortable on cold-weather outings. They’ll also keep you connected: Touchscreen compatibility lets you check your phone without taking them off, while silicone grip dots on the palms help you hold onto your device as well as smaller essentials like keys and credit cards.

For footwear, the Women’s THERMOBALL Traction Mule V will quickly become the standout of your seasonal lineup. These cloud-like slip-ons pair warm insulation with a quilted upper that gives them a casual, streetwear-cool vibe. The rubber outsole adds traction for quick trips outdoors.

The minimalist philosophy requires you to take off one item before leaving the house. But during the cold weather season, it’s in your best interest to do the opposite. As you walk out the door, make sure you grab a beanie since most of your body heat can escape from your head.

Beanies are simultaneously the most underappreciated outfit addition and the most indispensable. The North Face’s Wool Blend Beanie, for instance, pairs the warmth of its 65% merino wool knit with a refined, metropolitan style. Its classic cuffed design and textured knit add visual interest, while its deep fit offers roomier coverage. If you prefer the rectangular logo with the same cuffed look, you can never go wrong with the rib-knit and soft lining of the Salty Lined Beanie. On the slopes or on your morning commute, either will add a polished finishing touch.

The importance of a functional winter hat cannot be overstated—think of it as the final brushstroke in the art of winter layering.

#6695. The Holiday Party Etiquette Rules That Actually Matter, According to Lizzie Post

An expert shares how to celebrate the festive season with polish.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Welcome to In Good Company, where we ring in the festive season with fashion-insider entertaining tips, expert etiquette advice, and the perfect editor-approved hostess gifts.

Holiday entertaining brings with it countless conundrums—from uninvited guests to the cousin who won’t put down their phone—for both hosts and guests. Agility is the key word; being able to elegantly pivot and respond to unexpected situations is what makes people feel comfortable and appreciated. Just in time for festive gatherings, ELLE spoke with Lizzie Post, author, co-president of the Emily Post Institute, and co-host of the Awesome Etiquette podcast, about all the things to keep in mind for a kind and gracious holiday. Read on for Post’s refreshingly down-to-earth tips.

No one can keep up with the incredible party décor shown on influencers’ posts, and you don’t have to try. Elegance can be found in any space, so host within the realities of your home. “You are not meant to be entertaining at the level of someone with a million-dollar budget and a gigantic home. You get to entertain at exactly your level, whatever that is,” Post says. “All the little touches and the things you do to make people feel comfortable and welcome in your home are what make for a great host.”

Bringing the wrong item can be an error on the part of the guest, but hosts should always make sure to minimize discomfort. “You want to take the etiquette high road and let it not be a problem. If [someone brings] an extra guest, quickly enlist someone from your household or a trusted guest to help you zhuzh the arrangements in a discreet way so that the extra person does not feel like they were not meant to be there… [We have] the nearest-and-dearest to the host take smaller portions, so that everyone can have a decent portion.”

Part of smartphone etiquette is letting others make their own choices. “You don’t overly control someone’s phone usage. Their rudeness will show on its own and will be noticed by more than just you… [Saying] ‘Could you make sure your phone is off, so you’re not tempted to use it throughout the evening?’ That is not a polite thing, even though it sounds gentle and so reasonable. That’s micromanagement.”

Bringing a thoughtful gift is always appreciated, but truly engaging with the event is what really matters. “There is no burden for hosting you. You’ve been invited and should feel welcome and like you belong in this space. You are not there to repay the efforts of the host. We are greeting our host. We are engaging with the other people. We are helping ourselves to the refreshments that are offered. If there are games or entertainment, we’re participating in that. If there’s a theme, we’ve showed up in the theme. That’s really how we’re a good guest.”

Always say goodbye. “At the end of the night, say thank you and goodbye and seek out the host, whether it’s a big open house party or it’s a small dinner party to make that connection and express gratitude before we go.”

Serving an item that a guest brought is at the host’s discretion. “A hostess gift is a hostess gift. It is not meant to be used that night with the people who brought it. It can be, but it does not have to be.”

Whether you mail a card or not, make sure to share your appreciation with your host. “Thank-you notes are a wonderful idea and they allow you an extra level of expression, a little more thought. It doesn’t mean that a text message the next day would not also be appreciated. Any version of thanking your host after a party is a great version.”

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.
