#6693. What Is the Royal “Grid” and Why is the Palace Apparently “Infuriated” Over Prince Harry Wanting to Join It?

Prince Harry has been back in the UK for some weeks now, and he’s wasted no time in letting everyone know about it, with plenty of public moments including a surprise appearance on This Morning and a family outing to London's Winter Wonderland. But while fans of the Duke of Sussex celebrate his return, the Palace is said to be “infuriated” with his recent engagements and, in particular, his request to join the “grid.”

Speaking to The Express, royal commentator Richard Fitzwilliams accused Harry and Meghan Markle of purposely timing their public appointments to take attention away from other members of the royal family.

“We must assume this has been deliberate. This is deplorable,” the royal expert said, adding, “[The Palace is] indeed right to be infuriated.”

But while Fitzwilliams accuses Harry of intentionally scheduling his engagements to outshine his royal counterparts, perhaps it's all just bad timing—which is where the royal “grid” comes into things...

The royal “grid” is a meticulously organized schedule of the royal family’s engagements and appointments. Although the actual “grid” is not available to the public, fans of the royal family can keep up with their plans via the official website. According to the site, official royal engagements are published up to eight weeks in advance.

According to The Express, Prince Harry has requested that he be added to the royal “grid.” His motive for the move hasn’t been confirmed, but there are of course plenty of theories. Some critics, like royal commentator Fitzwilliams, believe that Harry wants to intentionally create more scheduling conflicts with the other royals. Others take the more agreeable stance that the duke simply wants to avoid any further calendar clashes.

One insider, for example, said that Harry is “mindful” of his fellow royals and their plans, and tries his best to avoid overlapping with them. “He knows about those things and he is obviously mindful of that as he’s planning his own activities as well,” the source told The Times.

The royal family is split into various households, with Clarence House taking care of King Charles and Queen Camilla and Kensington Palace looking after the Wales family, for example. These households are made up of large teams, covering everything from catering to housekeeping. Also in the royal household are those who plan and manage the family members’ calendars—these are overseen by private secretaries and diary coordinators.

A previous advertisement for such a job at Kensington Palace said, “The role will be responsible for managing the seamless delivery of multiple complex diaries to ensure the smooth and effective coordination of engagements and events.”

As for what the role involved? “Compiling and managing coordinated diary updates and weekly overviews; providing regular briefings; communicating diary changes to all key stakeholders, both internally and externally; and carrying out follow-up actions as necessary, as well as ensuring that all events and engagements are accurately recorded,” the listing explained.

“The successful candidate will have a proven ability to manage complex diaries and will have excellent communication skills,” the vacancy ad went on. Being a team player and having a proactive mindset were just some of the other requirements of the role, in addition to maintaining confidentiality and discretion, which the listing described as “paramount.”

#6692. Kate Middleton and Prince William Begin Their First Overseas Tour in Four Years

The couple returns to India for the Earthshot Prize, which awards £1 million each to five environmental initiatives.

Kate Middleton and Prince William have arrived in India for a three-day official visit, their first joint overseas tour since 2022. Announced by Kensington Palace over the weekend, the November 16–18 trip is in honor of the sixth annual Earthshot Prize awards in Mumbai. It also marks a significant step in the Princess of Wales’s return to public duties following her 2024 cancer diagnosis.

The couple will attend the awards ceremony on Tuesday, November 17, when five environmental initiatives will each receive £1 million to expand their work. Founded by Prince William in 2020, the prize recognizes solutions across five areas, including restoring nature, reducing waste, and addressing climate change. Mumbai is the awards’ first Indian host city, following past editions in London, Boston, Singapore, Cape Town, and Rio de Janeiro.

The royal itinerary extends beyond the ceremony. Kensington Palace said the visit would highlight sustainable urban transportation, responsible manufacturing, youth-led environmental projects, and India’s creative industries. Today, the couple joined cricket great Sachin Tendulkar and local children for a game of street cricket in Ballard Estate, a historic neighborhood in South Mumbai. The Prince and Princess of Wales are also scheduled to attend the inaugural Children’s Earthshot Awards, recognizing environmental solutions developed by young people ages 9 to 17.

The trip is Catherine’s first overseas tour with William since she disclosed her cancer diagnosis in 2024, and her first appearance at an Earthshot ceremony since the Boston awards in 2022. It is also the couple’s second official visit to India, a decade after their April 2016 tour.

#6691. Bella Hadid’s Radical Vulnerability

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

How do you stay open in an industry that demands toughness? Bella Hadid embarks on a new chapter with a renewed sense of optimism and her boundaries firmly in place.

I first met Bella Hadid on a fashion shoot I was styling more than a decade ago. We went on to shoot several stories together, including her first major American magazine cover. On one of those shoots, she arrived upset because she had just found out she didn’t get cast in the Victoria’s Secret show. I will never forget telling Bella that one day, Victoria’s Secret would be begging her to walk for them. Cut to today, and Bella is not only one of the most in-demand models, but she opened their most recent show. When I saw her hit the runway in a beaded floral bra and matching wings, I let out a scream because I knew what that moment meant to her.

A few days after the Victoria’s Secret show in October, Bella and I sat down to reflect on her career so far, forgiveness and success, turning 30, and where she goes from here. I have always felt connected to Bella because she, like me, is a heart-on-her-sleeve kind of gal. She is deeply vulnerable and human, and it’s what makes her one of the most compelling figures in fashion today.

Samira Nasr: This is a full-circle moment. On one of our first shoots together, you were really upset because you didn’t get the Victoria’s Secret show. And I said to you, “One day, they will be begging you to be in their show.” And I could tell you didn’t believe me. Your whole body shut down. And three days ago, you opened Victoria’s Secret. Holy shit.

Bella Hadid: I still don’t believe you, to be honest. I feel like people are just AI-ing me onto the runway. I think that it’s so crazy how time passes and you still have some kind of impostor syndrome. No matter how hard you work, there’s always another girl who is more deserving, more beautiful, and has worked so hard to be able to get that spot. Ten years ago, I remember that moment too, where I never thought I would get the show, but I think that God always has a plan, and I truly believe at that time I wasn’t ready. I don’t think I’ve been ready until this year, to be honest. In past years, I was going to my horse-show finals in the morning, flying straight to the Victoria’s Secret show that day, doing rehearsal right before, and then pulling what we call a Bella, and Belinda comes out, and we just act. But this year, I felt really strong.

I have had to make a decision for myself to have boundaries with my own health. And I think it’s gotten me to this point now where I’m able to do the jobs that I do want to do with people that I love, that I can feel inspired by. And that’s truly what [working in] fashion is about: being around people you feel inspired by. Until you find that inspiration, it’s hard to go back into our industry. I think I needed a little bit of time just to be able to be like, “Let me just remember why I’m doing this again,” because I know I’m more than just a face.

SN: We don’t really support a culture of care. I have always believed that boundaries are bridges.

BH: They really are. We know so many people who have been working for 30, 40, 50 years in this industry with zero boundaries. I’ve watched people over the years just get more depressed because they don’t know themselves and they don’t know who they are. And that’s how I was for 10 years, when I didn’t know what my favorite color was, what I liked to eat. I stopped listening to music. To not be able to know yourself in an industry that tells you how to be every single day is really hard.

SN: Going back to Victoria’s Secret, did you feel empowered, or was that your alter ego? What is her name again?

BH: Belinda. You know what? Fortunately and unfortunately, I think it was Bella this year.

BH: I made up my alter ego, Belinda, a long time ago to deal with the intensity of everything happening in my life. So she could deal with that, and I can deal with this, and we can do this together, which I’m realizing I should probably go to therapy about again. Belinda is the one that people are like, “Wow, this, that.”

I have POTS [postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome], and I have a lot of PMDD [premenstrual dysphoric disorder], endometriosis, plus my Lyme and things that affect my body very deeply. Right before I walked out on the runway for my opening look, I went to go stretch and my whole clavicle completely … I don’t even know what it was. My lungs almost collapsed, and I couldn’t breathe, and then it was like, “Five, four …” So my body goes through these different motions of feeling really good and then all of a sudden crashing on me at the worst moments. I need to realize maybe my body can’t handle all that I have going on. Because I know how to walk. One thing I can do is I can strut. But it’s a lot of pressure; we all have our own insecurities. Sometimes my body just can’t connect with my brain. My brain wants to do it, and my body is like, “No, we can’t do that today.” And I’m like, “Okay, great. So let’s figure out another way.” But I think people don’t realize how much I struggle with the fatigue and the body pain, and so I just try my best.

SN: You’ve achieved so much in your 20s. You just turned 30. What are you looking forward to in your 30s?

BH: It’s funny, today I woke up with—I wouldn’t say the word is anxiety, but this feeling of “Oh my gosh, my life is starting.” I want to get rid of what doesn’t bring me joy and bring in what does. I’ve tried to put myself in positions for work for a very long time that have made me feel out of body or like I don’t belong. Years ago, I was just grateful to get a job, and I would say yes to everything. I lacked boundaries then, but I do believe that that gave me the strength to be able to have the confidence in my own boundaries now. I’ve achieved pretty much all the goals that I wanted to when it comes to fashion as a model. And now I do want to go into other ventures to eventually get to peace and happiness and love within this universe that God has put me in.

In the next couple of years, it’s just about getting back to community, getting back to using my voice again. I got beaten down pretty heavy for a long time, which pushed me into this very big struggle with my physical and emotional health, which makes my brain turn off and makes me not be able to write or read. When you’re not around enough people that are like-minded, you feel really ostracized. That’s almost where I got to. Of course, I always want to speak from my heart, which I hope will never disrespect anybody, but still be able to have the confidence to speak my truth and hope that people will walk beside me along the way when it comes to that.

SN: Is there something you’re most proud of achieving?

BH: Well, first and foremost, starting [my fragrance line] Orebella was something that I truly needed for my own creativity and to be able to build community. I am not a 50/50 person. I’m an all-in type of girl. Being able to move forward into my 30s, really focusing on building the things that I’ve started—Orebella was something that I pushed so hard for since 2018, and the fact that it’s really now coming to fruition makes me so proud.

When I was 17, going to my first go-see with a stylist I will not name, I was basically told I was too big to wear Saint Laurent and needed to lose a couple of pounds. And that’s when I was just a baby coming out of high school. Twelve years later, now I’m the face of Saint Laurent. I’m the face of Prada, which is incredible and insane. Times have changed so much, and the people that have come into the industry to make it a better place for us as models are so important. Not that that comment ever impacted me. I went through enough struggles with my body before that, so by the time she said that, I was like, “Okay, girl, I’m actually coming out of something that I dealt with very heavily when it came to my body. And now I look at food in a different way, and I love my body, and that’s okay if you think that I don’t look the way that you think I should look.”

I just would love for everyone to think about if every day they had to walk outside and be photographed, what that would feel like, because it’s an uncomfortable feeling for anybody. My face would change so much. I would be super puffy one day, and one day I would look yellow and emaciated, and people would think I was on drugs or doing this or that. And when anybody knows about, one, hormones, just as a woman in general, and two, about immune problems and how weight fluctuates—again, I just think that there needs to be more sensitivity in our industry.

SN: I’m curious if you’ve always been this person—so sensitive, empathetic—from a little girl.

BH: I’ve always felt like my sensitivity and my openness were a disservice to myself because I felt like no one else was like me in those ways, so I shut it down. Now I realize that there is a generosity of thoughtfulness that is so much deeper than just buying somebody an expensive gift. I think sitting on the side of the street with somebody, even if you don’t have any money, is more important than just walking by without caring.

SN: You’re able to access compassion when a lot of people can’t, and that is a superpower. I want to talk about style because you mentioned your archive. I remember you once mentioned you collect Gaultier. Do you still collect?

BH: I do collect. Behind me are bags to sell. But downstairs, I have my Gaultier dress that the atelier made. It was a Naomi Campbell dress that she had worn in one of their shows, and I was obsessed with it. I called their PR, and within a week and a half, because that dress was in a museum, I had it made and sent to Cannes, and that was that white-and-black dress that I wore on the carpet. And so, that’s here. My Coperni dress is here. I have a whole section for Virgil [Abloh].

SN: The last time I saw Virgil was with you, actually.

BH: I don’t think I’ve done a real, full Fashion Month since he passed away. And Vivienne [Westwood] and Karl [Lagerfeld] passed, and I felt like all of my idols were leaving, and it felt really scary. I have all the art Virgil gave me, his letter he wrote me, his bags, his shoes he made me. And then I have my Prada area, my Cavalli area, my Bob Mackies that I’ve been collecting over the years.

BH: Hopefully for a future wedding. There are just some dresses you can’t find again. And Bob did it best.

SN: He’s an American icon. I wanted to talk to you about Cannes. You’re such a queen of Cannes now, and I look forward to seeing you there. Is it fun?

BH: This past year was fun. I felt grounded. To do the red carpet and feel, in those moments, sober and happy and healthy and strong, and go to sleep not having anxiety about going out or how I’m going to look the next day, and just being with loved ones. It did really well for me this year.

Cannes has always held a very special place in my heart. It begins long before I arrive. Working closely with designers on custom gowns, exchanging references, and watching those first sketches come to life is such a beautiful part of the experience.

This year, being there to support Marie-Clémentine Dusabejambo’s Ben’Imana, the first Rwandan film in the official selection, was especially meaningful. It explores memory and reconciliation after the genocide against the Tutsi through women whose lives carry its legacy. That’s what I love about cinema: It brings us closer and gives stories too rarely heard the attention they deserve. You can enter a theater knowing nothing of someone’s world and leave carrying a little of it with you.

SN: I look at you and how hard you’ve worked for so long, from our first job together. To me, it feels like you’ve achieved so much. But what does success look like for you? Do you feel you’ve achieved it?

BH: I talked to my business manager yesterday, so that made me feel a little bit more successful. But when you don’t think about the money part of it, I think success truly comes from peace within. At the end of the day, I really don’t need much. That’s why I do give back so much, because a lot of the money that I do make, I don’t feel deserving of. There are so many people in the world, whether it’s doctors or teachers, that I think deserve my pay rate a lot more than I do.

From an external space, success is about how known you are, how much you make, how many times you can be on TV, how many covers you’re on, how many fashion shows you do. And that is really important in our industry, especially when you’re building. But for me, I am different. Success, for me, can be living in peace, in a small town, with a man that I love, with my child, having my animals. I’ve always wanted to start a farm-to-table bakery, with flowers, and everything comes from the community. I want to do things that are fulfilling for me in my soul: having a greenhouse that I can garden in, having my glassblowing and ceramic studio, and having lots of children and being a mother. It wasn’t a dream that I’ve always had, but it’s something that now is so intense for me. My intuition is screaming so loudly at me about it.

I think knowing there is a reason behind everything is so important. My sister and I donated a million dollars to Palestine last year [the donation was split between HEAL Palestine, Palestine Children’s Relief Fund, World Central Kitchen, and United Nations Relief and Works Agency], which we could have never been able to do without the success that we’ve had.

God gave me the platform to be able to speak about the things that I hold dearly to me. And when it comes to Palestine or Sudan or Congo or Rwanda or places that mean so much to not only myself but the people around me—to give people a voice in any capacity, that’s success for me as well. To make my family wide and far proud, or to see themselves in me, it gives me strength.

This story appears in the Winter issue of Harper’s Bazaar.

#6682. A Kitchen in Bloom: How Tiffany Howell and Miele Reimagine the Heart of the Home

Eschewing conventional design for an moody, romantic kitchen, this wildly glamorous space proves that high-tech convenience can foster intimacy, slowness, and the art of living well.

When Tiffany Howell of Night Palm Interiors designs a home, her vision transcends conventional norms, veering into an ethereal realm—an approach she calls “the poetry of place.” In Howell’s mind, film, fashion, and music are as essential as decorative art, and she uses these inspirations to create theatrical spaces that ignite all the senses—layering texture, sound, scent, light, and form to evoke deep emotion. When executed with this level of intention, a room becomes an environment you don't merely look at but fundamentally feel.

When Miele partnered with Howell to create a dream kitchen for an installation at Art Basel, she seized the opportunity to reimagine what a kitchen can be. The result is the interactive 360-degree rendering seen here—a wildly imaginative digital environment that inspired the brand’s upcoming physical installation at Miami’s premier art fair. “I wanted an undeniable sexiness, a beautiful, sensory experience,” she says. “I created the space with characters in mind that I’d invite to a dinner party in this kitchen.” After all, the kitchen is a home’s heartbeat, an interactive space with multiple purposes.

“I do think the kitchen is really one of the most tender rooms in the house,” Howell says. She leaned into that romance by installing black cabinetry with blossoming branches and birds in iridescent greens and pinks. The cabinets are an homage to her love of 1920s chinoiserie furniture and fashion, seen through a very glamorous, Los Angeles lens. That California focus also meant bringing the outdoors in. The grand conservatory space harnesses diffused, natural light with the promise of the scent of jasmine wafting into the room. Howell’s ideal playlist sets the tone with jazz greats and ambient synth-pop.

“I wanted to create a kitchen that’s a completely sensory experience,” Howell explains. “The sound of a crackling warm fireplace, the aroma of a meal cooked for loved ones, or of making coffee in the morning.” For Howell, these aren't incidental details; they are the sensory architecture that turns raw space into lived emotion. In Howell’s view, this kitchen is “also a place where you can take a nap, read a book, or write in your journal. Every moment in this room can become a ritualistic experience of living well.”

When it came to the practical needs of a kitchen, Howell used Miele as a muse, an invitation to eschew the pale minimalism so common in luxury kitchens. “The appliances are so elegant, they kind of act as the jewelry of the room,” she says. “And their functionality and ease of operation give you back time to live better, relax with a drink, hang out with family and friends, and create the stories that make up a life.”

Consider the Miele Built-In Coffee System and wine cooler in matching dark finishes which are nearly camouflaged so the technology reads more as furniture than appliance. That tension between heirloom design and high-tech styling becomes a unified part of a lived-in, well-collected home. Accent pieces, including a vintage Fortuny pendant lamp over the kitchen island, and upholstered chairs in the conservatory with detailing reminiscent of antique lingerie, create a moody environment well suited to Miele's black-finished lines that resemble rich obsidian.

The 36” Full Surface Induction Cooktop features a frameless, flush design that disappears into a dark-veined countertop. Transforming the entire glass surface into one expansive cooking area, it fits up to six pots or pans at once and with Intelligent Pan Recognition and SmartSelect White controls seamlessly transfer power settings wherever pans are moved. On the same wall is the 30” Handleless Combi-Steam Oven XXL, with DualSteam technology that emanates ASMR sounds of steam cooking, and a black glass façade that resembles a framed panel or window. From the cool, flush glide of the induction cooktop to the whisper-quiet hum of precision steam, the technology engages the senses rather than interrupting them, turning modern convenience into an intuitive, tactile ritual. Rather than breaking up the wall with a stack of conspicuous appliances, these seamless integrations keep the architecture artful. And for added convenience, Miele’s elegant touch controls are enhanced with Wi-Fi connectivity for an easier user experience.

Howell’s concealment of technology is exhibited in her use of the 42” French Door Refrigerator with its elongated custom handles and a dark lacquered paneled front that echoes the floral chinoiserie motifs—an unconventional solution that turns a mundane appliance into a piece of decorative art. Once the refrigerator doors are open, the Brilliant Light Pro illumination makes it easy to survey the food inside the large capacity interior.

“How do you nurture yourself and others in the kitchen?” Howell asks. Her answer: a design that creates a restorative space evoking intimacy and slowness, where technology can encourage a lightness and give us back more time. By composing every sight, surface, scent, and sound into a single harmonious narrative, Howell proves that a kitchen shouldn't just be an aesthetic statement—it should be something you feel in your bones. “What excites me is building a new world with more possibilities,” Howell says. “I don’t think I could design any other way.”

#6678. How to Watch the ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Finale Live (on the West Coast, Too!) and What to Expect

Attention everyone confused about “Instant Dances….”

Dancing With the Stars is wrapping up an equal parts messy and incredible season, and the finale is doing things a litttttle differently. From a lineup of incredible performances, to Instant Dances that could turn somewhat chaotic, to a change to the show’s previously established air time, we have all the details on what to expect—and how to watch live!

So you will be busy all night. Prepare yourself accordingly.

For the first time ever, the show will air its three-hour finale live on both the East Coast and the West Coast. Typically, DWTS only airs live on the East Coast due to ABC prioritizing “local news and the coveted primetime programming slots,” and West Coast viewers have to avoid spoilers and watch the show late.

The Dancing With the Stars finale will air Tuesday, November 24, at 8 p.m. ET and 5 p.m. PT. It will then air again at 8 p.m. PT for those West Coast viewers unable to catch the live viewing in the late afternoon.

Just five! They are, in no particular order:

Each couple will perform three total dances: an elaborate Freestyle routine, a Judges’ Choice routine (in case it’s not obvious, this is a routine where the judges pick a dance style for each couple), and an Instant Dance, which they learn on the fly.

Per ABC, “The couples will each take on the ultimate test of improvisation: the ‘Instant Dance Challenge,’ without knowing the dance style or the song until only minutes before performing it LIVE!”

#6677. 3 Gilded Age Estates You Can Buy Right Now

Envious of HBO’s The Gilded Age? Live out your Russell family dreams in one of these real-life period estates.

Above: Listed for $75 million, the Hillandale estate sits on 268 acres in Stamford, Connecticut.

HBO’s The Gilded Age is back for a fourth season, once again bringing viewers into the lavish homes where the Russell and van Rhijn families’ dramas unfold. Filmed at some of the era’s most impressive estates, the series serves up grand ballrooms, château-style manors, and sumptuous drapery in abundance. It’s enough to stir up a little real estate envy. Fortunately, three extraordinary Gilded Age homes are currently on the market, offering the chance to live out that fantasy in real life. Take a peek inside below.

Dating to the turn of the century, this lavish European-style estate—known as Hillandale—once belonged to the Sulzberger family, owners of The New York Times. Located along the Connecticut border with New York, the star of the 268-acre compound is an eight-bedroom mansion designed by architecture firm Hunt & Hunt that spans more than 15,000 square feet. Featuring elaborate millwork, moldings, plasterwork, and stained glass, the manor features plenty of amenities. Olympic-sized indoor pool? Check. Elevator? There are two. Grounds? They include formal gardens, four guest houses, a private lake, and even a hedge maze. The opulent spread is listed with Leslie McElwreath of Sotheby’s International Realty.

An Anglo-Italianate brownstone in New York’s Chelsea Historic District, the Van Ness House was built in the mid-19th century and was later enlarged to its present 7,800-square-foot size. Revamped by ELLE Decor A-List designer Miles Redd, the seven-level landmark pays homage to its elegant roots, with preserved plaster moldings, custom millwork, elegant wallcoverings, and heated oak floors in a retro chevron pattern. In the back, a landscaped private garden is paved in limestone and dotted with linden trees that turn golden in the fall. Atop the five-bedroom townhouse, which is listed with Nick Gavin and Amber Gregory of Compass, is a rooftop garden with views of Saint Peter’s Church.

Spanning 14,100 square feet, this Beaux-Arts mansion right off Fifth Avenue was constructed in 1902 by architect James R. Turner and William G. Killian. Its interiors, meanwhile, are by John Russell Pope, the genius behind the Jefferson Memorial and the National Gallery of Art. Clad in a medley of brick, limestone, and marble, the eight-bedroom house has retained its stately proportions throughout the years, including a formal dining room lined in carved wood panels. Also inside are a trio of staircases, an elevator servicing six of the seven floors, and two rooftop terraces. It’s listed for $35 million with Brian Meier and Amanda Barba of Berkshire Hathaway HomeServices.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6675. This New Jaeger-LeCoultre Watch Looks Perfect for Robert Pattinson’s James Bond

The actor’s favorite watchmaker has unveiled a pink-gold Reverso with impeccable timing.

Just a week after Robert Pattinson was announced as the next James Bond, Jaeger-LeCoultre, the Swiss watchmaker for which he's an ambassador, has unveiled a new Reverso that looks rather well suited to 007.

It's certainly a good match for Pattinson's personal tastes. “I definitely like really thin watches,” he said in a recent interview. “That's the main thing. In general, I would probably prefer a leather strap.”

The new Reverso Classic Monoface Small Seconds checks both boxes. Measuring just 7.56mm thick, it has an 18-carat pink-gold case, a silver-colored dial with a texture inspired by stacked coins, and a black alligator strap. Its smaller proportions–40.1mm by 24.4mm—recall the original Reverso of 1931, whose ingenious swiveling case was designed to protect the watch during polo matches. Inside is a manually wound movement with a 42-hour power reserve.

At $18,400, it's a somewhat more elegant alternative to the more beefy dive watches traditionally associated with Bond. And with its art deco styling and slim profile, it would slip neatly beneath a dinner-jacket cuff with an ease the next Madeleine Swann would surely appreciate.

Of course, Omega has been Bond's official watch partner since 1995, and there's no suggestion that's changing. But as a watch for the man playing Bond, this new Reverso feels intriguingly on point.

#6661. Warren Beatty and Annette Bening’s Former L.A. Mansion Lists for Nearly $15 Million

The Hollywood power couple custom-built the gated Beverly Hills house.

The longtime Beverly Hills home of Warren Beatty and Annette Bening will soon hit the market, ELLE Decor can exclusively reveal. The married couple custom-built the gated residence in 1992 and raised their four kids there. The stars are one of Hollywood’s most famous power couples: Beatty has received 14 Academy Award nominations, winning for Best Director in 1982, and three Golden Globe Awards, while Bening has received five Oscar nominations, including Best Actress for 1999’s American Beauty, for which she won the Golden Globe.

The A-list actors parted with their longtime property in 2012, selling it for $6.7 million, per Zillow. Its new asking price will be $14.995 million.

The nine-bedroom estate’s current owners—hotelier Jonathan Baker and his wife, Jenny—acquired the property directly from Beatty and Bening, then spearheaded a $5 million renovation that concluded in 2019. They modified the Mediterranean-style home to have a more contemporary feel, with minimal moldings, glossy surfaces, modern furnishings, and bold splashes of color.

However, they still paid homage to the estate’s movie-star pedigree, creating what they call the “Warren Beatty Theater,” a 17-seat screening room in what was once Beatty’s home office.

Other nods to the legendary actor—who starred in classics like Bonnie and Clyde, Dick Tracy, and Bulworth—included integrating his drop-down projection screen into the new living room. Additional perks include a wine room with a suspended-wire storage system, a wood-paneled dressing room with plenty of built-ins, and a network of terraces.

The home’s amenities continue on the manicured grounds, which feature an infinity-edge pool, a lighted tennis court, multiple al fresco entertaining areas, and a separate guest house.

The couple are parents to four children, among them Ella Beatty, who is following in her parents’ acting footsteps and recently played Lizzie Borden in the Netflix series Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story.

The Beverly Hills house will be listed for $14.995 million with Cory Weiss of Douglas Elliman and Jacob Greene of the Altman Brothers at Douglas Elliman.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6660. Bono and Anton Corbijn Open Up About the Creative Partnership That Changed Rock ’n’ Roll History

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It is the most extensive collaboration ever between a band and a visual artist. Here, the frontman and photographer take Esquire behind the scenes of the album covers, music videos, and photos shoots that have defined their careers.

“Bono is usually a man with a lot of ideas,” says photographer/director Anton Corbijn, “so initially we have a conversation and then maybe I make it more theatrical or whatever.”

That overly casual-sounding process has led to the most extensive collaboration ever between a band and a visual artist. Since their first encounter in 1982, Corbijn has helped establish the evolving public image of U2 across album covers, music videos, and photo shoots for almost 45 years, including the landmark cover of The Joshua Tree, the all-out aesthetic reboot for Achtung Baby, and music videos including “Pride (In the Name of Love)” and “One.” (The partnership is extensively documented in Corbijn’s 2005 book U2&i.) Most recently, they reconnected for a connected pair of surreal and evocative videos—“The Greatest Show on Earth” and “Can’t Stop Us Now”—from U2’s new album Carnaval de Luz, the band’s first full-length release in nine years.

Bono recalls first noticing Corbijn’s work when he was still a teenager, especially a photo of Joy Division that ran on the cover of NME in 1980 following the suicide of lead singer Ian Curtis. “Seeing Anton make these images of these people, they were actual human beings,” he says. “We think from their music that they’re certainly not, but then these images prove that they’re people who lived just across the way. It’s like making movies.”

Indeed, Corbijn has gone on to direct films, including the acclaimed 2007 Ian Curtis biopic Control, as well as doing videos and album covers for Nirvana, Tom Waits, Metallica, the Rolling Stones, and R.E.M. and decades of ongoing work with Depeche Mode.

“It’s difficult for a photographer to shoot bands,” he says, “because bands have an identity, and you need to somehow get that right. It’s very important that the pictures I do with Depeche Mode don’t look like the pictures I do with U2.”

Recently, Bono (in New York City) and Corbijn (in Amsterdam) met up on Zoom to discuss their unprecedented alliance, playfully referring to each other as “A” and “B” and recalling shared moments and mutual inspiration. (They also revealed that Corbijn made a film about the American leg of the 2017 Joshua Tree 30th-anniversary tour, which they are planning to bring to local screens during next year’s Carnaval de Luz: The Greatest Show on Earth dates.)

“There are, of course, many occasions where a great artist springs up on their own—a painter, a songwriter, a photographer, a filmmaker, novelist,” says Bono. “But often there’s some kind of community. U2’s music certainly comes out of community, and Anton is a huge part of our community.”

This conversation was edited for length and clarity.

Bono: The great stories in songwriting are often unexplained, not resolved. And Anton, your gift to U2 was that you want the emotions to cohere rather than to explain the music. If everything adds up, it adds up to nothing. It’s the thing that you can’t explain that keeps you going back to the work—which sounds like that might be an excuse for laziness, for half-finished lyrics, but I felt over our time together that you finished some of the lyrics for me with your images.

The comedy and the tragedy of “The Greatest Show on Earth” is a real example of that. I hope it’s not too close to the bone with the singer playing the leader of a religious cult and the women doing all the work. But look, anyone who can get the members of U2 to put on beards and look like ZZ Top is some sort of shaman. To do one of your film clips is to risk being very silly—and whilst I don’t have that fear in general, as is obvious from the pratfalls I’ve made in my life and artistic life, the other three members of the band do have a certain amount of self-respect, and so only their faith in you as an artist and trust in you as a friend would have them participate at the level they did.

Anton Corbijn: I don’t want to make anybody look silly, but weird is good, and strange, and like you said. I have a very intuitive approach, and we collaborate. Famously, what became the cover of The Joshua Tree was me looking at the desert for a photograph for an album U2 were calling Desert Songs.

Bono: Yeah, yeah, that’s right. Another [potential] title was The Two Americas. But wasn’t it on that shoot we found the title? Via you, and especially the way you pronounced “Yah-shua Tree.” I hope you still find it amusing that we find your Dutch accent amusing.

But as often happens in art, the reason becomes clear years later. That’s the strangest thing—it didn’t start with the Joshua tree; it’s at the end you discovered it. On Carnaval de Luz, we knew from the beginning what we were thinking about, but we discovered it at the very end with the image maker, with the icon maker.

Corbijn: I remember we had a three-day shoot, and we were driving from Reno to Palm Springs, and after the first day, we walked together to the hotel in the desert. And I told you about this tree that I liked so much. I had this landscape camera where the lens moved, so you could get very long shots. I thought it was really nice that you have a tree on the front, and the image goes on to the back, and the band is on the back. So I was talking about that, and I mentioned the name of that tree. And then, the next morning, you came down with the Bible in your hand and said, “It’s got to be The Joshua Tree!”

Bono: We had no idea that Joshua is the same name as Jesus, Yeshua, and what post-punk band would put an album out called The Jesus Tree? But we forget, because the world is going through some parched times presently, that even though the ’80s seemed to be great for a lot of people—you had the Material Girl and dance music, and New York was flying—there was something up in the ’80s. Springsteen would say there was a darkness on the edge of town. And I think for us to dress as pilgrims, as Irish people coming to America, was absurd but actually very effective.

We were trying to figure out, How do you not look stupid on a magazine cover? How are you to be? I mean, we were in our early 20s, and we were not elegantly wasted, as was kind of the mood of the time—if you’re in a rock ’n’ roll band, you should have the good manners to at least look like you’re about to die any minute. You don’t want to look earnest. And we needed to find our own way of, How do we be ourselves? What masks can we choose to reveal ourselves? What is the iconography of whatever the project is? That naturally led on to Zoo TV and all the various shoots we did for Achtung Baby and Zooropa and all that.

My favorite session of ours, Anton, is when we shot for Pop in Miami. They’re some of my favorite photographs of the band because you seem to photograph what the work is rather than what the people are. So for Pop, you went for bright colors and mischief and charm. Anton, do you remember those sessions in Miami?

Corbijn: Yeah, vividly actually, because you were still recording at the time, in the basement in a hotel. We were young and playful, and so many different options with clothing as well. And we tried funny-looking shots and playful shots, serious stuff as well, but I think after The Joshua Tree, I was attempting to bring more life into the pictures with color use. I also remember you saying that at the time that you liked the colorful stuff I did with Depeche. It was not new for me, but we did cross-processing so the color became very vivid, and that’s why Achtung Baby looks so colorful.

Bono: We always live by this sort of modus that the songs will tell us what to do. The songs will tell us how to look, how to behave, what the live show is about, how to dress onstage and sometimes even offstage—and because your thing was, “I’m shooting the song; I’m shooting the material, not you,” I think that really worked for us.

One thing I’d like to ask you, Anton: Would you be comfortable talking about the religiosity of your work? It’s a recurring theme for you, and can you explain a little bit of that?

Corbijn: Not really, actually. I don’t go to church anymore. My father was a pastor, and my grandfather, and all my uncles, and so it sort of made me walk away from it. But I think because I grew up with it, it’s just in my visual language. We grew up next to a cemetery, and maybe that had something to do with it, but the visual iconography, the Christian iconography, is in our society so much that it’s hard to not use it. Sometimes it’s just there—like the first pictures I did with Depeche Mode in ’81, when they were a teeny-tiny pop band, was in this studio in London, and there was this cross with Jesus on it in the garden. So in the photograph, they were like teenybop guys, but still Christ is hanging in the picture.

Bono: Religion has often been such a negative force in the world. It’s very understandable that we would try to avoid the obvious imagery associated with it, but in my life it’s turned into a very positive thing. It’s sort of buoyed me up. But you have it in all your work, despite your attempt to get away from it, I suppose.

Corbijn: I was just thinking, in the “One” video, there’s the Father, Son, and the Holy Ghost, and the band, and the woman. It was all in there. I think it’s very nice that I knew your father and you met my father.

Bono: Yes, that’s right. Two powerful men in our lives. But again, your ability to talk people into doing things—you asked my father to stand on a seesaw, and he went along with it, he was fascinated by it. And then you got the four fathers of U2, the fathers and sons.

Corbijn: That was one of my favorite pictures of U2. There’s a lot of depth in it.

Bono: What other favorites do you have, Anton?

Corbijn: Well, I made a book of 500 pages on U2, full of pictures I like. I like some of the photographs we did around Rattle and Hum. That was not a good time for me, but I think there’s some good pictures there. Also on the rooftop in L.A.—I don’t mean the playing one, but the one when we looked at the rooftop, when we discovered it.

Bono: I found this hotel, a real hotel called the Rosslyn Million Dollar Hotel. I was reading Bukowski at the time, listening to Tom Waits, and I walked into this hotel. I couldn’t believe it existed. Eventually I managed to convince Wim Wenders to make a film there. And we so loved the sign, the Million Dollar Hotel, that when we played on that rooftop, we rebuilt the sign.

Corbijn: Yeah but on a much smaller rooftop. I’m in the video, actually. I’m trying to get through the crowd, pointing to the policeman that I have a pass, and he goes, “You go that way!”

Bono: That poor man, that policeman who pulled the plug. By all accounts, his children tortured him, and he was the most unpopular cop in Los Angeles. He was just doing his job.

Let me see if I one more question for my hero and friend...

#6659. Gigi Hadid’s “High School Curls” Are Actually Perfect for Anyone with Long Hair

And proof that a little teasing can go a long way

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Plenty of beauty lovers can appreciate an effective slick-back style—myself included—but supermodel Gigi Hadid is making the case for undone, playful hair that doesn’t take itself too seriously.

In her latest “High School Curls” look, created by the star’s stylist Dimitris Giannetos for a recent photo shoot (perhaps an upcoming Guest in Residence drop?), Hadid’s hair is as lived-in as it is carefree, featuring loose, beachy waves and a relaxed center part with plenty of body and texture. The airy style lets the tonal dimensions of her blonde and bronde coloring shine through.

“High school curls are the hair you don’t put too much thinking behind it,” Giannetos writes in an Instagram post about the style. “You know what you like, you know how to style your hair but not put too much effort to it! It’s the High School Curls!”

The easy beauty look is touchable and voluminous, almost next of kin to the staple bombshell glamour styles seen on the Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show catwalk, where Hadid is a longtime resident. Along with incorporating piecey, mid-back waves throughout her hair for a slightly grungier look, the stylist tousled Hadid’s roots at the crown to maximize overall fullness.

#6658. The Best Thanksgiving Tabletop Ideas for a Chic, Memorable Feast

Thoughtful themes and simple accents to make your holiday table feel memorable.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Just when Thanksgiving felt like a lifetime away, it’s finally here. If it’s your turn to host Thanksgiving or Friendsgiving this year, you have no shortage of themes to choose from, whether elevated, playful, or a little of both. Tableware has become an event in its own right, and the table you set can be as memorable as the meal itself.

And the best part? You don’t need a designer’s budget or a showroom’s worth of serveware to pull it off. Start with what you already own, then layer in a few thoughtful extras: a handful of taper candles, a fall foliage tablecloth, or elevated glassware. The most striking tables this year feel gathered rather than bought, and they leave plenty of room for your own personality to take a seat alongside your guests.

Ahead, our favorite Thanksgiving tabletop themes, curated to help you host your best dinner yet.

A fall foliage tablescape feels warm and inviting, built on rich browns and earthy neutrals. Fall leaves take center stage alongside amber or orange glassware, a leaf-printed tablecloth, and other nature-inspired accents like leaf taper holders, cream-colored placemats, and pinecones tucked between candlesticks.

Juliska Forest Walk 15" Gratitude Platter

L’Objet Prism set of four glass wine glasses

This vibrant green tabletop feels fresh and lively, perfect for a laid-back Friendsgiving filled with chatter, laughter, and plenty of second helpings. Layer sage linens with floral, green taper candles, hints of wood accents, and chic everyday glassware, then add a few grapes or artichokes for a touch of contrast and unexpected texture.

West Elm Organic Shaped Wood Serving Platters

Terrain Oaxacan Floral Taper Candle, Large Multicolor

For a Thanksgiving theme that feels chic and moody, consider a candlelit dinner with golden brass details, deep burgundy place mats, and rich natural textures. Cluster brass candlesticks down the center of the table, then add dark velvet napkins, smoky glassware, and a handful of dried florals in wine and plum tones. The mood’s all set!

Magnolia Cosmos Block Printed Reversible Placemat

Roan Iris Vintage-inspired Fluted Candle Holder

Who says Thanksgiving dinner can’t be bright and colorful? For a tabletop that truly livens up the party, go for a rainbow sherbet theme, pairing an ombré tablecloth with matching napkins and golden accents to ground the whole look. Finish with candy-colored glassware and a few pretty blooms for a tablescape no one will soon forget.

Williams Sonoma Presidio Antique Brass Napkin Rings, Set of 4

CB2 Rush 20-Piece Brushed Gold Flatware Set

For minimalists who still want a compliment-worthy dinner, this theme brings a thoughtful, elevated touch to a special meal. Stick to a restrained palette of burgundy, stone, and silver, then let texture do the work with textured servers, glossy ceramic plates, and simple white taper candles. Make it feel extra special with handwritten placecards and glass tea light holders. Less really is more here.

CB2 Prado Oxblood Dinner Plates With Reactive Glaze Set of 6

CB2 Aluminum Wine Chiller and Snack Bowl Set of 2 by Nina Garcia

CB2 Anis Burgundy Universal Wine Glass by Nina Garcia

Love Shack Fancy Silver Triple Condiment Server

Lulu and Georgia Piper Tea Light Candle Holder

#6657. Why Does A Crypto Bro On ‘The Altruists’ Have The Most Mature Erotic Fan Fiction Take?

Broken clocks are right twice a day about *checks notes* Dramione.

What sets The Altruists apart from other limited series about millennial scammers in the 2010s is a love story between two like-minded individuals who just *got* each other’s quirks, habits, and Harry Potter fandom. Sure, the relationship between Sam Bankman-Fried (Anthony Boyle) and Caroline Ellison (Julia Garner) was a contributing factor to their corporate failure and fraudulent activities. But it’s compelling. And, in a time where geeky pursuits are increasingly under the microscope, The Altruists presents the most mature and romantic view of erotic fan fiction that we’ve seen in a while.

Early on in The Altruists, Sam discovers that Caroline reads and writes explicit Draco/Hermione fan fiction. (Because television is a visual medium Caroline also prints her story out, which is odd. Not a bad idea, though, given recent events.) He laughs nervously at first, but then reacts positively. “This is hot,” he insists. “You know what you like. You know how to express yourself. You know what turns you on. And you can talk about it. I think you’re incredible. I think you’re so cool.”

Holy nuance, Batman! Why does a 23-year-old MIT grad have a less judgmental take on erotica than half of the think pieces I’ve read over the last 2 to 3 years? Yes, exploring fantasies and kinks and learning how to express them in a safe environment are some of the many positives about fan fiction and/or romance as a genre. This opinion is surprisingly mature for a guy who wants to turn finance into a video game, dresses like Mark Zuckerberg’s kid brother, communicates via memes, and almost exclusively hires his childhood friends. Granted, in the moment he wants to impress Caroline, and The Altruists is not a documentary. But it goes to show that, when it comes to nerdy things, you really do have to ask a nerd.

The real Ellison is a well-documented Harry Potter fan and a LARPer. According to the NY Post, she wrote a regency romance novel while awaiting sentencing. Fortune’s profile of Ellison includes her original fantasy writing. Gawker went ham on Ellison’s Tumblr and found a link to Gossip Girl fan fiction. You can skim an archive of an archive of those Tumblr posts and see references to Lore Olympus, the fic “Harry Potter and the Methods of Rationality,” posts comparing herself to Hermione, and more... but no real evidence that she posted to FF.net or AO3 herself.

The Altruists is fiction land. Not every character shares Sam’s tolerant views, sadly. When Caroline’s coworkers find the story, cheekily titled “A Ride On Draco’s Nimbus,” they make fun of her. Anticipating that she’ll no longer be taken seriously at pitch meetings, she quits. This frees her up to start working with Sam, who again assures her that he doesn’t find her writing fan fiction embarrassing. Later, one of the only other female employees at FTX mocks Caroline behind her back.

But in The Altruists finale, when the press tries to expose Caroline’s “controversial” writing, Sam shuts them down in a televised interview.

I’m just going to say it: that line is so romantic. It’s such an ingenious choice to have a public defense of writing fan fiction serve as Sam’s final declaration of love on The Altruists. He says it direct to camera, as if he knows Caroline is watching. That’s how he expresses to her, and the world, that he still cares about her. You know what else is hot? Having a moral line you refuse to cross, no matter what happens.

Also, in the words of terminally online millennials everywhere, he’s right and he should say it! As the fan-fiction-to-novel-to-screen adaptation pipeline (the After series, The Love Hypothesis, Game Changers AKA Kip and Scott’s book from Heated Rivalry, etc.) pushes the form into the mainstream, outsider efforts to understand fan fiction’s pull unfortunately tend to further “other” it. Even calling something a guilty pleasure still implies that it’s something one should feel shame about. When headlines about fan fiction open with “Why Are You So Into This” and “Celebrities Reveal How They Really Feel” the clear negative connotation hangs above the most positive arguments. What if we didn’t do that?

Throughout the series, The Altruists will occasionally show Caroline self-inserting as Hermione through the fic that she writes. It’s not framed in a way that makes her look especially silly or childish. It just tells the audience how she’s feeling about Draco Sam at any given time, simple as that. It’s a creative outlet! Maybe we should, and I can’t believe I’m saying this, be more like Sam Bankman-Fried*.

Unfortunately, I’m not even sure we can prove Dramione is Ellison’s Harry Potter OTP IRL. Pity, because Boyle originated the role of Draco’s bookish son Scorpius Malfoy in the play Harry Potter and the Cursed Child. What better meta silver lining could a convicted felon fangirl ask for? She needs it, too, because I don’t think the real Bankman-Fried defended her like that either. Bummer.

*A fictional version of Sam Bankman-Fried, written by Graham Moore and portrayed by Boyle in The Altruists, a work of fiction. Don’t get it twisted!

#6656. How Tiffany & Co. Made a Bird on a Rock Box

Can inspiration be infinite? Yes, if you know where to look.

Above: Tiffany & Co. Singing Bird on a Clock and Bird on a Rock brooch, 800-843-3269.

How does a yellow cockatoo flitting near an open window in Guadeloupe end up a jewelry icon? That bird knew what it was doing when it perched outside the home of Jean Schlumberger, one of Tiffany & Co’s best-known jewelry designers. Nature was always the apple of Schlumberger’s eye, and in his vision that bird would become a diamond-studded feathered friend nestled in lapis lazuli and pinned on Bunny Mellon’s beret (he would go on to make for her a full, whimsical bestiary. Theirs was a partnership of hundreds of jelly fish, camels, and seahorses, plus the beloved fruit and flowers inspired by Mellon’s own garden).

That was 1965. How to keep reaching new heights? A partnership between Tiffany & Co. and the Musée des Arts Décoratifs (MAD) in Paris offers inspiration: An archive of thousands of Schlumberger’s sketches, now being restored and digitized, reveal a mind constantly at work and a vision without boundaries. And so it continues, in spessartine and chrysoprase, in rubellites and mother-of-pearl, and turquoise. The next reincarnation of the bird, though, might be one of the most exciting horological spectacles of the year: a glimmering, singing automata atop a burst of sun, a singing gold and titanium bird on a clock. Though, we must note, it does not play music: instead, it tweets. Look, and listen.

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

Editor-in-Chief Stellene Volandes is a jewelry expert, and the author of Jeweler: Masters and Mavericks of Modern Design (Rizzoli).

#6655. It’s Time to Take Full Stock of Bond 26

Robert Pattinson is the new 007, with Denis Villeneuve directing and Steven Knight writing. From the villain to a possible release date, here’s what we know about Bond’s next chapter.

The search for the new James Bond was one of Hollywood’s most closely scrutinized casting processes. Producer Amy Pascal said the team was being “really, really methodical” in its search for Craig’s successor, and the shortlist of hopefuls was as long as Bond’s list of ex-flings, with about the same survival rate.

Josh O’Connor was an early favorite until he stepped back in October, saying he planned a career break in 2027; the Daily Mail later reported that he had turned the role down. Elsewhere, the betting and the gossip circled Slow Horses heartthrob Jack Lowden, Jacob Elordi, James Norton, Jack O’Connell, and Masters of the Air’s Callum Turner.

But after all that Bond-esque bluffing and counterbluffing from Amazon MGM Studios, Robert Pattinson was unveiled as the new 007 in mid-November. Everybody (mostly) breathed a sigh of relief—and turned their attention to the rest of the upcoming reboot. So, what do we know so far?

The second crucial element of Le Nouveau Bond is that French-Canadian director Denis Villeneuve will direct. He, too, has pedigree, not least his epic three-part Dune series, the first two films of which have already grossed more than $1 billion worldwide, with Part Three due out in December. “If there’s one thing we can be sure of with Denis, it’s that he will do something interesting with this,” a source within the film industry told me. “He is one of those directors who can turn even a bad script into a good film.”

Speaking of which, the other major name already signed up to the reimagining is Peaky Blinders creator Steven Knight. He will write the story.

Knight seems a natural fit for a darker, more character-driven Bond. His breakout was the screenplay for Dirty Pretty Things, which earned him an Oscar nomination. He went on to write David Cronenberg’s brutal Eastern Promises and the one-man thriller Locke.

But it was Peaky Blinders that made his name: a stylized, violent, brooding saga of a damaged man at the rotten heart of a criminal empire, which became a global hit for the BBC and Netflix. Knight, in other words, has previous when it comes to writing moody, morally murky men.

No need to say much—we know what we’re dealing with here. A man who makes good choices. A star.

At forty, Pattinson is a little older than Craig was when he got the job in 2005, aged thirty-seven. But the career behind him is hardly that of a conventional leading man. After the teen-idol years of Twilight, he spent a decade deliberately steering away from the obvious: Cronenberg’s Cosmopolis, the Safdie brothers’ Good Time, Robert Eggers’s The Lighthouse, and Claire Denis’s High Life, before returning to the blockbuster fold with Christopher Nolan’s Tenet, and The Batman.

He’s made stellar choices throughout his career, turning a heartthrob start into a credible run of good, risk-taking roles.

The first, most critical drop will, of course, be the villain. Because, as they say in Hollywood, a movie is only ever as good as its villain. Without a dastardly supervillain for Bond to shoot his PPK at, Bond is just a red flag in a black suit. No names have yet reared their heads. But that didn’t stop us from suggesting a few candidates.

Speaking of which, Amazon has shown no indication of a plan to depart from the Bond-girl tradition. Though it’s (thankfully) likely to be handled sensitively for 2026: expect a complex and fully realized love interest who will still be in a gorgeous dress by the second act.

Margaret Qualley has emerged as the bookies’ favorite, having already taken part in a Bond-tribute dance at the 2025 Oscars. Sydney Sweeney has said she’s up for joining the franchise, though she told Variety she’d have more fun playing Bond himself.

Anya Taylor-Joy, Florence Pugh, and Zendaya have also been linked, with the obvious advantage of having worked with Villeneuve on Dune.

They were most recently played, respectively, by Ralph Fiennes, Ben Whishaw, and Naomie Harris. It’s not known whether any of them will return to the roles, though history suggests it’s likely.

Craig’s run offers a rough template, for what it’s worth (which is not much). He was unveiled in October 2005, started shooting Casino Royale the following January, and had it in cinemas by November 2006. Pattinson’s unveiling comes just a few weeks later in the calendar, so a similar timetable would see cameras rolling early next year and a potential release date around the end of 2027. Though that feels like a tight turnaround for such a gargantuan project. Amazon will want to get this right, so given how long it strung out Pattinson’s casting, 2028 seems a more likely ballpark.

Here’s a boring-but-pertinent fact: three of Daniel Craig’s five James Bond films debuted in October: Quantum of Solace, Skyfall, and Spectre. The remaining two, Casino Royale and No Time to Die, were released in November and September, respectively.

But this is, of course, guesswork—Amazon has offered no indication that it will follow the Craig timeline, or anything else about the movie’s schedule.

This is perhaps the most interesting part of the whole process. It began in February 2025, when the Broccoli dynasty (led by long-serving Bond masterminds Barbara Broccoli and Michael G. Wilson) announced they were handing over creative control to Amazon MGM Studios (which was formed when Amazon bought Bond’s parent studio in 2022).

Within hours of the announcement, Amazon’s owner, Jeff Bezos—the world’s richest man—took to X to ask the world: “Who’d you pick as the next Bond?”

This followed rumors of a rift between the two parties, with the Wall Street Journal reporting that Barbara suggested to friends that she didn't like the idea of Amazon execs taking a data-driven approach to the series, as well as the use of the word “content” in creative meetings.

Just as Disney+ has produced many Star Wars spin-offs, Amazon MGM is expected to do the same with Bond—a process that began last year with the first TV series, 007: Road to a Million, a game show hosted by Succession’s Brian Cox.

Mike Hopkins, head of Prime Video and Amazon MGM Studios, said in February 2025: “We are honored to continue this treasured heritage, and look forward to ushering in the next phase of the legendary 007 for audiences around the world.”

#6654. Red Jeans Are Easier to Style Than You Think—Here’s How Fashion Girls Are Wearing Them

These color pairings make bold red jeans surprisingly wearable.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Red jeans have a reputation for being risky, the kind of pants that live at the back of the closet until a bold mood strikes. But here’s the secret stylists have known for years: adding a pop of red to your wardrobe is one of the easiest ways to make it more versatile. Think of it as a neutral with attitude. It pairs effortlessly with white tops, gray and brown knits, and classic leather sneakers or loafers, adding instant energy to outfits that might otherwise fall flat.

So how do you wear them? Let the jeans be the star, and keep the rest of your look grounded in colors that play well with red, such as black, white, purple, gray, camel, and even butter yellow.

Whether you’re dressing up for dinner, keeping it casual for the weekend, or looking for a way to refresh your everyday wardrobe, red jeans can do it all. Below we’ve rounded up some of our favorite ways fashion girls are styling crimson denim.

Give your look a romantic flair by pairing red jeans with a flowy, ruffled blouse. Adding statement accessories, such as on-trend sunglasses and a bold necklace, gives the outfit a modern feel.

Chloé Cropped ruffle top in cotton voile & lace

Gucci Ophidia Small leather-trimmed crossbody bag

Abercrombie & Fitch High Rise Wide Leg Jean

Diana Milkanova Studio Oversized Coat in Yellow

Red from head to toe sounds more daring than it looks. Yes, the look is bold, but teamed with a neutral bag and shoes, it makes for a surprisingly wearable statement.

We love every pairing in this post, but slide three gives red jeans a cozy-chic personality with a relaxed cardigan, suede loafers, and a sleek camel coat.

Zara MID-RISE BAGGY OVERSIZE SEAM DETAIL TRF JEANS

Red and brown is the pairing we can’t get enough of right now. These jeans, styled with elevated basics like a cashmere pullover, cozy socks, classic penny loafers, and chic sunglasses, prove that casual dressing can still feel elevated.

#6652. How a Quip About a “Low-Budget Vagina” Could Give ‘The Real Housewives of London’ a Bigger Place in Bravo’s Lineup

The viral reunion clip may have just made ‘RHOLDN’ Bravo’s next must-watch.

For some context: The Jamaican-born reality star hurled this insult at fellow cast member Amanda Cronin, noting that—while Karen “came to England at 25 years old” and now owns “five properties”—Amanda sleeps with princes, lords, and mayors and still only has “one mews house.” Please give it a watch, if you haven’t already.

While RHOLDN is the third UK installment of The Real Housewives franchise, it’s the first original series for Hayu, with season 1 only becoming available to stream on Bravo in the U.S. in July 2026 (nearly a year after its August 2025 debut on Hayu). But after the mega-virality of that season 2 reunion clip, RHOLDN will almost certainly get a season 3...and it definitely has Bravo’s attention.

It’s unclear whether a potential season 3 could air on Bravo in real time alongside Hayu, but what is clear is that RHOLDN is a force to be reckoned with, especially as other Real Housewives franchises are starting to lose their relevancy. (Please see: Real Housewives of Orange County.) And the “low-budget vagina” read isn’t the first time that RHOLDN has delivered a meme-able moment, either. Remember Amanda’s “Back to Paddington” clapback from season 1?

So...what does this mean about the future of RHOLDN (which, as a reminder, hasn’t yet been renewed for season 3)? According to The Sun, production is reportedly looking to shake up the cast to bring even more stars who can deliver drama like Karen.

“Karen has really set the bar for what they’re looking for,” a source claimed. “She’s got the personality, the lifestyle, and that larger-than-life presence that makes brilliant television. They’re keen to find another one or two women who can go toe-to-toe with someone like Karen, rather than fade into the background.”

What’s more: “Nothing is set in stone, but there’s definitely a feeling that the next series needs to be bigger, bolder, and a little less polite.”

Could this fan interest also mean that Bravo’s Ladies of London reboot gets taken off pause? No word on that yet, but a girl can dream!

#6649. Who Is Miley Cyrus’s Husband, Maxx Morando?

The couple officially said “I do” in Palm Springs

Miley Cyrus and Maxx Morando are officially married! Nearly one year after their engagement was confirmed, the couple tied the knot on Saturday, November 21, in Palm Springs, California.

The singer, who said “I do” in Saint Laurent, shared black-and-white photos from the wedding on Instagram, simply captioning the post, “M+M 🤍 11.21.26.” In photos, the couple could be seen exchanging vows in a poolside ceremony featuring a stunning display of sweeping white flowers and candles.

In December 2025, Cyrus confirmed her engagement to musician Maxx Morando—a relationship she has largely kept out of the spotlight following her highly publicized marriage to Liam Hemsworth. The Bass Persuades singer was first linked to Morando in November 2021, when the two sparked romance rumors with a joint appearance at the Gucci Love Parade fashion show. (Cyrus later shared a photo of herself with Morando from the event on Instagram.)

Rumors escalated the following month, when Cyrus danced and held hands with Morando in Miami during her New Year’s Eve Party special for NBC. By April 2022, they confirmed their romance during an outing in West Hollywood, where they went all out with the PDA.

Fast forward to the 2024 Grammys and Morando supported Cyrus at the show—where she won her first Grammy for “Flowers”—and he gave her a passionate kiss.

Since then, the two have kept their relationship low-key aside from a few red carpet appearances together. But while Morando has mostly kept his personal life out of the public spotlight, he, like Cyrus, has had a long career in music.

Ahead, everything we know about Cyrus’s new husband, Maxx Morando.

While best known as the drummer for Liily (and for the Regrettes, from 2015 to 2018), Morando also plays guitar. Additionally, he has also collaborated with Cyrus on several of her albums, including her most recent release, Bass Persuades. For the project, he co-produced the album’s title track alongside Cyrus, as well as several others, including “Aftermath” and “REDLIGHTS.” The musician also has songwriting credits, co-writing “Innocent Ways” with Cyrus and being credited on “Aftermath.”

Morando also produced two songs, “Handstand” and “Violet Chemistry,” from the singer’s Endless Summer Vacation LP.

He also collaborated with Cyrus on her 2025 album, Something Beautiful, contributing as both a producer and songwriter on several tracks. Morando also played drums on the record and made a cameo in the “End of the World” music video.

The drummer, born in 1998, is six years Cyrus’s junior. (His birthday also happens to fall exactly a week ahead of the singer’s, on November 16.)

Cyrus opened up about their slight age gap in a 2024 interview with Harper’s Bazaar: “He grew up with a laptop. I had a desktop computer that I shared with my brothers and sisters. … Honestly, he’s raised our dog off Reddit. I’m like, Are you sure we’re supposed to be doing this? And he’s like, On Reddit it says blah, blah, blah.”

Along with his musical endeavors, the drummer has dabbled in fashion design too, with Cyrus as his muse.

Cyrus revealed in September 2021 that Morando contributed cartoon designs to a custom vegan leather set by designer Shane Kastl, which Cyrus wore for a performance that month.

“This look is a one-of-a-kind collaboration between two of my favorite emerging artists, Maxx Morando and Shane Kastl,” Cyrus said of the special look in an interview. “Their collaboration is the perfect example of sustainability becoming the next fashion phase, and it proves that anything can be reinvented, which resonates with me deeply.”

In an interview for Bazaar’s December 2024 cover story, Cyrus opened up about her relationship with Morando, saying, “He’s very similar to me. We just don’t take life too seriously.”

She also shared how working with Morando on musical projects helped them bond: “I worked with my dad forever. That’s how me and my ex-husband met each other. I’ve always worked with the people that I love,” Cyrus says. “And Maxx just inspires me so much.”

The couple’s engagement was confirmed in December 2025, after Cyrus sparked rumors by wearing a diamond sparkler on her left-hand ring finger at the premiere of Avatar: Fire and Ash in LA.

Morando keeps an extremely private, low profile in both his personal and professional life. While the musician does have an Instagram, he rarely posts, aside from sharing new music that he works on with both his band and Cyrus.

In March 2023, a source told People that Morando is a “cool guy” who is drama-free, a trait that Cyrus finds extremely attractive.

“He has no interest in being a huge celebrity; he’s very low-key. Miley loves these qualities,” the source said. “Miley can be herself. They are supportive of each other’s careers. Miley is enjoying life and things are great.”

Following a year-long engagement, the Morando and Cyrus officially wed in an intimate ceremony held in Palm Springs, California. The couple shared photos from the wedding on Instagram, which included black-and-white portraits of the couple with their dog, as well as a sweet photo of them kissing after saying “I do.”

For the ceremony, the bride wore a long-sleeve, backless Saint Laurent wedding gown, while Morando kept things classic in a black suit.

The poolside ceremony took place at Frank Sinatra’s former home, which was transformed with hundreds of white roses for the occasion. In photos obtained by DeuxMoi, the couple could be seen exchanging vows by the pool, in front of an intimate crowd of guests dressed in all-black attire.

#6645. The Breakfast at Tiffany’s NYC Townhouse Sells for $11.5 Million

The historic residence traded at a $3.5 million discount.

Holly Golightly might have had questionable taste in men, but she had a keen eye for real estate. Memorably portrayed by Audrey Hepburn in Breakfast at Tiffany’s, Golightly lived in a handsome Upper East Side brownstone that had been subdivided into apartments. Though the 1961 classic film shot its interior scenes on a Los Angeles soundstage, the exterior of the New York City townhouse served as a backdrop throughout the movie.

In May 2025, the five-story residence hit the market for $15 million, following a total overhaul that included a slew of luxe upgrades, such as a new roof terrace and an elevator. Although StreetEasy listed the property as “in contract” last December, its official sale date is November 11, 2026. In the end, the two-family house traded hands for $11.5 million, roughly 23 percent off the original price, per Realtor.com.

Set on a tree-lined block between Lexington and Third Avenues, the East 73rd Street home is distinguished by its Tiffany Blue front door and matching window casings. Built in 1866 by architect John Sexton, the Italianate-style landmark encompasses nearly 4,500 square feet and has four bedrooms in total, three in the main residence—which spans the top four floors—and one in the garden-level apartment, which has its own separate entrance.

The seller was retired beverage executive Joseph Harkins, who acquired the property in 2015 for $7.4 million and spearheaded a complete transformation that modernized the interiors while still preserving the house’s 19th-century charm. The project even included excavating the basement level, which now has nearly eight-foot ceilings, a powder room, a second laundry room, and a wine cellar.

Outside, the 470-square-foot rear garden is fully enclosed by Walpole lattice fencing and sports stonework paving. It will no doubt be the scene of plenty of “Breakfasts” for the home’s newest steward.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

However, it seems there will be at least one major change in the next nine months.
