#6623. Everything Connor Storrie and Hudson Williams Have Said About Their Friendship

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

HBO Max and Crave’s Heated Rivalry has quickly become one of the most-discussed series of the year, catapulting its stars Connor Storrie and Hudson Williams into the celebrity stratosphere. The two actors portray hockey players Shane Hollander (Williams) and Ilya Rozanov (Storrie), who have a secret love affair spanning almost a decade. The series is known for its spicy intimate scenes as well as its beautiful depiction of romantic yearning.

Storrie and Williams have been open in interviews about their experience creating the show, along with their offscreen relationship as they navigate sudden fame together.

“Hudson is my best friend, and I literally can’t fathom doing this without him,” Storrie told W magazine in December 2025. “We were both in similar places in life before this. We quit our jobs within a day of each other. We booked this and flew out the same day. Now it’s turning into two people who are being seen internationally for the first time. This has been the highlight of my life, and meeting Hudson makes that 10 times sweeter.”

Here, everything the two actors have shared about their friendship.

Fans of the show tend to “ship” Williams and Storrie, hoping they end up dating in real life. Storrie addressed this head-on in an interview with Vulture, saying things are strictly platonic.

When asked about the intimate scenes they shot in the show, Storrie said, “For me, it doesn’t feel spicy at all—like, that’s me and my best friend. This is the first time in my life I’m having this many eyes on me. They’ve seen me naked, they’ve seen me kiss, they’ve seen me be in love with a man on-screen—it’s only normal for people to try to transfer that over to mine and Hudson’s real life.” He explained that he intends to keep his private life private, adding, “I feel honored to be able to bring someone to life that so many people feel seen, understood, and represented by, and I think that transcends whoever I’m sleeping with in my real life.”

Williams expressed a similar sentiment to Deadline, saying, “I want queer people telling queer stories, but also, there’s the element of Connor and I—we’re best friends, and we love expressing that physically. You see people who infer or assume, and you kind of have to let that go. But then again, I never wanna stop expressing the love I have for Connor physically, and I’m never really going to, and I think multiple things can be true at once.”

In November 2026, Williams’s interview on The Assembly aired. One of the audience members there asked Williams if he ever had romantic feelings for Storrie. “No,” he replied. “Quickly, me and Connor realized we were, like, platonic soulmates, just completely in love with each other. I think that’s what made our friendship so stable and beautiful, is that we realized we could be completely ourselves, be completely guard down. So, I think the thing that made our relationship as rich as it was is, like, me and that man could cuddle for 10 hours, and it just mean cuddling, just mean we just love each other dearly, but don’t wanna beep each other’s bop.”

He then added playfully, “Who doesn’t want Connor Storrie in their shower? You know, someone needs to get my back. I wish Connor was just with me every day. I’m like, ‘Hey, I have a loofah but I can’t reach [my back].’ If I had Connor with me every day, he could just come in there.”

Sophie Nélisse, who plays Heated Rivalry’s Rose Landry, interviewed Williams for Wonderland magazine in January 2026 and asked him how he and Storrie balance promoting the show with forging their own identities. Williams admitted that they purposely go to different events, in part to keep their friendship more private and their brands separate.

“It’s been really nice, because Connor and I are aligned,” Williams began. “We want to be different artists. We don’t want to be the Olsen twins. We want to be Connor and Hudson, with different filmographies and different deals. As much as we love each other, our friendship doesn’t need to be public. Jacob [Tierney] has that quote: ‘Shane and Ilya are for the public. Connor and Hudson are for themselves.’ And that’s how we feel. People want to see us front row together all the time, but we actively avoid that, because it starts to feel like branding. If you’re always seen together, you become attached at the hip. I FaceTime him whenever I’m free. But if we’re offered the same fashion shows, we’re like, ‘Which one are you going to? Okay, I’ll go to a different one.’ We’re stubborn, selfish artists who want to be our own people. We’re like, ‘I love you. I don’t want to do everything with you.’”

Both Williams and Storrie got into great shape for the show. In a profile with GQ in December 2025, Williams talked about meeting his co-star and being intimidated by his build.

“Connor pushed himself up onto the island in the kitchen,” Williams said. “I remember seeing his arms. They were the size of my legs. I was like, Holy fuck.”

“It’s not true,” Storrie responded. “Because you have crazy legs.”

The show’s creator, Jacob Tierney, also commented on their affection for one another in the piece, saying, “They’re living through a thing right now that is bananas—absolutely fucking bananas—and they’re handling it with grace, with humor, with generosity. They adore each other, which is like, thank fucking God.”

While discussing his own workout routine with Men’s Health in January, Williams had a playful quip about Storrie’s physique: “I’m okay talking about my butt, but poor Connor Storrie—who has the fattest butt I’ve ever seen on a man—I’m sure just wants to stop talking about his beautiful cheeks.”

In a December interview with Entertainment Weekly, the friends discussed a viral video from the Heated Rivalry wrap party where they are rolling around on the floor together.

“Very plainly spoken, me and Hudson are freaks,” Storrie explained. “We’re clowns. We just love to riff and have fun and go crazy, and those were two situations where we were caught off guard doing our thing. The wrap party, that was all improvised. I think we were just putting on a show.”

Williams added, “Also, there might be something else, I think, with—we’re both very physical actors, and movement is a huge part of it.”

During his Vulture interview, Storrie spoke about moving into an apartment next to Williams’s while filming in Toronto.

“We would literally just go to set, go home, cook, work out together, and geek out over how excited we were to finally be working on this level,” he said. “If we were any less close, we probably would’ve gotten annoyed with each other really quick.”

The American actor told Vulture that they leaned into their chemistry while promoting Heated Rivalry’s first season. “If Hudson says something jokingly, like, ‘Spit in my mouth,’ I think that gives everyone else the go-ahead to get in on that too,” he said. “People are ruthless talking about our holes and our butts.”

During his discussion with Interview, Storrie added that spending time with Williams has helped ground them both during their press tour. “Hudson’s been in town, so we’ll have some quality time and come back down to ground zero,” he said. “It’s funny, because all the press and stuff is getting easier and easier and easier. You would think you would get burnt out and exhausted. Even though it’s only been three weeks of doing press for this, I’m clicking into an interesting sort of confidence and appreciation for all of this.”

Williams, meanwhile, credited Storrie for helping him keep the press tour fun during an interview with The Hollywood Reporter. “I have one of my best friends for life right beside me through it all, which is already a luxury that a lot of actors don’t get,” he said. “I’m sure I would’ve probably had a smaller appetite for the amount of stuff we’re doing if I didn’t have him through this. I’m an omnivert, leaning more to an extrovert, but I still get—I have ADHD, I’m unmedicated, currently, for the last while, so my social battery can just tap [out] pretty quick. At the end of the day, I’m pretty forgiving with myself if I’m talking with a reporter in a polite, more boring way. Then when I’ve had coffee, and I’ve had a nap, I let myself be chaotic. I let myself off the leash. I try to say, whatever mood I’m in, just be Canadian, don’t be mean. Whatever they get, they get.”

Timid magazine asked Williams how Storrie’s acting has helped him be a better performer. Williams responded, “He’s so incredibly present and so incredibly truthful. As an actor, it’s so much easier to think of what you should be doing or what you think would be an entertaining version of this or the most dramatic version or make sure you’re hitting these lines properly or doing the scene properly or the director wants this and the script says this. But just to actually listen to the person and let what they’re saying affect you—Connor is so good at that. There’s so many moments where I am saying something and the script says he does this, and he does something so unexpectedly emotional or so unexpectedly vulnerable that even if I’m very robotic and going through a scene, he can crack me and change my trajectory in the scene. He’s a huge resource in affecting me and that makes my job way easier because I don’t have to. He makes me be present. He’s so good at that. That’s his superpower.”

Storrie, meanwhile, credited Williams for helping him understand Ilya so quickly in his Interview story. “I feel like I got the character pretty instantly,” he began. “I believed in his circumstances without almost any effort, and I just trusted Hudson so much. It’s so easy to act when you know where you’re coming from and you believe the person opposite you. I mean, I’m so gooey. I’m such a romantic. So any of the really heightened emotional moments—their love or confession or an outbreak or a fight—those are the moments that I really enjoy as an actor.”

Williams listed out all the nicknames he and Storrie have for each other during his Timid magazine interview. “He calls me Huddy, Papi, Bubba, Baby,” he said. “I call him Pookie. I used to call him Pookie a lot. Papi. Oh, Con Con. I call him that both in text form and verbally. But yeah, a lot of nicknames.”

GQ revealed that when the series wrapped, they got matching tattoos that read “SEX SELLS.”

The Canadian star revealed that he stayed at Storrie’s Los Angeles apartment when he came to the U.S. to do some press. “We were like, ‘Oh, my God, if people saw us just getting ready in the morning, giving each other big spooning hugs, just to say we’re proud of each other,’ people would be going crazy,” he told Variety. “If only one fan had access, God forbid. But yeah, we just love each other very dearly.”

The outlet asked him what creating Heated Rivalry taught him. Williams responded, “I think I had my own biases, maybe that would be too strong of a word, but my own discomfort if I expressed myself fully, physically and affectionately, both with women and men. It would feel inappropriate. I realized if you really do love someone—platonically or not—I’m such a tactile, sensory person. There are so many taboos. It’s much harder on men. I had to let that go, and I got to learn how freeing that is. That also went hand-in-hand with being myself, truly as an artist. Connor was also a great person to have at my side. I think we both opened each other up to just be freer, more fully expressive.”

During Williams’s January 2026 appearance on Andy Cohen Live, he revealed that Storrie is an “awful texter,” but they largely keep in touch with frequent video calls. “We’ll Facetime each other or call each other randomly, very consistently,” he told the host. “That has sort of been my stability, especially through this. Just having my best friend who is in the same position I am.”

In a November 2025 interview with The Permanent Rain Press, Williams said he would pitch Storrie as “a beautiful little, sensitive little radical, artist boy. Who should be treated with the utmost care and hugs and friendliness because he can’t handle anything else. And that’s perfect. And that’s the way he should be treated.”

He added, “And anyone who doesn’t treat him that way should die.”

In May 2026, Williams and Storrie made their Met Gala debut. They took a bathroom selfie together, which Williams later posted to his Threads account.

The pair’s appearance at the Met Gala excited fans, with one user taking a solo shot of Williams and posting it alongside lyrics from Rihanna’s breakup song, “Woo.” (Williams has a longtime girlfriend, Katelyn Larson.) The post has since been taken down, but Williams responded and wrote, “Rpfing [real person fan fiction] gotta stop y’all.”

During his November interview on Vogue’s The Run-Through podcast, Storrie revealed that he and Williams were living together while shooting season 2 of Heated Rivalry.

“Right now we’re filming [the next season of Heated Rivalry], so I literally live with him, which is amazing,” he said. “We were at this hotel, which was a brilliant hotel, but I just….I needed a washing machine. I needed a kitchen.”

Storrie also discussed how their red carpet looks sometimes seem coordinated without any advance planning, pointing to their similar outfits at the Vanity Fair Oscar Party.

“We’ve had a few moments where it just matched. But that’s just very telling of Hudson and I in general. We are very cut from the same cloth. We have a soul tie thing. So there’s a lot of synchronicity. But no, we don’t coordinate before at all,” he said.

He also expressed admiration for Williams’s personal style, particularly his approach to streetwear and tailoring.

“Hudson is really cool. You know what I mean? Hudson has this sort of, there’s something kind of like street style or edgy about what he does,” Storrie explained. “And like when he dresses up, when he does like fine suits and tailoring, it’s a little bit different. And I always kind of envy that, like him being able to wear really cool stuff, because it doesn’t really work the same on me. But I like my thing too.”

#6619. Louisa Jacobson’s Stylist on 10 Things That Inspire Her Red Carpet Looks

She’s been on a winning style streak—here’s a glimpse at the conversations behind the ensembles

In fashion, anything from niche archival research to a favorite film to an outfit you saw on your morning commute can make for inspiration. In our new series 10 Things, we ask industry professionals and tastemakers to divulge the (you guessed it) 10 things that had the most impact on their aesthetic choices.

Method dressing has become the road most traveled for Hollywood A-listers and their stylists. We’ve seen Zendaya and Law Roach turn out goddess-like dresses in homage to the actress’s role of Athena in The Odyssey, while the cast of the Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping has been channeling their outlandish onscreen personas into equally over-the-top red carpet looks. But even though period pieces present a prime opportunity for imitation, The Gilded Age star Louisa Jacobson, who plays Marian Brook in the Julian Fellowes series, says she’d rather dress like herself.

“One thing that we are always aware of is not being the character she plays on TV, because she is more than that,” Jacobson’s stylist Edward Bowleg III told Harper’s Bazaar over Zoom. “She is a fully fleshed-out human being. She is Louisa and she is not Marian. That is an important distinction.”

Bowleg started working with the actress in 2022 after a mutual friend introduced them right around the time that The Gilded Age was set to premiere. The editorial stylist, who has worked for countless publications and brands like Hermès, Valentino, and Marc Jacobs, recalls his first official project with Jacobson: the 2022 Vanity Fair Oscars party, to which she wore an ivory off-the-shoulder Khaite gown with a Jeffrey Levinson clutch.

Much has changed in the four years since that evening. “When she cut her hair short, that was the moment where a lot shifted,” said Bowleg. “It was a new her.” With the fresh haircut came a slew of delightfully unexpected red carpet moments, spanning a handful of tailored suits (and a cummerbund!) from Haider Ackermann’s Tom Ford, Conner Ives, and Calvin Klein Collection to a Dilara Findikoglu minidress with an armored bodice made from safety pins, metal accents, and a tattered chiffon train.

Jacobson’s fashion evolution paralleled her own self-actualization. “Exploring sexual identity and being more free with that led to having more fun with experimental fashion and being unapologetic about what she likes and who she is,” said Bowleg. And it shows: She’s been on a winning style streak recently.

Coming off Jacobson’s recent Gilded Age press tour, which saw looks from Haider Ackermann’s Tom Ford, Ashlyn, Commission, and Mel Usine, Bowleg sat down with Bazaar to decode 10 influences that shaped his fashion choices, from Saint Laurent’s le smoking to an Azzedine Alaïa collection that lives rent-free in his head.

“We love tailoring, we love a suit. I could have her in a suit every day of the week. She went to her first Tom Ford show in March. When she told me she was invited, I was like ‘Oh my god, that is so cool,’ because I am a big fan. The 1996 Gucci show is burned into my brain. That is when my interest in fashion really began, so for me in a weird way it was almost like a full circle moment.

“I think with creative director Haider Ackermann’s tailoring in particular, the cut is so exquisite. When we have fittings with them, the only thing we are adjusting is the length of the trousers. There is also a confidence that she has when she puts them on. It’s very commanding. Then you mix that in with all of the elements—there is always a really sexy shoe that goes along with it.”

“An image that could have been on the moodboard would be Helmut Newton’s photos of Saint Laurent’s Le Smoking with the women on the street in Paris. There is one image with a naked woman wearing just heels, standing with another woman in Le Smoking. That is very much the kind of vibe we are going for: mixing something sexy and feminine with tailoring, which is inherently masculine.”

“She went to the Met Gala this year with Dilara [Findikoglu]. When I had the conversation with Dilara and her team, the idea of armor came up. She had been to the Met ahead of our conversation, and she sent me all of these pictures that she had taken in the Medieval section where they have the helmets and the chain mail and the body armor. Those were just things that she loved and that became a big inspiration for the look.”

“For Louisa’s first Met Gala in 2022 (the red carpet theme was ‘Gilded Glamour, White Tie’) there was a John Singer Sargent painting of a woman in a black dress. The image helped direct our choice of look”—a futuristic gown with a gold trompe l'œil skeleton molded in leather and hand-painted in gold leaf from the Schiaparelli Spring/Summer 2022 Haute Couture collection by Daniel Roseberry.

“I love Azzedine Alaïa. Specifically the 1986 show—the one with all of the bandage dressing with the cut-outs on the sides and the infamous denim and leather zip that goes all across the body. I believe Grace Jones walked. Even with the McQueen that she just wore, in a way it’s that sort of vibe—the bomber jacket but with the little skirt. It’s that kind of energy: A strong, powerful woman.”

“There has been quite a bit of leather. The McQueen that she just wore, the Khaite full leather suit that she wore to the Vanity Fair Oscars party. She put it on, and it was like ‘This is fab.’ We were wondering if it was right for Vanity Fair and whether she would be the only one in a suit. I told Louisa that Zendaya had worn a suit the year before and I also was kind of like, ‘Fuck it, you look hot, this is great.’ Then there were two more leather Louis Vuitton looks that she wore. One was to the Vanity Fair Oscars party in 2024. And then before that, she wore a Vuitton look to the SAG Awards and the whole bodice was sculpted in leather, as well.”

“Hedi Slimane is a big inspiration of mine. Again, it’s a lot of tailoring, it’s leather, it’s a great silhouette. It’s the aesthetic overall, but in particular, it was the fall/winter 2006 collection that he did. There were things that were skinny and then there were things that were a bit more loose in terms of the trousers. There was a softness to the hardness of it all. It was still very edgy and a bit rock ‘n’ roll, but there was a soft glamour to it. That is something that I like with Louisa, even when it comes to the jewelry.”

“I am really curious to see what Pieter Mulier will do with Versace and Atelier Versace is in January. [I should also say that] I am obsessed with Donatella Versace. When I started looking at all things [high] fashion, Donatella was another designer that I loved. That glamour and excess and sexiness—she is unapologetically who she is.”

“With music and ‘90s hip-hop, you look at the bling, the diamonds, and things like that. I love a great jewelry moment, so accessorizing Louisa’s looks with the right jewelry is always important part to me. I love diamonds. When she recently went to a Tiffany & Co. event [in Ashlyn], we initially had our brooch up on the lapel where you would traditionally see it. Then she was like, ‘It doesn’t feel right.’ So I moved it down to the peplum of the jacket. It was a big, big yellow diamond, which I was obsessed with. I just loved how much it sparkled and shined.”

“Helmut Lang is also a big inspiration. There is a Spring 1998 men's collection and a Spring 1999 collection that I think about a lot in terms of silhouette and things that still resonate today. It all comes from having researched some of those collections in the past, so they tend to be recurring elements in the work.”

#6618. Miley Cyrus Got Married at Frank Sinatra’s Midcentury Palm Springs House

The home still has its piano-shaped pool—and you can rent it for the weekend.

Miley Cyrus and Maxx Morando just got married in Frank Sinatra’s Palm Springs backyard. The singer and the Liily drummer, who met on a blind date in 2021, exchanged vows on Saturday, November 21, beneath an arch of white flowers at the edge of Sinatra’s former swimming pool. As far as wedding backdrops go, this one makes sense for two musicians: The pool is shaped like a grand piano.

Sinatra’s former home takes its name from the two palms that still stand on the property. When he hired architect E. Stewart Williams in 1947, he wanted a Georgian-style house, but Williams talked him into a single-story design with wide stretches of glass shaded by deep eaves. It was his first residential commission in Palm Springs, and he arranged the house around the pool. From the living room, sliding glass doors open onto a terrace bordered by the house, a pergola, and a cabana.

For a house built for Sinatra, a pool shaped like a grand piano sounds almost too on the nose. But Williams said the resemblance was incidental: He intended the curve to draw the eye toward the mountains. Sinatra lived at Twin Palms for about six years, including part of his marriage to Ava Gardner. In 2011, Palm Springs granted historic status to the house and parts of its grounds.

Yes, though it’s a private residence, not a museum. The estate is available as a vacation rental, with four bedrooms and room for up to eight guests. It also offers private guided tours for groups of 20 or more.

#6617. A Rare Alexander McQueen Gem Is Coming to Auction

The iconic Alexander McQueen “Plato’s Atlantis” dress is headlining Christie’s December Luxury Week.

Few remnants of fashion history manage to stir up emotions and draw headlines quite like pieces from Alexander McQueen’s revered runways. Rare, technical, and sensorial, these are certainly not your average, everyday pieces of clothing. McQueen runway pieces are often flexed at the world’s top fashion exhibitions at museums around the world. That’s just one of the reasons why the multicolor silk minidress from Alexander McQueen spring/summer 2010 “Plato’s Atlantis” is the star of Christie’s December Luxury Week, where the runway gem will be hitting the auction block with a $150,000 to $300,000 estimate. The sale runs online from November 25 to December 10, 2026.

“Based on what we know about McQueen’s distribution at the time and the types of garments that went into production after a runway show, we estimate there were probably only fewer than two dozen examples of this exact dress produced,” Max Brownawell, head of handbags at Christie’s, tells ELLE. “Then on top of that, the importance of ‘Plato’s Atlantis,’ as McQueen’s final fully realized collection, really can’t be understated. He himself said that his previous collection was his final as a young man. And so this was his most mature, fully fledged collection that he’d ever produced.”

The dress was the fourth look in the runway collection, styled with the iconic Armadillo boots. Designed with a futuristic sculpted drape, covered in prints from the natural world (think: a collage of abstracted imagery pulled from butterfly wings, snakeskin, and insects), the silhouette is a favorite of fashion fans and archivists. Brownawell shares that the piece still has the tag attached and has likely never been worn. The ensemble may very well exceed the estimate: for reference, a “Plato’s Atlantis” look complete with those aforementioned boots sold for $1.5 million including fees at a Fair Warning auction in New York on Sept. 17, 2026, setting an auction record for McQueen.

“He was going for something that was entirely new,” says Brownawell. “This dress is a really prime example of that, the way that the print has been designed to lay perfectly within the architecture of the garment itself is really a spectacular sort of couture level of creation. On top of that, you have this unbelievably architectural draping, which, you know, McQueen has, it’s been said that he really actually draped a lot of these pieces directly onto the forms themselves because it was so complex.”

Aside from the dress itself, Alexander McQueen’s spring/summer 2010 show was ahead of its time in every way. It was the first show to be livestreamed in real time on the internet, and it was the first fashion show to debut a major pop single during a show; Lady Gaga’s “Bad Romance” premiered during the show’s finale. “McQueen’s legacy has been just growing stronger and stronger every year,” adds Brownawell.

The dress is hitting the auction block at a time when Christie’s is launching its inaugural fashion auction. “We really believe that the market is at a point right now where people do appreciate the important designs,” he says. “There’s a lot of clothing out there, but there’s also a lot of very important pieces that define what we wear and where fashion is going and where it’s been.”

#6616. The Inconvenient Truth About the Electricity AI Requires

AI companies are facing political backlash, but they’re staring down an even bigger problem as they look to take over the world.

He-Man liked to exclaim: “I have the power!” AI should be so lucky.

When it comes to the wielders of artificial intelligence, and the data centers needed to fuel it, language and numbers become inescapably extreme: trillions in investment, gigawatts of electricity, the extinction of the human race. It’s almost cartoonish, until you get down to some brass tacks: The power is not there to fuel these dreams of the would-be Masters of the Universe.

“The U.S. grid is not designed for large data-center loads,” says Anubhav Arora, senior director of global infrastructure at Fitch and a coauthor of a recent paper the ratings agency published on data centers. “Securing reliable, sufficient power is the single greatest bottleneck in data-center development,” the paper’s authors write. (Esquire and Fitch share the same parent company, Hearst.)

In the U.S.—which is by far the world leader in data centers—electricity demand from the facilities could reach up to 649 terawatt-hours (TWh) a year by 2030, according to a 2026 Berkeley Lab analysis, or roughly 12 percent of all U.S. electricity consumption. Powering New York City for an entire year requires one tenth that energy.

Meeting global demand for compute could require nearly $7 trillion in capital investment by 2030, including $5.2 trillion for AI-related data centers, according to McKinsey estimates. Estimates from Goldman Sachs Research say that Europe will need to invest more than $3.5 trillion on its grid in the next ten years to cope with increased demand. (China looks to be fine, but it is slow to the data-center boom.)

And the problem is not just a lack of juice. “The actual grid infrastructure . . . is also basically limited in terms of transmission capacity, and the interconnection process is a little bit outdated,” says Arora. That grid—the network of transmission lines, transformers, and power plants that create and distribute electricity to hundreds of millions of users—is constrained, and the interconnection backlog is massive. Put another way: Not only is the shop running low on milk, but the line to get inside is stretching around the block. “You could build a data center in one and a half to two years, but to get interconnection, it takes five to ten years,” Arora says.

According to Cleanview, a company that tracks U.S. power infrastructure, there are currently 1,454 operating data centers, as well as up to 578 under construction and 2,663 proposed. Companies like Alphabet, Meta, and Anthropic are scrambling. With that scramble, there is money to be made. PJM, the largest power-grid operator in the United States, has been trying to devise a new way to accommodate the data-center boom. Its proposed “Bring Your Own New Capacity” (or BYONC) framework would have allowed new data-center projects to buy energy in the form of a battery and add it to the grid. But in October, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission rejected that portion of PJM’s proposal, sending the grid operator back to the drawing board.

Important here is what kind of data centers we’re talking about. Of the more than 3,200 data centers proposed globally, 800 of them are hyperscalers. These are the biggies. The campuses. Sprawling centers with square footage in the millions and power demands upwards of 100 MW (which could power 100,000 homes). Collectively, some estimates put the global power usage at 200 TWh annually. (That’s juice for 11 million homes.)

How can companies even think about the grid beginning to handle that demand? Increasingly, they aren’t. Many hyperscalers are looking to “bring your own power [BYOP],” says Jonathon Smith, associate director of Sustainable Finance Research at Fitch. In other words, build their own power plants. The problem here is twofold: First, very few people on the planet know how to build something like this. You could buy the biggest army the world has ever seen; try moving it without engineers. Second, it poses very real and very dangerous environmental threats.

Recently, the journal Nature Sustainability published a study revealing that the annual carbon dioxide emissions of the largest data centers in the U.S. are equal to that of a small country, like Paraguay or Denmark. And that study looked at data centers from 2024 and 2025. Data centers have exploded since then, with as much as a 190 percent increase from 2025 to 2026.

When companies like Anthropic say, “Don’t worry, we’re not even touching the grid,” it brings up a cartoon situation more akin to The Simpsons than a Saturday-morning serial. Picture it: a giant data center, clean, cool, not an emission in sight, just humming machines and maybe a security guard. But there’s a pipe going out the back, and if you follow it as it snakes around the corner, it connects to a behemoth power plant, burning enough natural gas to power a city, with plumes of smoke assaulting the sky above. Animated, but closer to the reality of BYOP.

“From an environmental-impact perspective, most of these developers are deploying natural-gas-based assets for power, which release GHG [greenhouse gas] emissions and air pollutants,” says Smith. The cleaner alternatives are renewables (unlikely), nuclear (terrifying), and batteries (no environmental picnic, either).

Among the environmental concerns—which inevitably bleed into the social and economic ones—are these centers’ use of water, which they need in great volume to keep those humming stacks of servers cool. While many experts point to these issues as overblown, especially as data centers look to closed-loop systems (there is an initial dump of water that gets cooled and reused), the reason you won’t stop hearing about water use is because 1) it’s concerning, and 2) water is inherently political and always has been. (Traditionally, we’ve needed the stuff to feed our animals, grow our crops, and, you know, live.) Go back to the early chapters of the Bible and what’s causing strife? Water. Go back even further and some of the first wars ever recorded—4,500 years ago in ancient Sumeria—are over the stuff. And data centers have become a fast-moving political football.

A recent Gallup poll indicates that 71 percent of Americans do not want an AI data center near where they live. Those are some very big numbers. It’s important to clarify that the data centers big tech wants to build near dense populations are for cloud computing, and they make up roughly 70 percent of all data centers. While centers for AI computing and AI training are catching up, these can be built farther away, for the simple reason that we’re happy to wait a few minutes for AI to do our taxes but not for the cloud to recombobulate an email or Instagram post.

That’s still a huge number of data centers that need to be near you and me, so what will we do about it? The politicians are listening. New York State has issued a moratorium. There’s legislation limiting the centers on the table in nine other states. Gina Hinojosa, who just pummeled Greg Abbott in the Governor's race in Texas, campaigned on promises of a moratorium for permitting and construction on new data centers. Norway, the Netherlands, and Denmark are leading the charge on moratoriums and other restrictions in Europe as governments catch up with popular opinion.

Moreover, back in the U.S.A., Donald Trump has touted a “deal” with big tech on data centers, the Ratepayer Protection Pledge, supposedly protecting grids from the biggies. But never has Tip O’Neill’s maxim that “All politics is local” been more salient. More than 500 localities are forming their own moratoriums, zoning freezes, and permit pauses. Even after the midterms, how and if to move forward with data centers is a debate for states, counties, cities, townships, and any other geographic entity that makes laws, collects taxes, and breathes collective air.

As the Democrats take over Congress and data centers become every pol’s favorite new cudgel, as the European Union and member states enact regulatory frameworks (often tied to renewable energy and climate), as elected officials turn away from AI’s once sunnily vague promises, something is becoming clear: It’s humans who have the power.

#6614. What Is a Thanksgiving Cactus? Your Guide to the Other Holiday Cactus

The Thanksgiving cactus makes a good case for skipping the centerpiece. Here’s how to keep it going long after dinner.

By the time the garden has mostly called it a year, the Thanksgiving cactus is just getting started. Its red, lavender, or soft salmon flowers appear at the tips of flat green stems, often right around the holiday, and its stems arch over the rim of the pot.

You’ve probably seen one without knowing exactly what it was. Thanksgiving and Christmas cacti often share a shelf and a label (“holiday cactus”), and both can flower in December. The giveaway is in the stems: the Thanksgiving cactus has little pointed teeth along the edges. And it’s far from the kind of cactus you’d put on your sunniest windowsill. It comes from the forests of Brazil, where it grows on trees in filtered light. Here’s what to know about the Thanksgiving cactus.

The Thanksgiving cactus (Schlumbergera truncata) is a flowering cactus native to the forests of Brazil, where it grows on trees instead of in soil. The flowers, which come in shades including white, red, lavender, and salmon-orange, typically open from mid-November through late December.

Look at one of the flat stem segments. On a Thanksgiving cactus, the edges have pointed teeth; on a true Christmas cactus (Schlumbergera × buckleyi), they’re rounded, like the edge of a scalloped plate.

Set it near an east-facing window or somewhere else with bright, indirect light. When the top inch of potting mix feels dry, water it thoroughly and let the excess drain. If you’ve tucked the nursery pot into a ceramic planter, lift it out to water so it doesn’t end up sitting in a puddle.

While the plant is budding and flowering, keep the mix lightly moist. You can water less often once the flowers fade. There’s no need to repot it as soon as you bring it home; if it’s outgrown its container, wait until spring and go up one pot size.

In early fall, give the plant about 14 hours of uninterrupted darkness each night for roughly six weeks. A spare room that gets daylight but where nobody switches on a lamp at night works well. Cool nights—around 55 to 60 degrees Fahrenheit—also encourage buds to form. Once you see buds, keep the plant away from drafts and don’t let the potting mix dry out completely.

#6610. Carolina Herrera Takes Miami with a New Art-Filled Boutique

The store brings Herrera’s signature color and feminine spirit to the Design District, with works by women artists throughout.

Above: Inside Carolina Herrera’s Miami flagship, a sculptural chandelier by artist Liz Oliver hangs suspended above a soft pink couch.

In September, Carolina Herrera held an extravaganza of a runway show during New York Fashion Week, celebrating the brand’s 45th anniversary. This past week, the festivities went further south, to Miami. Last Thursday night, creative director Wes Gordon and members of the city’s fashion and arts community gathered beneath a cherry-red awning to toast the brand’s newest boutique—and sixth official location in the U.S.

Opened on August 15, the 3,000-square-foot shop sits in the middle of the Miami Design District, with a dramatic arched entrance welcoming guests into the limestone-finished structure. Designed in collaboration with interior designers Brandi Howe and Jessica Hyland of Hyland Studio, the store is bursting with color and feminine details—soft pink travertine covers the floor while furnishings feature red and pink accents. In the center of the main salon, a custom pink sofa with graceful curvature offers an elegant seat; in another area, the same red awning from the boutique’s entrance reappears suspended over a pair of regal armchairs.

“The Carolina Herrera woman is bold, elegant, and joyful. She embraces color, expresses herself with confidence, and never shies away from making an entrance,” says Gordon.

But the new store is not only an ode to the Carolina Herrera brand but also to other female creatives. On view throughout the space are artworks commissioned specifically for the boutique as a part of the Carolina Herrera for Women in the Arts initiative. In the main salon, Los Angeles-based artist Liz Oliver designed a rose-colored textile installation that doubles as a sculptural chandelier. Elsewhere, ceramic planters made by the women-owned Choplet Ceramics are on display. Throughout the boutique, a recurring custom floral motif by Argentine artist Constanza Vallese appears on vases and tables.

In raising a glass to the boutique, guests at last Thursday’s event honored both the Carolina Herrera woman and her new city. “Her energy is magnetic—much like the spirit of Miami,” says Gordon. “It feels entirely natural that we should open our next home here.”

#6609. We Tried Dolce Vita’s Fall Shoes to Find the Pairs Worth Wearing

From morning commutes to evening plans, we wanted to see if these shoes keep up.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There’s a particular art to getting dressed this time of year. One minute, you’re heading into the office after navigating the morning’s unpredictable weather; the next, you’re rushing to make a dinner reservation or squeezing in a holiday party before the weekend. And while a great outfit can usually rise to the occasion, finding shoes that can do the same is another story.

That’s where Dolce Vita comes in. The brand’s fall collection makes a case for shoes you can wear just about anywhere. To bring that vision to life, the company opened The Dolce Vita Flat, a month-long Williamsburg residency inspired by the way personal style extends beyond our wardrobes.

Naturally, we decided to put that philosophy to the test. ELLE editors slipped into some of the collection’s standout styles to see which pairs could earn a permanent spot in our rotations. From polished office staples to shoes we’re excited to style for the holidays, here’s what made the cut.

“I’ve been searching for a suede boot for fall and the Jazmins were exactly what I wanted—they’re great to pair with dresses or knee-length skirts for a minimalist ’90s vibe. They have a chic pointed toe and a low kitten heel that makes them easy to walk in. The knee-high shaft is soft and not tight; plus it helps that there’s a little elastic panel for a bit of stretch. I hover between a size 7.5 and 8, so I opted up, which turned out to fit perfectly. It offers just enough wiggle room for socks without being too loose.”

“These shoes really exceeded my expectations. The leather is soft, lightweight, and supple straight out of the box. They don’t require any breaking in, and they even have room for my orthotic inserts. One note, though: definitely size down.”

“Every girl has a little black dress. The Kinzy boots are my little black boots—although, maybe not as little as they appear. Paired with sweater dresses, pencil skirts, and tucked under jeans, these knee-highs make me feel like a million bucks each time I wear them.”

“Not only do I own these boots in every color, but most of my shoe collection is actually from Dolce Vita. The brand makes some of the most comfortable heels I’ve ever worn, and these chic, surprisingly wearable Kinzy stiletto boots are no exception. They’ve become my fall go-to, pairing perfectly with all my favorite cold-weather looks, especially midi skirts and dresses. I can even walk across the city in them without a single complaint. I also adore the silver buckle detail, which adds a stylish touch and sets them apart from the other black boots in my lineup.”

“If there’s one thing I can’t resist, it’s a shoe that looks more expensive than it actually is. The Emani heels checked that box immediately. I’m obsessed with the neutral-colored suede, but it’s really the shape that sold me. It feels modern, a little fashion-forward, and just different enough from your standard heel.”

“The ballet flat is easily the most versatile shoe a girl can own, but the slightest variations in shape and style can determine whether it becomes a wardrobe staple or simply a shoe you wear until it wears out. The Andey quickly earned its place in my everyday rotation, so much so that I had to own it in both black and cream! The perfect low stacked heel, slightly squared toe, and sleek, high-cut vamp make it the ideal silhouette for any occasion. Whether peeking out from a pair of jeans or paired with a midi skirt, this style lends an effortlessly polished feel to any look. Size down half a size for a fit that feels like a glove.”

“If you’re anything like me and you’ve been searching for the perfect everyday black boot, look no further. With a low enough stiletto heel, this style gives me the look I’m going for without sacrificing comfort. My one note? If you’re planning to use thicker-than-mesh socks or would consider your foot anything other than narrow, go up by a half size.”

#6607. 13 Menswear Sales Worth Shopping on Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Here’s how to save big on Lacoste polos, Filson jackets, and designer menswear.

If you’re looking hard enough for sales on menswear, you can almost always find a discount on something decent, a pair of shoes, a shirt maybe, but you have to scrounge. Every other week of the year, that’s just how it is. But this week, the week of Black Friday, the opposite is true. Every store and their mother is having a sale and suddenly, it’s not easy to tell which markdowns are gonna go fast and which ones will still be there when you log on again in December. Luckily, you have me. Year-round, it’s my job to keep tabs on deals so I know which ones to pounce on this Black Friday.

On Amazon, there’s my favorite Lacoste polo, which is actually on an outrageous sale going up to 40 percent off if you’re the right size. Filson jackets are 25 percent off. Lululemon isn’t just for the gals, it has a ton of great stuff for men and its Black Friday sale is absolutely stacked. And REI is doing its classic “We hate Black Friday but are doing a sale anyway” thing, and as always we are here for it.

Let’s start with the personal favorites; after that we’ll just list deals alphabetically by brand. First, Amazon. Yeah, yeah, everything is Amazon now. But when Bezos is offering big discounts on Lacoste polos and Levi’s, we listen.

Nothing is ever going to beat the cool of a beat-to-hell Filson jacket. If you’ve been holding off on buying one, I can’t recommend it enough. Plus, Black Friday is the perfect time to get one for 25 percent off.

I tell regular—meaning normal guys, not fashion-y dorks like me—that Huckberry is really the answer to 90 percent of what they want. All of a man’s casual wardrobe can be bought here; the only thing it doesn’t stock well is suits and tailoring. I’m seeing up to 40 percent off on seasonal shirts, and up to 30 percent off jackets, sweaters, and pants; that’s as good as it gets, folks.

J. Press Made-in-England Khaki Drywax Cotton Overshirt

Any time Lululemon does a discount is noteworthy. The brand is averse to any seasonal sales, so Black Friday is your best bet here. Through Cyber Monday, you can get big discounts (I’m talking 40-plus percent off) on the usual best sellers—just note that for the most part, only certain colors are discounted.

Lululemon Smooth Spacer Classic-Fit Pullover Hoodie

If you’re not a regular Huckberry guy but a man who considers himself “into fashion” or at least “stylish,” you ought to be hitting the Mr Porter sale. The sale is absolutely massive—over 12,000 items are up to 50 percent off—so I’d go in with a plan. Have one item, or even one brand, you want to check out; do that and that only. My hunt is for a really good jacket—sport coat, blazer, suit separate, or whatever you want to call it. That’s what I’m showing, but you need to do some of your own hunting on this one.

Del Toro, which has for a long time just been a really good rich guy made-in-Italy shoe brand, has started putting out some really lovely elevated casual clothing—all vintage Italian-inspired stuff—and a majority of it is 20 percent off. And J. Crew has its usual sale going on, but an extra 40 percent off sale styles thrown in with the code FRIDAY on top of that. Todd Snyder is giving 30 percent off a ton of items with the code BLACKFRIDAY. And of course, you can’t go wrong at perennial favorite outlets REI and Nordstrom—both are featuring affordable menswear at a discount for Black Friday.

#6605. A Landmark All-Lalanne Sale Is Heading to Sotheby’s

Expected to fetch as much as $25 million, “Les Lalanne: Beyond Imagination” is the first-ever various-owner auction dedicated to Claude and François-Xavier Lalanne.

The market for Claude and François-Xavier Lalanne’s irreverent creations has been on a high lately. In April, a single wooly sheep sculpture by François-Xavier fetched $2.3 million. Over the summer, a quartet of his frog fountains smashed their $3.5 million estimate to achieve nearly $6.3 million. And the record for most expensive collectible design work belongs to a set of flower-adorned bronze mirrors crafted by Claude: They netted $33.5 million earlier this year, eclipsing François-Xavier’s $31.4 million hippo bar.

Now, ELLE Decor can exclusively share that Sotheby’s will host the first-ever auction dedicated to Les Lalanne, featuring pieces sourced from various collections. On December 8, more than 30 works by the late French artist duo will hit the block in “Les Lalanne: Beyond Imagination.” It is expected to fetch in the region of $25 million.

“Les Lalanne have become a global phenomenon, changing the way generations of collectors think about design,” says Jodi Pollack, chairman of 20th century design and major collections for the Americas. “A sheep can be a seat, a crocodile can be a console, and both can hold their own alongside the most important works in a collection.”

The collection spans everything from a gold-patinated bronze gorilla (estimated at $8–12 million) to a white marble owl (estimated at $4–6 million), and a flock of five sheep, including two lambs ($300,000–500,000 each). Highlights also include lesser-known motifs, such as a playful pair of bronze monkeys with deep-green patination that makes them almost look like jade ($800,000–1.2 million each), and two matching crocodile consoles ($700,000–1 million) that complement the famous chairs Claude created after obtaining a deceased crocodile from the Paris Zoo.

Those hoping to get an in-person glimpse at the creatures can see the menagerie starting December 2 at the Breuer building, Sotheby’s new Manhattan headquarters. To accompany the show, the auction house enlisted Milan-based artist Gaia Alari to devise custom illustrations for the catalogue, which reads like a fairy tale.

“The story begins in the catalogue and comes to life at the Breuer, where visitors will step into an enchanted winter garden and meet the cast,” says Pollack. “It’s our way of celebrating these works and the spirit in which they were conceived. We want visitors to look at familiar things as Claude and François-Xavier did and to see the extraordinary in the ordinary.”

“Les Lalanne: Beyond Imagination” will be on view at Sotheby’s New York from December 2 to 7. The live auction will take place on December 8, 2026, at Sotheby’s New York at 11 a.m..

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6604. Under-$10 Holiday Makeup MVPs That Bring the Sparkle

These festive SHEGLAM picks serve major holiday glam.

No matter your makeup aesthetic, the holiday season is the time to embrace shimmery, lustrous looks. For minimalists, a luminous complexion and a glassy lip offer a subtle way to glow, while maximalists can fully commit to the sparkle with glitter-flecked metallic eyeshadow and liner.

The best part? You don’t have to drain your gift budget to make your dream makeup look a reality. From glitter mascara to body mists that gleam, SHEGLAM has everything you need to create all things merry and bright without maxing out your credit card. Here are our top four holiday makeup must-haves from the brand, plus more under-$10 finds to round out your glow.

Available in festive shades like gold, rose gold, and silver, this multi-use, cooling jelly stick delivers a high-shine, glassy finish on the eyes or as a highlighter on the cheekbones and nose. Sweep it onto skin and diffuse with your fingers for a touch of sparkle or layer it for a mirror-like chrome effect. For a more pared-back look, try the Lunar Frosty Eyeshadow Stick, which features a soft shimmer and matte base.

This silver mascara imparts light-catching sparkles onto lashes while lengthening and separating them for a doe-eyed effect. Wear it on its own for a wintry touch or layer it over the Lashlighter Mega Boost Mascara in Black for high-impact shine. If you’re feeling extra bold, swipe some onto your brows for a holiday look that feels uniquely you. True shimmer enthusiasts can also spray the Go Go Glow Highlighting Body Mist onto skin and hair for an allover, prism-like shine.

Ice out your lips with this frosty gloss that comes in shades like gold, rose gold, and bright bubblegum pink for a high-impact glassy finish. The formula is transfer-proof, so you can nibble and sip your way through the holiday party without constantly reapplying. For even more frost, sweep the Enigma Sparkly Eyeshadow in Such a Prize onto your lids and glide the Boldline Longwear Multi-Function Gel Liner in Pearl on your waterline for a complete look that sparkles like freshly fallen snow.

If your holiday-party ‘fit is doing all the sparking, luminous skin might be all you need. This smooth and buttery highlight stick glides onto skin to deliver a dewy shine that feels totally weightless. Apply it on the high points of the face, such as the cheekbones, Cupid’s bow, bridge of the nose, and browbone. Diffuse with your fingers for an ethereal glow.

#6600. Everything but the Plane: Here’s How One Brand Is Bringing Glamour Back to Travel

PS’s private terminals are the epitome of real luxury.

Imagine a trip to the airport with no lines. No lines to check in, to drop your bag, to be told your first-class ticket is the wrong first-class ticket for the first-class lounge. Now add to that no interpersonal friction. There are no snappy desk workers, no turbo TSA employees, and no graveyard-shift customs agents that give you grief.

Airlines can solve only so many issues. These are terminal problems. PS, a Los Angeles–based company operating its own luxury private terminals, is solving them. The goal is to get you as close to the charter experience as possible, all while still flying commercial. If you ask around or sit in the common areas and eavesdrop long enough, you’ll likely hear something along the lines of “I usually fly private, but when I don’t, this is what I use.”

The company has locations in Atlanta, Dallas Fort Worth, Miami, and Los Angeles, and the next order of business is expansion to the airports its clients use most—business hubs, mostly. But it started at LAX in 2017, and that’s the perfect airport for something like this. The horseshoe design and the traffic it creates, the small terminals, and the paparazzi culture do not cater to the A-list and executive frequent fliers that use it as a home airport. PS offers a way around that with on-tarmac connection from your flight to its terminal and vice versa. A chauffeur pulls you straight off the jet bridge and into a luxury fleet vehicle; that partnership is currently with BMW. If you don’t have any checked luggage, that 7 Series can (for an additional price) drive you straight to your destination within 35 miles of the airport. So if you are sitting in row 1 of first class, that’s about 50 steps between you and the i7 that takes you to your hotel. Or it can take you back to the PS terminal, which is not a lounge but acts as an über-private lounge experience.

It ain’t cheap: $1,300 per head one-way for the Salon (a shared bar area), $4,950 for a private suite (essentially an airport hotel room with butler and spa service; this price is all-inclusive), and $1,650 for that PS Direct drive-you-straight-home service. There are discounts and perks if you opt for yearly membership, but this is all on top of your ticket, folks.

And it can lean comically luxurious, with Champagne and caviar offered every time you enter—like before my 8:00 a.m. departure, to which I’m not opposed but which caught me a little off guard. There’s the jet-set marketing—tagline: The “back door” to the airport—and meant-for-the-timeline branded cocktail napkins: “You’ve arrived.”

But once you accept that PS could not afford to excuse itself from the flex-driven airport exclusivity economy, you can really admire its hospitality-inspired finishes. Its raison d’être is to get you through the airport with as little friction as possible, but it stands out in its commitment to hospitality within those confines.

For example, interior design and artwork are curated. The centerpiece artworks of each location are done in collaboration with local artists, made to fit the location they are in. The crown jewel thus far is in Miami, where PS took over the old Pan Am headquarters and flight-training academy. The mid-century building is a Miami landmark, with geometric thermal walls acting as the facades and this pinkish sandy and limestone-heavy concrete throughout. Argentinian-born ceramicist Nina Surel matched this with tile-work water features and a ceramic mural at the center of the property. Design like this, small and mostly unnoticed in the grand scheme of things, is what turns your mind back to a golden age of travel. It’s not credit card points and moneymaking schemes—though, actually, you can buy any of the non-centerpiece artworks—just beauty for the sake of beauty.

On top of that, the food and beverage program is considered and regional. The blue-corn cornbread with honey and a ricotta spread that chef Kelsey Kirwin (chefs’ names are on the menus, an indication of the atmosphere) is making at the DFW location overtook my mother’s as my all-time favorite. I would consider the signature PS martini—with (guess what) caviar-stuffed olives—worthy of a place on any hot big-city cocktail menu. And all the coffees, snacks, and water options are head and shoulders above those of both the general-population terminals and the litany of airport lounges within them.

As far as service, yes, an agent handles your party’s check-in and keeps you abreast of delays so you don’t have to open an app—but that’s to be expected. It’s more in the interim. Servers don’t hover, they listen, and they’re backed by an extensive behind-the-scenes operation: Mention that you prefer coffee, not bubbles, before a morning flight? A note is made on your profile for the next time you visit. Only ever ask for sparkling water when you fly out of LAX? They’ll know that in Atlanta. The entire operation seems to run on Guidara’s Unreasonable Hospitality, the same way all modern restaurants do. If you’re leaving Miami and mention to a business partner that you forgot to try a Cubano (on menu) and a cafecito (off menu), both will likely be delivered to your table, compliments of the house.

But every time I start to get wowed by the service details, I remember the price point and the level of clientele. That should be expected.

Standard terminals are air-side, post-security, where everything (even a fancy and expensive airline lounge) is working within those confines. Since PS operates land-side, with security the last stage of the terminal experience, it has the flexibility to be more hospitable to guests. For example, if you book a private suite, you can fly in, use it for a business meeting, and fly out. Or your parents can accompany the family to departure, hang out in the suite, and squeeze every last second with the grandkids out of your visit. It is an airport experience actually designed around the ways we interact and what matters to us when we travel. That, more than the Champagne and caviar, is the definition of luxury.

#6599. The 5 Must-Have Fall Boots I'm Wearing Straight Through Winter

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Growing up in Southern California, I was a flip-flop girl through and through. But since moving to New York, I've come to appreciate the importance of a cold-weather shoe collection. In fact, boots now outnumber sandals in my closet.

Whether I'm wearing a plaid mini skirt and sheer tights or straight-leg denim and a knitted sweater, boots complete the look. They're what ballet flats are to the warmer seasons, providing a tried-and-true footwear staple to rely on for the rest of fall and all winter long.

While I certainly own a few striking pairs of boots (like bubblegum-pink iterations and cow-print block heels), curating a timeless collection that effortlessly pairs with your wardrobe is essential. This way, you can build upon the curated collection season after season, rather than splurging on trend-specific footwear every fall. Because these styles can be worn for years to come, investing in quality pieces that will stand the test of time is key.

There's still a month of fall left, and these five styles will carry you through winter too. Ahead, discover the ultimate fall boots starter pack, including classic knee-highs, Western boots, and rich burgundy hues.

If you only own one pair of boots, make it these. The little black dress of footwear can be dressed up or down, transcending occasion, age, and style. The sleek and timeless shoe has even elevated my simple shift dresses with ease. Suede iterations offer a cozy appeal, slick leather delivers sophistication, stilettos are sultry, and block heels are comfortable.

Stuart Weitzman Stuart Leather Power Boots

On casual fall days, the '70s-style pull-on boot will be your best friend. The straight, stovepipe shaft skips zippers in favor of pull tabs. Typically hitting at the mid-calf or knee, the heritage style boasts a low, stacked heel and sturdy sole. Wear the boots with a mini skirt, tights, and a chunky button-up cardigan for a cozy, laid-back look.

Steve Madden Riggs Banana Leather Square Toe Boots

Like black, brown, and navy, burgundy acts as a neutral come fall. The deep, saturated color feels grounded, while still bold enough to make an impact with a simple outfit. If you're looking for a style that is fashion-forward but still timeless, this is it. Rich velvets, warm suede, and textured bouclé effortlessly pair with the deep red shade.

When I don't want to tuck a tall boot beneath my pants (think flare jeans and billowy trousers), I reach for ankle boots instead. The short shaft looks clean underneath denim, silk, cotton, and wool, creating a smooth finish that never looks frumpy. A neutral beige shade ensures your short boots pair with everything from a colorful knit shrug to a cream sweater dress.

Tory Burch Asymmetric Cap Toe Ankle Boots

You don't need to be on a ranch to wear Western boots. The rodeo-inspired footwear is officially a part of the cool-girl uniform because it's both utilitarian and dressy. While it's certainly more adventurous than the other options on this list, it's just as practical, especially when you choose an earthy hue. Style your cowgirl boots with a Fair Isle pullover and billowy skirt for an of-the-moment take.

When she’s not reporting on her latest fashion obsession, she can be found listening to Taylor Swift, hunting for the best vanilla latte, playing with her cats, or smiling her way through a dance workout. #6598. Let Your Thanksgiving Tablescape Inspire Your Outfit

From cranberry velvet to bird motifs, holiday dressing starts at the table.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It finally feels like we’re in the thick of fall. It’s just beginning to get cold enough to wear our favorite coats, all the leaves have turned graceful shades of red, yellow, and orange, and somehow, nothing feels as comforting as a hot cider cupped in the perfect pair of leather gloves. We’ve almost arrived at Thanksgiving, which officially marks the start of the holiday season.

When it comes to fashion, Thanksgiving is actually one of the best holidays for holiday dressing. There is ample inspiration to rework fall classics and experiment with new trends that have just recently been presented on the runways, like berry tones, antique Victorian-inspired style, or even statement coats. The weather (not too cold just yet, but cold enough to layer) also makes it a prime time to play with accessories, whether it be medallion belts or grandmacore-inspired tapestry bags.

So, this year, why not pull your Thanksgiving outfit inspiration directly from your tablescape? Aside from celebrating with friends and family, a decorative tablescape is one of the most fun parts of this holiday. Make a Pinterest board for both, and you’ll match: There’s nothing chicer.

Read on for some ideas for Thanksgiving tablescape-inspired outfits.

Look to Thanksgiving’s ultimate favorite hue and layer up juicy shades of it. Rich, stunning tones of saturated cranberry pop (and you’ll probably spot them on your plate, too). It gives you a dramatic look with low-key effort. Opt for mixed textures. Think: leather, velvet, silks, and satins all done in deep cranberry.

FRAME FRAME x Amelia Gray The Corduroy Gray Pants

It’s completely okay to be a little bit literal. In fact, it can actually make your outfit more fun. Consider traditional tablecloth textiles—gingham, plaid, and tartan—and make them a part of your look. You can use them as an accent (a scarf or the lining of a coat) or make them the main focus.

By now, we all know the Thanksgiving mascot as the turkey. And many editorial tablescapes incorporate some kind of turkey or autumnal bird. Bird motifs are both a trend and extremely seasonally accurate (and inspiring) for a Thanksgiving outfit, too. Go conceptual: bird prints, peacock feather patterns, winged jewelry, and feather trims are all visually interesting.

FARM RIO birds tree long sleeve maxi dress

Chan Luu Crinkle Techno Taffeta Feather Dress

The easiest Thanksgiving outfit (and tablescape) trick is to lean into the natural codes of Mother Nature. Accessorize your outfit and table with dark fall florals, leaf motifs, gourds and pumpkins, or atmospheric forest and woodland fall elements. There are so many different directions to go in. But when in doubt, think of berry tones and fun details like walnut-shaped clutches or mushroom motifs.

Lela Rose Floral Organza Embroidered Crepe Dress

Choose a swanning maxi dress in shades of rich pumpkin orange or saffron yellow; even better if it has a fall-ready-feeling textile trend like fringe or tassels (which can also be found on tablescapes). Match it to your décor and the fall foliage: Layer up rich shades of red, rust, terracotta, chestnut brown, olive green, caramel, and sienna.

#6597. Is It Rude to Ask Guests to Bring Food for Thanksgiving Dinner?

At its best, Thanksgiving is an opportunity to gather around and share our appreciation for one another through contribution and collaboration. But let’s be honest, any holiday can be a minefield, and there’s always something new to navigate—even beyond a dry turkey. One particular concern is preparing a meal that will suit everyone and one large enough to make sure no one leaves hungry. And so, a host may be inclined to ask guests to contribute to the buffet.

However, there is a right way and a wrong way to broach the issue, and luckily, it’s a topic that the best hosts and advice columnists have adeptly tackled time and time again. This begins, perhaps, with the most vaunted of all: Emily Post. Well, her descendants, anyway. The Post Institute suggests that it is acceptable, given the “potluck” nature of the day, to ask guests to contribute, but hosts must “Have a flexible menu plan...be prepared to be ‘coordination central.’”

The Post Institute’s advice also highlights something important: The onus is very much on the guest to offer to contribute. Here’s how they should do it: “Offer to contribute to the meal—but don't dictate the menu. Your best bet is to make your offer open-ended and follow your host's direction.” Plus, it never hurts to take responsibility for your own dietary needs, though good hosts will always do their best to accommodate.

Martha Stewart and Ina Garten share similar protocol. In an interview with the Los Angeles Times, Stewart suggests that during potlucks more broadly, “The host can provide the main course,” and the guests can bring the sides. Using her “duck breast with fig sauce menu” (a multi-course dinner party plan starring the namesake dish) as an example, she says, “One person can bring the braised red cabbage and someone else can prepare the potato pancake or the hazelnut brittle for the ice cream.”

Garten is enthusiastic about guest contributions, telling Bon Appétit, “I actually love to ask people for help.” She also offers specific marching orders: “I might put somebody in charge of wine if they like wine.” Using a guest’s talents and interests to get to the best outcome is a smart idea, and can even go beyond sweet potatoes and pumpkin pie to napkins or place cards.

Myka Meier, the Beaumont etiquette founder, has an entire treatise on the topic, “A guest who accepts a dinner invitation should not discover the day before that they are responsible for the main course.” Instead, she suggests offering a choice, telling guests: “We are having a potluck supper on Saturday at six. I will make the main dish. Would you enjoy bringing a vegetable side, bread, or dessert?” She also reminds hosts to tell their guests how many are joining.

The most important aspect, as with all facets of Thanksgiving, is to have fun and celebrate with loved ones—be lenient, forgiving, and courteous. With a bit of preparation, everything will turn out well.

Dorothy Scarborough (she/her) is the assistant to the Editor in Chief of Town & Country and Elle Decor.

#6596. Is Your Phone Messing With Your Skin?

Even just an hour of exposure can start to impact your skin’s health

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Our phones are essentially glued to our hands, from keeping up with TikTok and Instagram to answering emails and jumping on Zoom calls. People widely recognize screen time and its effects on sleep and eye strain, but one area is skin health—the blue light emitted from our devices is the sneakiest culprit behind skin damage.

“Blue light has high energy and therefore is able to penetrate deep into the skin,” says dermatologist Macrene Alexiades, an associate clinical professor at Yale University’s School of Medicine and the director and founder of the Dermatology & Laser Surgery Center of New York. “I caution my patients when using their devices because screens may inadvertently emit UV light, which is known to cause skin cancer.”

If blue light radiation is news to you, don’t fret. We spoke with experts about the harmful light, how it affects your skin, and ways you can protect it from damage.

“Blue light, also known as Artificial Visible Light (AVL) and High Energy Visible Light (HEVL), is the spectrum of light that is very close in wavelength to ultraviolet light,” says Loretta Ciraldo, a Miami-based dermatologist and the co-founder of her eponymous skincare line. “The sun emits large amounts of blue light, which is why the sky is blue. Digital devices, including our cell phones and computer screens, also emit blue light but in smaller quantities, so we don't see it as intensely as the sky’s blue hue.”

Like the UV rays emitted from the sun, blue light has a short wavelength. But when high amounts of blue light are emitted, like from our computers and phones, it can damage deep layers of the skin and cause premature aging and skin cancer.

“Long-term exposure to concentrated sources of blue light energy can cause skin damage, including color changes, inflammation, and weakening of the skin's surface,” adds Ellen Marmur, a New York dermatologist.

The National Center for Biotechnology Information reports that blue light emitted from electronic devices—even for as little as one hour—can cause damage to the lipids and proteins that hold moisture, keep impurities out, and repair the skin.

“The topic of blue light protection is very new,” Ciraldo explains. “For many people, blue light causes more redness, swelling, and hyperpigmentation than UVA. It is for this reason that we dermatologists are now focusing on protecting skin from damage of blue light.”

First and foremost, sunscreen is a must, even if you’re sitting on the couch all day. “Any sunscreen that contains zinc oxide or titanium dioxide can help in blocking blue light,” explains Marmur, who reaches for EltaMD’s sunscreen to protect her skin. “Arginine can help to prevent photoaging and treat hyperpigmentation. Iron oxide is another ingredient that can help with reducing sensitivity to blue light.” Also look for antioxidants, including green tea, vitamin C, ferulic acid, blue algae, and pomegranate, to protect and heal your skin from blue light damage, says Alexiades.

This is the gold standard of vitamin C serums. With a whopping 41 percent (!) of vitamin C and ferulic acid, this powerful elixir results in firmer, more radiant skin.

While algae promotes collagen and peptides tighten the eye contours, the chromabright (a vitamin E analog) in this gel protects against the entire light spectrum.

This daily supplement from Hum Nutrition shields skin from the effects of sun exposure and provides vitamin C to boost collagen production and protect cells from oxidative stress.

This SPF-infused primer also has a technology that reduces the effects of blue light and environmental stressors on skin, so you’re protected all around.

Beyond slathering on a sunscreen that protects against HEV light, consider turning on the “Night Shift Mode” on your smartphone and computer, which shifts your screen to warmer tones to reduce emitted blue light. Many smartphone settings let you set night mode to turn on automatically each day. Warmer hues can be gentler on your skin and may help you fall asleep compared to harsh blue light.

You can also opt for a screen protector that blocks HEV light. Ciraldo also suggests dietary supplements that contain the botanical Polypodium leucotomos, which has been shown to protect skin from blue light, environmental stressors, and free radicals. Try her go-to from Heliocare.

Dermatologists have turned to LED blue light and light therapy devices to treat acne and kill bacteria—but that doesn’t mean you can treat breakouts by watching TV for hours. “Blue light kills acne through a process called photodynamic therapy,” says Alexiades. “The bacteria produce porphyrins, which make them sensitive to light destruction, and they absorb blue light preferentially, hence the effectiveness of HEV light in treating acne.” Microdoses and controlled wavelengths of blue light for 20 minutes can benefit acne-prone skin, Marmur adds. That said, don’t use blue light therapy as an excuse for more screen time.
