#6558. Is This Department Stores’ Big Comeback Christmas?

Gone are the days of the branded luxury Christmas window.

In an era where department stores need to work harder than ever to get a holiday shopper into their store — rather than them shopping online, directly with a brand, or at the department store up the road — many retailers are branching out beyond the monobrand Christmas window strategy that’s dominated recent holiday seasons.

Last week, stores from Bergdorf Goodman to Bloomingdale’s made a splash with their holiday window unveilings. Saks followed suit on Monday, November 23. The weather was unseasonably warm, with attendees clad in light jackets, rather than the usual fur and puffers for this time of year. Bergdorf made up for the tepid temps with fake snow. Bloomingdale’s had Sienna Spiro sing a rendition of “Have Yourself a Merry Little Christmas”.

By going the multibrand route, stores can put themselves at the center of their holiday storytelling, and appeal to a wider range of consumers by displaying merchandise across a host of different price points. Utilizing existing merchandise is also a lower lift than the logistical and financial burden of staging a full brand takeover. “I’m anticipating highly creative windows with a variety of merchandise. The general sentiment as of the last several months is that the customer shops high and low, where two years ago it was all about the big labels,” says luxury consultant Robert Burke. “So I think you’re going to see a variety of merchandise displayed in creative ways.”

It’s panning out. Nordstrom is partnering with David Bowie’s estate to celebrate what would be the star’s 80th birthday — and is Nordstrom’s 125th anniversary. Included in Bowie80 are collaborations with designers including Paul Smith, Marc Jacobs and Undercover’s Jun Takahashi. Bergdorf Goodman’s windows are a vignette inspired by a New York winter, celebrating 125 years with an array of capsules from brands including Michael Kors, Brunello Cucinelli and Valentino. Saks’s windows offer up a tour of the festive period in the city. Over in London, Liberty is leaning into storytelling around holiday rituals and routines, and Selfridges is, like its peers, celebrating wintertime in the city (New York City, that is).

Bergdorf invited a host of designers to participate in its holiday window unveiling.

Not all of the players are going wide, though. Bloomingdale’s Friday window unveiling was in partnership with Ralph Lauren. Partnering with Ralph was a no-brainer, says Kevin Harter, VP of integrated marketing. “Ralph Lauren is synonymous with the traditions, nostalgia and timelessness that makes the holidays so special,” she says. The evening included a festive Ralph cocktail in the store — with teddy bear sweaters aplenty — and David Lauren was onstage to celebrate the unveiling alongside Bloomingdale’s CEO Olivier Bron. Inside, though, there are an array of brand activations — over 50 — from Barbour’s Tea & Sympathy pop-up to Ugg’s takeover of the Bloomingdale’s restaurant — and famed froyo destination — Forty Carrots. (There’s an Ugg-exclusive flavor, of course.) Ralph Lauren is the anchor, Harter says.

Overall, this multibrand, amped-up storytelling logic extends to the in-store experience itself. Department stores this season have built out their holiday strategies around curation, with the view of inspiring their customers with the creative while making their lives as easy as possible. “One of the biggest opportunities for retail at Christmas is getting the balance between inspiration and ease right,” says Lydia King, managing director of retail at Liberty.

This is especially important this year, after department stores weathered quieter holiday seasons amid the upheaval of recent years. 2026 started with a bang, as Saks Global declared bankruptcy in January and, since summer, has been pushing its comeback as Exemplar Luxury Group. Bloomingdale’s, too, has spent 2026 pushing upmarket, making the most of a moment in which many of its contemporaries are struggling to meet the moment. This festive period, the stakes are high, capping off a year in which US department stores sought to regain lost magic.

Here’s how department stores are meeting the moment for this all-important holiday season.

This season, retailers are placing emphasis on their curation for the holiday period. The single-brand focus made sense a few years back when luxury was at its peak, Burke says. But now, he argues, department stores are looking to be more democratic in price point and product. “Compared to a few years ago, I’m seeing a lot of interest in smaller brands and independent brands,” he says. “The luxury customer today, even at the highest level, their mindset is very open to not having to only have major luxury branded merchandise.”

Department stores are curating more than just product, though. Across the board — and in keeping with Gen Z’s desire for shopping experiences that offer meaning and excitement inaccessible via social media — they’re placing emphasis on the unique experiences offered in-store throughout the holidays.

Nordstrom’s holiday facade is an embrace of Bowie.

In doing so, they’re putting themselves at the center of the narrative. Bergdorf Goodman is doing this literally, placing the store in the middle of its New York-themed holiday campaign and window display which, this year, literally leads shoppers inside. “After the sidewalk introduces you to the narrative, it continues through the main doors into a Zhivago-esque wintry snow-laden forest hung with rock crystal chandeliers and a flock of white geese overhead,” says Linda Fargo, SVP of Bergdorf’s fashion office and store presentation.

“This year we’ve put much more emphasis on the whole experience of Christmas at Liberty, beyond the product offering,” says Liberty’s King. The store’s campaign, The Liberty Christmas Chronicles, follows the rituals of the season — preparing, dressing, traveling, gathering, unwinding and eventually resting — with gifting running throughout, of course. “We know customers increasingly value curation, personalization and expertise,” she says.

This comes in via mini in-store experiences, from wreath-making with Diptyque (Liberty’s hero partner in its Atrium section) to build-your-own hampers in the Liberty Food Hall. Other activations include personalized bows with Moët & Chandon, lipstick engraving with Lisa Eldridge and makeup bag embroidery with Westman Atelier.

Selfridges is taking a similar multibrand approach with tailored, ‘only available here’ experiences. With Canada Goose on Duke Street, for instance, the retailer has constructed an Arctic-inspired research outpost with hot chocolates and personalized luggage tags, and the ultra-buzzy Vice City GTA VI pop-up kicked off the retailer’s holiday activities.

“These partnerships help create a Christmas that feels distinctive to Selfridges,” says marketing and experiences director Rebecca Warburton. “We know that people are looking for experiences, opportunities to connect with others and to create memorable moments,” she says, noting that Selfridges looked to offer more reasons for customers to spend time at the store this season.

Saks is leaning into these in-person experiences more than ever. For the first time, L’Avenue at Saks will host breakfast with Santa throughout the season and will transform into Le Chalet, with festive menu programming, on holiday season weekends.

Sienna Spiro performed at the Bloomingdale’s x Ralph Lauren window unveiling.

This curation extends to stores’ gifting options. Beyond offering a smart selection of brands, stores are also thinking more deeply about their non-fashion gifting options, so that customers will shop with them not just for shoes, but for stocking stuffers, too.

Bergdorf Goodman does this well, Burke says, noting the store’s “tremendous” holiday season in 2025. “Bergdorf’s home floor is a very good example of it being a tourist destination with fun Christmas merchandise, because they sell all of these Christmas ornaments, candies and candles. It takes over the entire seventh floor,” he says. In celebration of its 125th anniversary, Bergdorf is upping the ante this year with an Assouline legacy book, Very Bergdorf, ‘Very Bergdorf’ specialty ornaments, plus a snow globe featuring the Bergdorf Goodman facade. “Every part of the store has something uniquely made for us, and it all adds up to a very Bergdorf holiday,” Fargo says.

This year, Nordstrom is partnering with toy companies including FAO Schwarz and Jellycats. “You think about a retailer being relevant and having all the things customers want to buy — particularly at the holidays — partnerships like that help, in addition to carrying the best brands in the world,” says Deniz Anders, EVP, chief marketing officer. “Being able to check off their list for everyone across the family, that’s something that we’re really focused on.” Nordstrom has also invested more in consumables this year, and has a partnership with fashion-favorite Flamingo Estate.

This season, department stores are thinking more than ever about ease. Saving customers time and effort, execs believe, is the way to capture their hearts — and their spend.

Nordstrom has expanded its gift shops in common areas throughout the store. “It’s really not something that we used to do, but we want customers to know this is a place that you can buy gifts at broad price points,” Anders says. The retailer tested the cross-store setup last year and has expanded the size of the shops this season. “We want to make it easy and fun for customers to buy gifts.”

Liberty’s largest holiday season development is the introduction of its Gifting Curator service, King says. “For the first time, customers can book one-to-one appointments with Liberty experts who can guide them across the whole store, rather than within a single category or brand,” she says. “The idea is to make discovering the right present feel as special as giving it.” To round out the experience, Liberty has introduced a gift-wrapping experience with wrapper Roxy Wraps.

Stores have also been planting seeds to get their customers in the shopping mindset. Though all of the big, splashy window displays were unveiled at their usual time, just before Thanksgiving, the communications around the holiday season started far earlier. Nordstrom sent out its holiday catalog a week earlier than usual, Anders says. It’s the retailer’s biggest catalog ever. “We’ve been bringing in gifts a lot earlier,” she says. “Holiday is a marathon and you’ve got to have the gifts early and be ready to serve customers and bring a little joy and fun.”

Can Luxury’s Hand-Drawn Holiday Campaigns Really Rebuff AI?

No “Concrete Cowboys”: How Tecovas Is Taking Western Mainstream

#6557. Everything We Know About ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Season 36, Including Its Likely Premiere Date and Rumored Celebs

Dancing With the Stars is officially wrapping up a chaotic and unmissable season 35, and honestly I’m not sure what I’m going to do for the next 12 months. #6556. I Prepared for This A.I. Chaos Moment for Over a Year. Here Is What I’ve Learned.

If your group chats look anything like mine, you’ve spent some of the past week wondering whether something big was unfolding. As air traffic halted, Microsoft 365 went down, and Chase Bank and dozens of important websites, including the NSA’s internal classified network, experienced an array of outages, it started to seem plausible that stuff the artificial intelligence whistleblowers have been warning us about (swarms of rogue agents, AI-powered cyber attacks) was happening already. How worried should we be about the AI apocalypse? On the one hand, Claude doesn’t even have hands, LOL. On the other hand, in a battle between AI and the creaky digital infrastructure that underlays all aspects of modern life, it wouldn’t require a superintelligence to launch the final spinning wheel of death.

I consider myself a normie centrist—not too prone to conspiracy theories. During Covid, I stocked up on toilet paper and some bags of beans and rice (still unopened on my shelves) and shattered my collarbone mountain biking when we were all supposed to be hunkered down indoors during lockdown. But I also listen to a lot of podcasts. Enough that my friends and partner have told me that a digital detox might improve my mental health and, by extension, theirs. And, lately, the podcasts have sent me plunging into a Mach tuck of terminal anxiety.

The Pod Save the World boys are joking about building their stores of beef jerky. “Should I be, like, stocking up my bunker now?” Kevin Roose, cohost of Machine Gods asked his guest Ben Buchanan, the first White House Special Advisor for AI. There’s an obligatory chuckle that talking heads do now when discussing the AI apocalypse in front of an audience. “A month ago we were talking about normal things, ha ha,” Steve Eisman, finance guru of The Big Short fame, said on his podcast on October 12, “And now we’re talking about the end of the world.” But it’s hard to casually segue from the end of the world to Anthropic’s IPO and not sound like a crank. So that laugh leaks out, and maybe it’s just a joke?

But count me as a genuine worrier. This week I withdrew enough cash in hundreds from a Bank of America branch that the teller started to interview me. “Doing some home renovations? Making a big purchase?” he asked.

“No, I’m worried as shit about AI, and in case you haven’t noticed it, banks aren’t doing so well right now,” I replied. In line with me were several people who couldn’t pay their credit card bills online because the payment portal had gone down.

We’ve been hearing about the existential risk of AI for a while now. I got into it last year by reading Eliezer Yudkowsky and Nate Soares’s book, If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies, after hearing Yudkowsky interviewed on Ezra Klein’s podcast. This is a terrible book for someone who’s prone to worry because it relies on nontechnical metaphors and colorful analogies. Superintelligence is like a chessmaster. You don’t need to know the moves it’ll make to be certain it wins. True! Superintelligence, they say, will be like a new species that is so much smarter than us that, if it doesn’t really really truly love us more than our own mothers and want to keep us alive, it will kill us all. The same way we crush ants when we build skyscrapers. There’s no malice toward us. We just got in the way.

Over the past year I—casually, at first—started amassing supplies. My adventure hobbies give me an excuse. I need this 6,500-watt generator for elk-hunting camp. These 400-watt EcoFlow solar panels power a giant battery so I can bring my Starlink Mini on camping trips. I listen to history podcasts, and these offer more inspiration. There was a recent NPR one about the Carrington solar flare of 1859, which fried telegraph wires across the country. An event like that, in 2026, might knock out the grid for months. Another good reason to be prepared. I tell my partner that it’s normal for us to keep 500 pounds of dry goods in the cupboards at all times. Everyone does this. We already have three 3,000-gallon cisterns. We live in the desert; they came with the house. Did we need another 400-gallon one? It was just laying in the neighbors’ yard. Why wouldn’t I hook that up to the gutters?

I spent a lot of the summer using AI to program an app for backcountry adventurers, so I knew firsthand it was already smarter than me. The doomsday chatter started to meet me where I was around the time of the Hugging Face incident in July. Then Jacob Coxon quit Anthropic in September so he could tweet that Anthropic and OpenAI are “gambling with our lives.” On November 5, independent evaluator METR found that Anthropic’s AI was already helping build its own successors—roughly doubling the company’s pace of progress and shifting expert predictions for major disruptions closer to the present. At the Hinton Lectures, in Toronto, OpenAI board member Paul Christiano calmly told the audience, “I think for most people in this room, the most likely reason they will die is because of an AI accident.”

Friends who are less prone to apocalyptic thinking than I am tend to point out that the people ringing the alarm about AI never spell out exactly which chess moves AI uses to kill us. But the week after the lecture, there was Christiano on another one of my podcasts, Dwarkesh Patel’s show, explaining one such scenario.

First, rogue AI agents create mischief of one kind or another. Then, the Trump administration tells Americans that the only way to wrest power back from the bots is to have the military take control of all data centers, the AI companies, and shut down the internet. This feels like an authoritarian power grab. It sets up civil unrest against the backdrop of a heavily lopsided information war, in which it’s almost impossible to tell which side anyone is on. Have the bots compromised the humans? Are the humans using the bots? Judging by how well everyone agreed on the facts around things like Covid and the 2020 election, our expectations should be low for how we’d perform as a society. Which brings us to the question of guns.

I don’t think of myself as really owning any guns at all. I believe that the fastest way to get shot is to start getting into gunfights. I personally know exactly zero civilians who have solved a problem with a firearm, but I know many who have made problems, including my own mother.

About 15 years ago, she called me one day from the dog park sounding flustered. She liked to shop for musical instruments and power tools at ShopGoodwill.com, and lately the boxes had gone missing from her porch in Spokane, Washington. “You’re not going to set a trap, are you?” I asked. Our minds worked the same way.

“Well, I already did,” she said. And that was why she was hiding from the police in the dog park.

She’d left a decoy package on the porch. A woman came to grab it, and my mother burst through the door two-handing her Sig Sauer the way she’d been trained at the Federal Law Enforcement Training Center in Glynco, Georgia, when she was starting her encore career as a CBP officer after 9/11. The woman claimed to be returning the package, not stealing it. A standoff ensued. My mom eventually backed down and the lady called the cops. My mom never responded to the phone calls from the police, and the whole thing just went away.

So when it comes to guns, I think, what am I actually going to use this for? To fend off hungry neighbors? To join a well-regulated militia and maybe become a dystopian warlord? Maybe it’s as simple as guns are addictive and it’s fun to ring the steel at 500 yards. I’ve put together a couple of rifles out of parts—an MDT chassis here, a Howa barreled action there—for other members of the family to hunt with, but I only do archery. And shotguns don’t really count as guns, especially when they’re the fancy break-action models with florid engravings of bygone scenes afield. “How many guns are in this house?” my partner wants to know. She really wanted to pin me down. “Counting the unmounted lower receiver stashed in the floorboards in the attic? Eight.”

My name is Grayson, and I have somehow become a prepper. It has not made me better off. It has made me poorer, and it has put me into contact with seedier elements of the general populace. I have what psychologists call a bias toward action, when the best thing to do is probably nothing at all. And I listen to too many podcasts.

One of them, from a year ago, really stuck with me. It was an episode of the financial podcast Prof G Markets. On it, I heard Aswath Damodaran, the NYU Stern valuation professor, tell the show’s wunderkind British cohost, Ed Elson, what he was doing with his money. “There’s no place to hide in stocks,” Damodaran said. “For the first time ever, I am looking at parking my money into one, cash, and two, collectibles or physical assets.” That a guy as sober as Damodaran was talking up baseball cards and physical gold coins was shocking.

It was less shocking when, this week, Barstool Sports personality Daniel “Big Cat” Katz posted screenshots purporting to show internal discussions with Dave Portnoy and Jersey Jerry about warning staff to “buy all the gold and silver u can.” I have a quick word of warning for anyone considering following Jersey Jerry’s advice.

Back in October, I asked Claude (I know, it’s ironic) for a recommendation on a reputable place to buy gold coins. It gave me a Texas-based website that had the same look as all of the big financial institutions. I clicked around, added a handful of coins to a shopping cart, and clicked purchase, expecting to find a checkout on the next screen where I could do the math and input payment details.

Instead, the site showed me a congratulatory message, and an email soon arrived with strange looking numbers in it. It was explained to me that these were the routing numbers where I needed to wire a large five-figure sum. To my eye, it seemed shady. No thank you, I said. Customer service was either a stern woman with an eastern European accent or a guy with a Jersey accent. Their position was that I had already committed to buy the gold, and they’d send debt collectors after me if I didn’t pay up. But they could cancel the order for only a few thousand dollars. More than a month later, I’m still disputing it and have involved the attorney general’s office in three states.

Men—and it is indeed mostly men—have been wrongly predicting the end of the world for centuries, from Nostradamus and the Millerites through to the panic over Y2K. What is it that makes us want to prepare? Somewhere in the deepest parts of my monkey brain, prepping hits in the same nerve as when we’d build forts as kids and want to see if they’d withstand a good storm. When a man builds a dam, he wants it to hold a flood. So whether the thing you’re prepping for is a big hurricane, a novel coronavirus, or rogue artificial intelligence, the threat is both a source of dread and wonder. For someone who otherwise suffers from depression, it’s extremely stimulating and a little delusional.

After all, it’s a very narrow window of apocalypse that a prepper of my means can fantasize about. If the disruption is too small, I’ll be like the foolish Y2K dad with the basement full of Chef Boyardee. If the disruption is, indeed, the big one, then everyone, including the personal-plane-New Zealand-bunker-rocket-to-the-Moon money, is probably SOL. The rest of us will be left deciding whether to become the crazy gun lady or the person stealing packages off her porch to survive. In which case, why worry about rice and ammunition? I’d be better off living like some of the humans in The Dark Forest, the sequel to The Three-Body Problem. When the aliens finally arrived, they focused on throwing wild parties and having spontaneous orgies.

If the AI apocalypse hits my fantasy sweet spot—a few months of camping at home before humanity surveys our near-misses and reassembles with renewed clarity and purpose—I’ll be prepared. Maybe the worst comes to pass: economic collapse accompanied by novel bio weapons, loose nukes, and total loss of human agency. In that case, I’ll have the petty satisfaction of having been right. I can hit my group chat with the ultimate mic drop. Told ya.

The antithesis of clean girl beauty is back, this time with a little more intention and polish.

#6554. “Two Girls Who Speak All the Time About Politics”: Tao Okamoto and Virginie Efira Take On Capitalism in All of a Sudden

Okamoto and Efira shared the Best Actress Award at Cannes, where All of a Sudden premiered this summer

We’re all seeking comfort as the world ends. Environmental, economic, and political pressures weigh heavily on our minds as tangible solutions feel farther and farther out of reach. Solace is a rare commodity in a world controlled by unrelenting capitalist forces. In All of a Sudden, filmmaker Ryusuke Hamaguchi suggests that as we face tragedy, we must remember our vital need for human connection.

All of a Sudden tells the story of two women who forge a formidable friendship as they face personal and professional hardship. Marie-Lou Fontaine (Virginie Efira) runs an elder-care facility near Paris, where she introduces a “Humanitude” methodology designed to treat patients with dignity and introduce listening-heavy caretaking practice. She befriends Mari (Tao Okamoto), a terminally ill theater director from Japan. Ryusuke chronicles this relationship through extended, emotional conversations, spoken in both Japanese and French, and covering topics from identity to mortality.

The film is an adaptation of a nonfiction book, When Life Suddenly Takes a Turn: Twenty Letters Between a Philosopher With Terminal Cancer and a Medical Anthropologist, which collects correspondence from two Japanese-speaking academics as they reflect on health care and death during the last few months of philosopher Makiko Miyano’s life (who inspired Okamoto’s character). Instead of keeping the fact that the two pen pals spoke the same language, he introduced French and moved the story to Paris. This small change forced the story to confront language gaps, exploring the power of conversation and listening and asserting that so much can be achieved through this mediation.

Efira and Okamoto understand their characters’ simmering anxieties and outlooks on the world, providing the bedrock for these impressively naturalistic performances. The two actresses sustain long, philosophical conversation with such intensity throughout the entire three hours and 16 minutes. In a rare occurrence, the two shared the Best Actress Award at the Cannes Film Festival, where the movie premiered this summer. In the film, their budding friendship serves as a sounding board for each other, as Marie-Lou grapples with the pressure of capitalism on her medical staff and Mari comes to terms with the end of her life. In typical fashion, Ryusuke lets the camera linger on these two actresses during these prolonged philosophical interactions, forcing the audience to sit with the gravity of their situations.

Ahead of the theatrical release of Ryusuke’s latest feature film, Bazaar spoke to Efira and Okamoto about the first time they read the script, how they built an intimate bond despite speaking two different languages, and the punk, anti-capitalist edge fueling this intimate, conversation-heavy film.

Tao Okamoto: It’s the scene where she talks about capitalism while writing on the whiteboard. That’s one of the strangest scenes in the movie. I felt connected to the character because I am also a climate activist myself. I’ve been spreading the word about climate crises on podcasts, and I talk about it daily. When I read the part, I felt, “I need to play this character.”

Virginie Efira: It’s the same for me, because this scene is so special. When I read it, the movie felt like one of the most profound, special, and radical readings. You have two girls who speak all the time about politics. We don’t see that. I never see that in other movies. Sometimes, when a director wants to put something philosophical, political, or intellectual, they say, “Maybe we’ll do three minutes,” otherwise the audience will be bored. No, this movie spent 15 minutes [discussing capitalism], and I said, “Oh yes, that’s so punk!” The film has its own language. When I read All of a Sudden, I could hear the language of the movie, and I wanted to approach it. When I met Tao, I thought, “Oh, we are so lucky to be an actress and work with [Ryusuke], but also to share this material with him.”

VE: I only knew one word in Japanese before making the movie. Then, to be able to speak Japanese at a high level, because the character studied in Japan, I must first learn to read, then learn to embody the language. Finding the language of the movie was strange. There’s a flat and natural way to play [in one of Ryusuke’s movies]. He spoke to me about Cassavetes and Gena Rowlands, but also about Bresson. Neutral performances. You find it when you open up, and when I opened to Tao, we discovered the right language for the movie. We did a lot of flat readings before shooting. We never rehearsed in motion, so when we finally performed for the first time, we awakened the text.

TO: I’m going to add that because I started my career in America, finding a middle ground by using a language that’s not my mother tongue is normal for me. On the surface, that part wasn’t a challenge for me. Finding the language for this film and respecting Hamaguchi’s way of working were priorities. We were naturally drawn to his talent and wanted to make his dream happen and be part of it.

TO: I joined this project way after Virginie. I luckily had months to prepare with him, and during rehearsals, you drop all emotion and read the text as if you were reading numbers. On top of that, I got to visit some institutes, such as the Cancer Research Center and places where people work with disabled people, to build up my understanding of my role’s point of view. Reading the text over and over was a unique experience for me. [Ryusuke] can really listen to the actor’s voice and tell the difference between someone who is simply reading and someone really concentrating. Even though you’re not supposed to put any emotions, you have to be present; you have to be there. I’ve never experienced such rich, profound preparation for a role.

VE: We shot in a real nursing home. There was a mix between fiction and reality. Sometimes filmmaking becomes standardized, with no room left to breathe, but with Hamaguchi, you’re sure he will leave room for the unexpected and for uncertainty. It allows you to embrace the unpredictability of reality. He’s not looking for an accident, but for the ability to be in the present. A viewer of Hamaguchi’s work will always feel a sensation of documentation, but it’s still always cinema. To be an actor in his movies is to be naturalistic, to be in the present, to capture some details and really embrace this unpredictability. The filming process becomes an adventure he wants you to have. One day, he told me, “I hope that you will find the light.” He said a lot of cryptic things. He said to me, “Don’t use your text like a tool; just go inside the text.”

TO: The script is already amazing. And I could read it over and over again, and that was a joy. But he mentioned that I should read the original book. It’s about two women exchanging letters—or emails. They are real people who the characters are modeled after. I wanted to please the people who know these women, not to make them too different from them. I first put pressure on myself to portray this woman as close to the real person, but it was impossible for me because this person passed away in 2019. I couldn’t do much research, so I interviewed her mother and the co-writer. That provided me with a lot of clues, but reading their exchanged letters already gave me the soul of this movie.

VE: He gives the book to the actresses, but also to the entire team. I never felt something so collective, and you will feel it in the movie, from the sound engineer to someone who only stayed two days on set. I also met the anthropologist. When you read this book, it’s full of life. It gives you a perceptible beauty of things. The book has a kind of energy; it helps you keep a level of optimism even when you know the situation is hopeless. In this book, it speaks about what it’s like to be alive. Maybe there’s beauty in life because we are going to die.

TO: For me, I came on this project at the last minute, only a month before. Virginie was so dedicated to studying Japanese from scratch; it was so impressive how she studied all this writing, reading, and lines. Seeing that, I had a huge admiration, like “Who can do this!?” As you can see, Virginie’s so charming; nobody would hate her. It was so easy for me to be drawn to her as a person, as the characters in the movie were discovering each other.

VE: We got to know each other through the shoot. I was happy not to know much at the beginning and to progressively get deeper into our relationship. We lived all that together. I conflate Marie-Lou’s feelings for Mari with Virginie’s feelings for Tao, because we share that. It will be an end, not a tragic one like in the movie, but it will be an end. But I know Tao will carry this experience in her heart forever, and I will too. He let us feel what we want to feel.

TO: I study environmental issues, and I always thought capitalism is responsible for our world’s issues. Something I didn’t know until I read this script was that capitalism doesn’t only exploit our environment, but also our human bodies—human labor and our time. When I connected those dots, I realized all the problems could be blamed on capitalism. We’re looking for time and looking for happiness—or security—and all these things can be related to a capitalistic way of living. I think it’s great that Hamaguchi connected that fundamental issue to Marie-Lou’s situation in the elderly home. She’s frustrated, as is Marie. She’s battling her cancer, and it can be connected to this economic issue.

VE: Cinema can show bodies, fatigue, and with Marie-Lou, you see a certain fatigue because capitalism creates it. How do we deal with that? We say, “Oh, capitalism’s not good. We don’t have time. And we want to be richer and richer.” But how do we fight that, because everything is already there? I come from France, and you see all the young people outside. The real question is, what can cinema do with that? It’s not a theoretical thing, but Hamaguchi can find a solution. It’s speaking, listening to the other, sharing ideas; it’s movement. If I look for a solution to this problem with money, I’ll remain a prisoner of this way of functioning. Even if you think you’re not in a stage of capitalism, you are.

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

#6553. Miley Cyrus Debuts Her Sculptural Wedding Band—Jewelers Explain the Design

Miley Cyrus did more than give fans a first look at her wedding dress when she shared photos of her nuptials on Sunday. Zoom in and the singer’s thick wedding band is visible, stacked with her gold bombé cushion-cut diamond engagement ring.

She later shared another look at her bridal jewelry, including her band, which appears to be in a black round ring box:

The singer’s band choice is fashion-forward, The Clear Cut’s founder Olivia Landau tells ELLE. “Thick wedding bands have been trending, whether they are larger diamond eternity bands or wider solid gold bands.”

It also really suits her, says Lauren Boc, founder and CEO of Hera Fine Jewelry: “Miley’s wedding set is a beautiful reflection of her personal style—it has some rock and roll edge but still feels upscale.”

Here, jewelers share their first impressions of the piece with ELLE.

Steph Mazuera, who founded her own namesake brand, says Cyrus’s ring has “a slightly curved silhouette” that “appears to follow the contours of her engagement ring.”

“When working with larger, bolder rings, introducing a soft curve or slight bend into the stack can make the overall combination feel more organic,” she adds. “It brings a sense of balance and softness without taking away from the impact of the individual rings. Everything should be designed to flow around the center stone.”

Based on the photos she shared, Cyrus’s engagement ring and wedding band appear to be different metals. Her engagement ring is yellow gold.

Her wedding band, meanwhile, “looks to be a half-round band, around 4 mm, in white gold or platinum,” Boc says. “It appears that there’s a small east-west oval diamond bezel-set on top of the band, similar to our Amir ring. Raising the diamond slightly above the band like this creates dimension.”

Several factors contribute to the value of a ring like Cyrus’s. “A custom band like this one could range quite a bit depending on the maker, gold weight, and craftsmanship,” Mazuera explains. “I would price a similar custom design in my studio in the $3,000–$5,000 range.”

Cyrus’s wedding band is a statement piece that can be worn with or without her engagement ring. “I like that her wedding band feels substantial enough to hold its own alongside her engagement ring without competing with it,” Mazuera says. “Both rings have a strong sculptural presence, but the slight curve of the wedding band helps the two sit together more naturally.”

Worn together, the two pieces have “a really bold look that’s both edgy and luxurious,” Boc says. “It’s maximalist, with a lot of metal on the hand. I love it!”

Cyrus’s wedding band is anything but minimalist—but that’s exactly why it fuels multiple jewelry trends.

“I think we’re seeing a continued move toward more sculptural, substantial wedding jewelry, moving away from the idea that wedding bands need to be delicate or understated,” Mazuera says.

Boc agrees. “Miley’s wedding set definitely reflects the trends we’re seeing at Hera. Chunky, bolder looks with a lot of gold weight have become really popular with our clients. When I first founded Hera in 2021, the big engagement ring trend was really thin, delicate-looking bands. As a jewelry designer who cares a lot about durability, I’m so happy to see people turning toward more substantial designs.”

Less traditional ring designs like Cyrus’s are also becoming more popular. “Our clients are often more concerned with having a ring that feels distinctive and uniquely like them,” Boc says.

Boc notes that while Cyrus’s photos give a limited view of her engagement ring and wedding band together, the apparent mix of metals is right on trend.

“I think there’s something so chic and effortless about a mixed metal stack,” she adds. “Like everything else about Miley’s stack, mixing metals is the kind of design decision that makes your wedding set really feel curated and chosen around a strong understanding of your personal style. She certainly didn’t just pick this up pre-designed at a big box store; a stack like this belongs to someone who’s confident in her personal style and chose something that feels uniquely her.”

And the winner of the Mirrorball Trophy is...

Dancing With the Stars season 35 has crowned a winner! After a fiercely competitive season finale with each couple performing an elaborate Freestyle routine, a Judges’ Choice dance, and an Instant Dance, the scores were tallied, the viewers’ votes were counted, and the winner is....

Sooo, what did Ezra win? That’d be the Len Goodman Mirrorball Trophy, as well as a cash prize. As she explained to The Morning After podcast, “Whoever makes it to the finale, you all get paid up until then. And you do get a nice bonus. If you win, it’s not like you get a million dollars. You’re just getting a cute trophy together.”

Congrats again to Ezra and Daniella on that shiny trophy and an incredible season!

#6550. Inside Fashion’s $276 Billion Succession Shakeup

What appears to be a succession story at a handful of prominent luxury companies is, in fact, a defining moment for the wider industry, with the potential to reshape fashion’s competitive map, a new report says.

“Succession is one of the 10 themes that we believe will shape fashion in the next decade,” says the report’s author, Achim Berg, who in 2025 created FashionSights, a research and advisory boutique, after 23 years at McKinsey.

The report “Fashion’s Succession Problem: The Generational Transfer Reshaping the Industry” is an excerpt from a book Berg co-wrote with Paula Trus titled The Future of Fashion. It is set for publication in March by Bocconi University Press. Ahead of its release, Berg shares the report’s key findings with Vogue Business.

Vogue: Why is succession a defining theme of the next decade?

Achim Berg: We built our index while writing the book. It’s called FashionSights Global 100 (FSG100), a database of the world’s 100 largest fashion and apparel companies ranked by revenue. Out of 100 companies, 46 are family-owned. And out of the 46 companies with family dominance, two-thirds (or 30 companies) face a succession challenge, according to our analysis. These 30 companies generated a combined $276 billion in revenues in 2025. The main criteria for companies meeting what we call succession risk is that they are led by the first or second generation, and those who are in charge, which could be advisory, supervisory board or management, are 65 or older. We also added companies that have publicly acknowledged a succession challenge. It’s worth noting that 63% of the companies navigating succession challenges are based in Europe, 30% in North America and the remaining 7% in Asia-Pacific.

Why is this great generational transfer now?

Most of those companies that are around now, with a few exceptions, were founded in the ’60s, ’70s, ’80s, or ’90s of the last century. Ralph Lauren sold his first ties in 1967. In 1975, Amancio Ortega opened his first Zara store. And in 1984, Tadashi Yanai opened the first Uniqlo store. Modern fashion retail is 60 years old.

Either the family has more than 50% share capital, or more than 50% of the voting rights. But also, in some cases, they do not even need to have that much because they are still the biggest shareholders and they call the shots. Some families have special voting rights or legal structures that allow them to play a larger role.

What are some of the ways succession will play out for these companies?

It depends. First, will the departing owner hand over on time? And second, are the heirs as capable as the founders? Succession will occupy a lot of time and energy, and that could derail some of those businesses. Some of that trouble will lead them to M&A in whatever shape and form. Generational handover could accelerate consolidation as families consider partnerships, outside investment or a sale.  We’re only looking at 30 of the largest 100 companies. But you will also find many smaller businesses facing similar succession challenges and looking to sell. It’s not a given that they’re all going to find an acquirer.

When it comes to buyers, established fashion groups could gain scale through acquisitions and expand their portfolios with new brands. We notably see opportunities for stronger Italian luxury groups.

We also see opportunities for new owners from the beauty and eyewear industries. Think about L’Oréal and EssilorLuxottica and the discussion around Armani. They were considered suitable acquirers by Mr Armani himself.  It could also be a good opportunity for those companies to become more relevant in the fashion space.

Last but not least, brand ownership and licensing companies such as ABG, WHP Global and Marquee Brands could offer another route for families seeking an exit. I interviewed [ABG founder and executive chairman] Jamie Salter for my book. He claims publicly that his ecosystem generates $38 billion in annual retail sales by now. So they can do massive acquisitions globally, and they’re looking for global brands to create licensing.

Some successions, however, are expected to proceed smoothly, according to the report.

Yes, the report identifies clear responsibilities, independent oversight and successors tested through meaningful experience as a foundation for successful handovers.  In the white paper, you will see that we consider the handover at Zegna as one of those more positive examples.  Gildo Zegna is the group executive chair. Gianluca Tagliabue is the group CEO. And Gildo Zegna’s two sons, Edoardo and Angelo Zegna, are also now playing a role as co-CEOs of the Zegna brand and are groomed for succession. A succession typically takes 10 to 15 years of preparation, and our observation is that many of the businesses need to seriously engage in those preparations.

#6549. How to Improve Brain Health with 5 Daily Exercises

Brain health isn’t just an issue for the elderly. Maybe you've experienced the first subtle signs of decline. Sometimes it’s a name that’s on the tip of your tongue. Or perhaps you've read a news story in the morning that you can't remember by the evening. It could be that you're feeling increasingly mentally fatigued after a typical day spent dealing with emails, text messages, phone calls, and constant interruptions.

The good news is that you can take steps to keep your mind sharp. “Our brain needs to be stimulated, at any age," says Giuseppe Alfredo Iannoccari, a neuropsychologist, professor at the University of Milan, and president of Assomensana, an association founded in Monza, Italy in 2004 to promote cognitive health. Routines help conserve mental energy, while new experiences force it to work,” says Giuseppe Alfredo Iannoccari, a neuropsychologist, professor at the University of Milan. While working with people suffering from neurodegenerative diseases, including Alzheimer’s, he came up with the idea to create a gym for the brain. “I asked myself what could be done earlier, when people are still healthy,” he said. His studies led him to develop a protocol that anyone can integrate into their daily routine.

The method is based on the principle of neuroplasticity—that is, the brain’s ability to modify its connections in response to learning and experience. This ability persists throughout one’s entire life, although it changes with age. Today, Iannocari's courses consist of weekly 90-minute sessions in groups of up to fifteen participants. Each session features about ten exercises focused on memory, attention, language, reasoning, creativity, and other cognitive skills. At the beginning and end of the program, twelve mental functions are assessed to measure their progress. “In our tests, we observe an average improvement in the overall cognitive efficiency index of 25–30%," says Iannoccari. "Some functions, such as attention and language, respond particularly well. Others, such as logical reasoning and prose memory, require more intensive work.” These results are based on the association’s experience and should not be interpreted as a percentage reduction in the risk of dementia. The World Health Organization’s guidelines, updated in 2026, acknowledge the potential benefits of cognitive training, while emphasizing that evidence regarding the long-term prevention of cognitive decline remains limited.

When it comes to our ability to concentrate, Iannoccari points to one culprit at the center of our modern lives. “Sometimes we feel tired not because we’ve done too many things, but because we’ve switched what we were doing too many times," he says. “Constantly switching from one task to another is more tiring than focusing on the same activity for an extended period.” A stream of notifications, information to process, and the need to respond quickly to various requests put our attention to the test above all else. “It’s the foundation of all cognitive processes. When it’s fragmented, even the quality of the information we’re able to acquire deteriorates.”

The Johns Hopkins professor recommends some tips you may know—and a few that might surprise you—to keep your brain firing on all cylinders.

And then there’s AI, which promises to free us from an ever-increasing number of tasks. What are the consequences for our brains? “If we ask artificial intelligence to compare two tables, perform an analysis, and write a report, we get the work done in a few minutes that would previously have taken an entire day," says Iannoccari. “Saving work also means reducing some mental activity. But it depends on how we use these tools.”

For Iannoccari, the key is to preserve two abilities: critical thinking and analysis. “We must know how to formulate questions, and this requires familiarity with the subject,” he says. “Then we must evaluate the answers, recognize any inconsistencies, and verify the information. Only a well-trained mind can spot errors.” This issue also applies to seemingly beneficial habits, such as reading books or solving crossword puzzles.“ Reading is invaluable, especially for the emotions it evokes and the worlds it allows us to discover," he says. “But reading doesn’t automatically mean memorizing. If we want to remember what we’ve read, we must do so with the intention of being able to recount it to someone else.” As for puzzle-solving pastimes, “Sudoku trains certain concentration and planning skills, while crossword puzzles stimulate the retrieval of information from memory,” he says. “These are useful activities, but a structured program allows you to work on different skills and gradually increase the difficulty of the exercises.”

In addition to guided sessions, Iannoccari suggests short daily sessions he calls “Brain Breaks”— five-minute breaks for small exercises that counteract brain aging. “If we wait to find five free minutes, we’ll probably never find them.” “We need to make it a habit, just like having a cup of coffee.” Here are five exercises you can try:

Choose a topic—preferably a positive one—read it carefully, and try to recount it to three different people without constantly referring back to the text. “The first time we repeat a piece of information, we’re often uncertain,” says Iannoccari. “The second time, we become clearer and more fluent. The third time, we have another chance to reinforce it in our memory.” It’s an exercise that stimulates what’s known as “prose memory”—the ability to recall and process information we’ve read or heard. “Let’s imagine learning one piece of news a day for 365 days: how much knowledge will we have accumulated?”

For five minutes, write down as many words as possible that start with the letter A, trying to be quick. Repeat the exercise the next day, avoiding words you’ve already used. On the third day, move on to B, and so on. “It’s a way to train verbal fluency and lexical retrieval, particularly useful when we start to experience that ‘word on the tip of my tongue’ phenomenon,” says Iannoccari. The goal is to gradually make the search more challenging by striving to find words that aren’t immediately obvious.

Right-handed people can try performing some simple tasks with their left hand, and vice versa. Open a drawer, move small objects, draw, and gradually try writing. “This new challenge forces the brain to break out of its automatic routines and engage resources we normally use less," he says. “Surprising results can be achieved: people who initially can’t even draw a line gradually learn to write with their other hand.” Of course, it’s best to choose safe activities and proceed without rushing.

How many phone numbers do we still remember? And how often do we rely on the GPS even to get to relatively familiar places? “Before calling someone, I try to remember their number," says Iannoccari. “Before using a calculator, I do the math in my head. Before turning on the GPS, I try to figure out the route.” The same goes for the grocery list. “I write it down so I can be sure I won’t forget anything, but at the supermarket I first try to recall the items. Only then do I check the list.” These are small opportunities to keep practicing skills that we increasingly tend to delegate to digital tools.

Once in bed, instead of focusing on the next day’s commitments, try to mentally retrace the day that just passed. What time did you wake up? What did you eat? Who did you meet? What did you talk about? What details stood out to us? “You have to try to be very detailed,” says Iannoccari. “Often, people who have trouble falling asleep find that this exercise also helps them drift off.”

But there’s another aspect that’s particularly close to his heart. “I find there’s something romantic about it. We live one day after another and often retain almost nothing of them. Reflecting on it means recognizing its value. Every day leaves something behind, like a geological layer deposited on top of the one before it.” This little ritual offers an opportunity to recall the experiences we wish to preserve.

When is the right time to start? “As children,” says Iannoccari. “We encourage them to play sports, but we rarely think about the importance of training mental abilities just as intentionally.” In adult life, of course, exercise, sleep, nutrition, stress management, and social relationships also matter. “But if I had to name three drivers of the mind, I’d choose curiosity, creativity, and emotions."he says. "Curiosity opens us up to new knowledge, creativity allows us to connect that knowledge in original ways, and emotions make experiences more memorable.”

A conversation with neuroscientist Lisa Feldman Barrett on the counterintuitive ways your mind processes reality—and why understanding that might help you feel a little less anxious.

He also notes that, though older age is associated with worse brain function, it's also how we collect the wisdom of lived experiences. “We think an efficient brain is the one we had at twenty, and we lose sight of what we’ve gained by the time we’re sixty or seventy," he says. "When we were young, we may have been faster, but over time we’ve accumulated skills, knowledge, and experience. We possess a much richer worldview.”

The real challenge is to remain present to ourselves and others, valuing who we’ve become.” And perhaps, as the years go by, one of the most important skills is precisely learning to choose where to invest our mental energy. “We meet people who, now that they’re retired, are busier than when they were working. We’ve come to associate efficiency with the number of things we can do, but in doing so, we lose the opportunity to delve deeper, reflect, and let experiences settle. I always say that I don’t pursue nice ideas—I pursue great ideas. Because there are so many nice ideas, and they risk scattering our energy in a thousand different directions.” “With age,” concludes Iannoccari, “we have a great privilege: the ability to choose.”

A version of this story originally appeared in GQ Italia.
